**Summary.** This paper analyzes quantum thermal machines by studying how on-site interactions modify key transport properties like efficiency and rectification. By comparing results from multiple master equation formalisms against an exact stochastic method (HOPS), the authors demonstrate that interactions enable novel functionalities, such as tunable efficiency and non-reciprocal heat flow.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the fundamental problem of modeling open quantum systems where multiple theoretical approximations (LME, GME, RME) exist. The use of HOPS provides a powerful, numerically exact tool to validate predictions in complex non-equilibrium regimes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how on-site interactions affect the efficiency, rectification, and entanglement properties of continuous quantum thermal machines.

**Main result.** Interactions can tune the machine's efficiency at high temperatures while leaving it robustly unchanged at low temperatures, and they are necessary to induce non-reciprocal transport and steady-state entanglement.

**Method.** The authors compare results derived from multiple theoretical frameworks—including Redfield, Local, and Global Master Equations—against exact stochastic methods like HOPS.

**Model / system.** The model is a continuous quantum thermal machine consisting of two coupled oscillators (sites) connected to two distinct thermal reservoirs (hot and cold). The system Hamiltonian includes an on-site interaction term ($U$).

**Key observables.** Efficiency ($\eta$), rectification ratio ($R$), steady-state entanglement (Log-negativity $\mathcal{E}_N$), heat currents, and particle currents.

**Important parameters / regimes.** On-site interaction strength ($U$), reservoir coupling strengths ($\kappa_\ell$), temperature ratios ($T_h/T_c$), and system frequencies ($\Omega_\ell$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the weak system-reservoir coupling regime for master equation derivations, though HOPS extends applicability. Comparisons are made between different approximations (LME vs GME).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare steady-state populations and fidelity across LME, GME, RME, and Exact methods; other figures show the dependence of power, heat currents, rectification ratio, and entanglement on temperature and interaction strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares various theoretical descriptions (LME, GME, RME) for open quantum systems dynamics. It then applies these tools to analyze thermodynamic quantities like efficiency and current flow, demonstrating the role of interactions in enhancing non-equilibrium features like rectification and entanglement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Advances in experimental techniques have opened new routes for harnessing non-equilibrium dynamics in mesoscopic quantum systems. In this context, we study the impact of on-site interactions on the transport properties of a continuous quantum thermal machine composed of two coupled oscillators connected to two thermal reservoirs. In the weak system-reservoir coupling regime, where a long-standing debate concerns which reduced description should be preferred, we first show that the Redfield master equation (RME) provides an accurate and unifying framework that interpolates between two well-known limits: the \textit{local} and \textit{global} master equations. By relying on the Hierarchy of Pure States (HOPS), a numerically exact stochastic method, we then explore the full parameter space and show that interactions can be leveraged to tune the efficiency of the thermal machine at high temperatures (while leaving it essentially unchanged at low temperatures), induce non-reciprocal transport under asymmetric reservoir couplings, and generate steady-state entanglement within the junction. We derive expressions for system-bath correlators, such as heat and particle currents, consistently across different frameworks. Our work features on-site interactions to enhance the versatility of quantum thermodynamic junctions and clarifies the role of non-Markovianity and non-linearities in quantum transport.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes superradiance in a Kerr-Dicke model where light self-interacts. By introducing negative nonlinearity and accounting for cavity dissipation, the authors predict a stable 'Kerr-radiant phase' that lowers the threshold for lasing compared to standard models. The analysis relies heavily on mean-field theory and stability analysis of open quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends standard cavity QED models by incorporating nonlinear photon interactions, providing a detailed theoretical framework for understanding dissipation-assisted quantum phase transitions in open systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates finding a low-threshold route to superradiance in light-matter systems by incorporating self-interaction (Kerr nonlinearity) into the standard Dicke model.

**Main result.** Negative Kerr nonlinearity ($U<0$) stabilizes a novel 'Kerr-radiant phase' (KP) via cavity dissipation, allowing for lasing thresholds below those predicted by the conventional superradiant transition.

**Method.** The analysis employs mean-field theory, analyzing the Ginzburg-Landau potential and performing stability analyses on the resulting coupled differential equations derived from the Lindblad master equation.

**Model / system.** The system is a Kerr-Dicke model: $N$ two-level emitters coupled to a single-mode cavity with a photonic Kerr nonlinearity ($U$). The dynamics are governed by an open quantum system approach using the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Order parameters ($\langle S_x angle/N, \langle S_z angle/N, \langle a angle/\sqrt{N}$), excitation spectrum (polariton branches), and stability eigenvalues ($	ext{Re } \lambda_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Kerr nonlinearity strength ($U$), light-matter coupling ($g$), cavity dissipation rate ($\kappa$), and system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mean-field approximation ($\langle AB angle \approx \langle A angle \langle B angle$) is used extensively for both closed and open system treatments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram showing coexisting NP, SP, and KP regions; they also map initial conditions in cavity phase space to final steady states, demonstrating the robustness of the KP basin.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the Kerr-Dicke Hamiltonian and mean-field potential (GL analysis) to analyzing open system dynamics via the Lindblad equation. It then details stability analysis using eigenvalues and concludes by proposing initialization protocols to access the exotic KP phase.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Light-matter interactions are often modeled via the Dicke model, namely, by two-level systems coupled to a cavity mode. Alas, the threshold for superradiance is often experimentally inaccessible or hindered by light's diamagnetic term. Here, within the Dicke setting, we consider self-interacting light in a cavity, modeled by a photonic Kerr nonlinearity. We show that negative Kerr nonlinearity gives rise to a low-threshold superradiant phase with spin inversion. While unstable in a closed system, cavity dissipation stabilizes this lit phase, opening avenues for lasing and bath-engineered phases.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive cost framework for MPS-based simulations of open quantum systems by analyzing the trade-off between trajectory complexity and statistical sampling effort. It introduces two inflation factors, $\alpha$ and $\kappa$, to quantify how different mathematical representations (unravelings) affect computational cost. The key finding is that the optimal simulation strategy depends critically on the specific hardware resources available, not just the physics of the noise.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial practical guidelines for simulating realistic open quantum systems on near-term noisy hardware, moving beyond simple entanglement measures to account for resource limitations like memory bandwidth and parallel processing capability.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The total computational cost of simulating open quantum systems using MPS-based stochastic trajectories depends on a complex interplay between per-trajectory complexity (entanglement) and statistical sampling effort, which is poorly understood.

**Main result.** The choice of physically equivalent stochastic unraveling is not an intrinsic optimization but rather a hardware-aware design problem, determined by dimensionless inflation factors $(\alpha, \kappa)$ relative to available memory and parallelism.

**Method.** The authors introduce a cost-resolved framework that decomposes total simulation cost into three components: per-trajectory memory, runtime, and sampling effort. They quantify the trade-off using two inflation factors, $\alpha$ (bond dimension) and $\kappa$ (sampling size).

**Model / system.** The study models open quantum systems whose evolution is governed by Lindblad master equations. Simulations are performed on 1D systems using Matrix Product States (MPS) techniques.

**Key observables.** Bond inflation factor ($\alpha$), sampling inflation factor ($\kappa$), total wall time ratios ($T_A/T_B$), and the peak average trajectory bond dimension ($\chi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength, time-step resolution ($\delta t$), system size ($L$), available parallelism ($P$), and physical memory ($M$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that total cost can be separated into distinct channels (memory, runtime, sampling) and models the resource constraints using specific scaling laws for finite parallelism.

**Figures summary.** Figures map out decision geometries in planes of hardware resources $(M, P)$ or $(\alpha, \kappa)$, showing which unraveling is computationally favored under different noise conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for cost analysis in trajectory simulations, develops a resource-accounting framework using $\alpha$ and $\kappa$, benchmarks this framework across various noise channels, and concludes by mapping out hardware-aware decision maps to guide simulation design.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Efficient simulation of open quantum systems is central to modeling noisy quantum hardware and many-body dynamics. In trajectory-based tensor network methods, cost is often associated with trajectory-level quantities such as entanglement growth or bond dimension. However, the total cost of a fixed-accuracy simulation also depends on statistical sampling, and the interplay between per-trajectory complexity and sampling effort remains poorly understood. Here we introduce a cost-resolved framework for matrix product state (MPS)-based quantum trajectory simulations that decomposes total cost into memory per trajectory, runtime per trajectory, and sampling effort. We show that physically equivalent stochastic unravelings of the same Lindblad dynamics do not necessarily reduce total cost, but instead redistribute cost between trajectory complexity and statistical convergence. This trade-off is quantified by two dimensionless inflation factors: a bond dimension inflation $α$ and a sampling inflation $κ$, which together determine the preferred unraveling under hardware-dependent memory and parallelism constraints. We provide a practical protocol for extracting $(α,κ)$ from modest pilot simulations and demonstrate it using benchmarks across multiple noise channels. The resulting decision maps show that the computationally favorable unraveling can change with noise strength, time-step resolution, system size, and available parallelism. These results establish unraveling choice as a hardware-aware simulation design problem rather than an intrinsic optimization of trajectory entanglement alone.

</details>

**Summary.** The research details the experimental implementation of two-qubit quantum gates on $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ atoms by comparing two distinct Rydberg excitation schemes. The authors demonstrate that using a $6P_{3/2}$ intermediate level, coupled with a zoned architecture, yields high fidelity (94%) because it circumvents difficult beam focusing requirements inherent in alternative setups.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed comparison of physical implementations for quantum gates in neutral atoms, directly addressing practical engineering challenges (like beam focusing and intermediate state selection) that limit fidelity in current quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central goal is to experimentally implement high-fidelity two-qubit quantum gates on neutral Rubidium-87 atoms.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a two-qubit operation fidelity of 94% using the $6P_{3/2}$ intermediate state scheme, which was shown to be superior to an alternative scheme involving the $5P_{1/2}$ level.

**Method.** They utilized Rydberg blockade physics and implemented the Controlled-Z (CZ) gate via precise laser pulsing sequences following the Levine–Pichler method.

**Model / system.** The system uses neutral $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ atoms trapped in optical tweezers, with qubits encoded in hyperfine ground states. Two schemes are compared: one using $5P_{1/2}$ and another using $6P_{3/2}$ as the intermediate excitation level.

**Key observables.** Two-qubit operation fidelity ($F$), measured via parity measurements on entangled states like $|\Phi^+angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The choice of intermediate level, spatial configuration (zone separation), and laser parameters ($\lambda=420 	ext{ nm}$, $\lambda_2=1012 	ext{ nm}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Numerical modeling suggests that the spatial configuration has a greater impact on fidelity than the specific choice of intermediate level. The $6P_{3/2}$ scheme is favored due to technological ease in generating high power at $1012 	ext{ nm}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the optical schemes for both excitation pathways (Scheme A and Scheme B) and show experimental sequences used to measure CZ gate fidelity and parity oscillations.

**Paper structure.** The paper compares two Rydberg excitation schemes ($5P_{1/2}$ vs $6P_{3/2}$) theoretically, analyzes their limitations based on scattering rates and beam profiles, presents numerical modeling results emphasizing spatial dependence, and concludes with the experimental demonstration of the higher-fidelity scheme using $6P_{3/2}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This work presents an experimental setup for implementing two-qubit operations on neutral atoms ($^{87}$Rb) with the possibility of using two different Rydberg excitation schemes. One of them uses 5P$_{1/2}$ as the intermediate level and applies the second-stage beam locally to the addressed atoms. The second scheme uses the 6P$_{3/2}$ level; in this scheme, the particles to be entangled are moved to a separate zone through which both Rydberg beams pass. The advantages and limitations of both schemes are analyzed. Based on numerical modeling performed with a Julia package developed by the authors, it is demonstrated that the spatial configuration has a greater effect on quantum-operation fidelity than the choice of intermediate level. An experimental implementation of the scheme using the 6P$_{3/2}$ level is demonstrated, making it possible to achieve a two-qubit operation fidelity of 94%.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces and validates Neural Quantum States (NQS) as a powerful numerical technique to simulate light-matter coupled quantum systems. It models Rydberg atoms interacting with cavity photons, accurately predicting ground state phase boundaries by capturing crucial spin-photon correlations missed by mean-field theory. This method offers a scalable framework for studying complex strongly correlated physics in hybrid quantum platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant advancement in simulating strongly correlated quantum systems by providing a scalable, highly accurate numerical tool (NQS) that surpasses the limitations of traditional methods like Mean-Field Theory or Tensor Networks for specific hybrid Hilbert spaces.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a robust numerical method to study complex many-body quantum systems where short-range interactions coexist with long-range photon-mediated couplings, exceeding the capabilities of mean-field theory.

**Main result.** The Neural Quantum State (NQS) approach successfully captures spin-photon and spin-spin correlations, leading to quantitative deviations in predicted ground state phase boundaries compared to mean-field calculations.

**Method.** The study employs Neural Quantum States (NQS), utilizing a multi-head ResNet architecture to represent the wavefunction on hybrid Hilbert spaces. A unitary transformation is used to map the system to a stoquastic Hamiltonian, enabling Variational Monte Carlo optimization.

**Model / system.** The model is a light-matter coupled system, specifically a 2D lattice of Rydberg atoms interacting with a cavity photon mode (spin-photon coupling). The Hamiltonian includes spin interactions ($H_{	ext{spin}}$), cavity terms ($H_{	ext{cavity}}$), and the interaction term ($H_{	ext{int}}$).

**Key observables.** Ground state properties, phase boundaries (FP, AFM, SR phases), Renyi-2 entanglement entropy, and energy expectation values.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strengths ratios like $g/\Delta$ and $V/\Delta$; photon cutoff ($n_{	ext{cutoff}}$); system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method requires truncating the infinite photon degree of freedom at a finite cutoff ($n_{	ext{cutoff}}$) and assumes adiabatic preparation for ground state calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing predicted boundaries, comparison plots between NQS and mean-field results for order parameters (AFM/SR), and convergence diagnostics like energy variance vs. time step or cutoff size.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by introducing the physical model and Hamiltonian, detailing the necessary unitary transformation to ensure stochasticity, presenting the advanced multi-head ResNet NQS architecture, validating its stability through initialization techniques, and finally applying it to map out phase diagrams and quantify correlation effects beyond mean-field theory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent advances in cold atom manipulation enable the study of many-body systems where short-range interactions between neighboring atoms coexist with long-range interactions mediated by photons. Such a combination of interactions makes a theoretical approach challenging beyond mean-field methods. In this work, we develop a neural quantum state based approach to study these systems numerically. We introduce a neural-network architecture capable of handling hybrid Hilbert spaces with large local bosonic dimensions in strongly interacting spin-photon systems. We benchmark this approach on a model of a two-dimensional lattice of Rydberg atoms coupled to a photon mode. The superradiant ground states found in the large spin-photon coupling regime allow us to demonstrate the efficiency of the method in the presence of high photon occupation. Furthermore, the ability to capture spin-spin and spin-photon correlations leads us to observe quantitative deviations in the ground state phase boundaries with respect to mean-field theory. The method extends to other systems with a similar hybrid Hilbert space structure, such as spin-phonon systems, and provides a scalable framework for investigating their ground state properties.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that incorporating non-Hermititian physics into spin chain models can dramatically enhance the generation and stabilization of long-distance entanglement in quantum networks. By analyzing dynamics near exceptional points, the authors show that these engineered systems achieve higher, more robust levels of entanglement than their Hermitian counterparts.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses engineered quantum networks using non-Hermitian physics, providing concrete mechanisms (EPs, broken phase) to enhance robust quantum correlations beyond what is possible with standard unitary models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how non-Hermitian dynamics can dynamically generate and stabilize long-distance entanglement (LDE) between distant nodes in quantum networks, comparing this to standard Hermitian evolution.

**Main result.** Non-Hermitian dynamics near exceptional points allow for the emergence of near-unit time-averaged entanglement that cannot be achieved by corresponding Hermitian systems. Furthermore, LDE persists robustly even in the stationary regime.

**Method.** The analysis combines analytical techniques (like determining exceptional points) with numerical simulations to track entanglement evolution over time and across parameter space.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as interacting spin-1/2 bulks weakly coupled to two quantum links, analyzed using an effective non-Hermitian nearest-neighbor Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) model derived from open quantum system dynamics.

**Key observables.** Long-distance entanglement ($\mathcal{E}_{\ell_1\ell_2}$), time-averaged link entanglement ($E_{	ext{avg}}^{\ell_1\ell_2}$), and the location of exceptional points (EPs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak coupling strength ($\lambda$), imaginary magnetic fields ($h'$), system size ($N$), and the SSH model parameter $\delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonians from open quantum systems (e.g., via GKLS master equation) and assumes weak coupling regimes for perturbative calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare entanglement dynamics in the broken vs. unbroken regimes, show how LDE depends on system size $N$ and interaction range ($\alpha$), and map out high-entanglement regions near exceptional point lines in parameter space.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the role of non-Hermiticity by comparing dynamic entanglement generation across different SSH model parameters ($\delta$) and coupling strengths, culminating in a comparison between stationary and time-averaged entanglement results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In distributed quantum networks, interacting spin systems can mediate the generation of highly entangled links between distant nodes. We investigate the role of effective parity-time (PT)-symmetric non-Hermitian spin-1/2 bulks weakly coupled to two quantum links, obtained due to the environmental interactions affecting both the bulk and the links. Focusing on effective non-Hermitian nearest-neighbor (NN) Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) models, we analyze how non-Hermiticity influences the dynamical formation of long-distance entanglement (LDE). For a paradigmatic model consisting of a quantum XX bulk subjected to imaginary staggered magnetic fields, we analytically determine the exceptional points arising from the resulting bulk-mediated interactions between the links. Combining analytical and numerical methods, we demonstrate that an initially fully separable state can dynamically evolve into highly entangled link states near these exceptional points in the broken regime. Further, after optimizing over time and system parameters, near-unit time-averaged entanglement between the links emerges under weak imaginary magnetic fields and bulk-link couplings, which cannot be attained in the corresponding Hermitian systems. Moreover, the non-Hermitian dynamics exhibit a freezing of high entanglement in the vicinity of exceptional points, a feature absent in Hermitian counterparts. We also identify regimes of long-range interaction strengths that yield a higher time-averaged entanglement than the corresponding NN models. Furthermore, we establish that LDE persists in the stationary regime, highlighting the promise of engineered non-Hermitian dynamics for realizing robust and frozen entangled links in quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors successfully implemented a programmable quantum simulation of spin-dependent electron transfer using a trapped ion platform. They modeled the process through a synthetic chiral lattice bridge connecting donor and acceptor sites. The key finding is that spin selectivity arises from quantum interference between multiple competing pathways, providing a robust experimental route to study CISS effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an unprecedentedly controllable quantum simulator platform for studying complex solid-state phenomena like CISS. The ability to tune parameters ($\lambda/t$, $\phi$) allows direct mapping of theoretical predictions regarding interference mechanisms onto measurable quantum dynamics, which is highly valuable for AMO research into open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the unresolved microscopic origin of the Chiral-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) effect in electron transfer through chiral structures.

**Main result.** The research identifies that spin-dependent interference among competing multi-step pathways within the synthetic chiral bridge is the microscopic mechanism responsible for observing spin-selective electron transport.

**Method.** A programmable quantum simulation approach was implemented using a single trapped ion to model the donor–chiral bridge–acceptor system dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system models $	ext{D}–	ext{B}_\chi–	ext{A}$ electron transfer, where the chiral bridge is encoded across four sites ($|	ext{B}_1angle$ to $|	ext{B}_4angle$) on a trapped $^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$ ion. The couplings are tunable using laser and RF drives, simulating nearest-neighbor hopping and spin-dependent next-nearest-neighbor interactions.

**Key observables.** Spin polarization ($P$), population distribution across bridge sites ($	ext{|B}_2angle, 	ext{|B}_3angle, 	ext{|B}_4angle$), and the overall donor-to-acceptor transfer dynamics over time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hopping strengths ($t_j$), SOC strength ($\lambda$), phase ($\phi$), and coupling amplitudes/phases. Key regimes include $\lambda/t$ ratios and specific time windows (e.g., $200–300 \mu	ext{s}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes coherent evolution over specified time windows, and the mechanism relies on interference terms derived from path summation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental scheme (Hamiltonian structure, laser implementation), show spin-dependent time evolution within the chiral bridge, and demonstrate tunable spin polarization in both the bridge dynamics and the final donor-to-acceptor transfer.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model Hamiltonian for $	ext{D}–	ext{B}_\chi–	ext{A}$ on a trapped ion. It then analyzes the intrinsic spin-dependent interference within the chiral bridge, followed by extending this analysis to measure the resulting spin selectivity during the full donor-to-acceptor transfer process.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron transfer through chiral structures can exhibit spin asymmetry, known as the chiral-induced spin selectivity effect, whose microscopic origin remains an open question. While path-interference within the chiral moiety has been proposed as a key mechanism, its experimental validation requires precise and versatile tunability of system parameters. Here we implement a programmable quantum simulation of spin-dependent electron transfer in a donor--chiral-bridge--acceptor model using a trapped ion. The bridge is encoded in internal states of the ion with tunable nearest- and next-nearest-neighbor couplings, while donor and acceptor states are coupled via a spectator bosonic motional mode. We observe spin-dependent interference within the bridge, and further reveal spin-dependence in donor-to-acceptor transfer dynamics, controlled by amplitude and phase of the coupling parameter. Our results identify interference among spin-dependent pathways as a microscopic origin of spin-dependent transfer, and open a route toward quantum simulations of complex chiral lattices with multi-level and bosonic degrees of freedom.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a perturbative theory to analyze the stationary measure of active systems that violate detailed balance. It shows that such conserved active systems exhibit generic nonlocal statistics, characterized by algebraic decay in correlations, which is fundamentally different from local equilibrium states. The analysis successfully predicts this nonlocality and demonstrates its failure to be captured by standard Landau–Ginzburg expansions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings provide a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding how broken detailed balance fundamentally alters the statistical description of steady states in active matter, moving beyond equilibrium concepts like free energy minimization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the generic nonlocal statistics of the stationary measure ($P_\infty$) in conserved active systems, contrasting them with local equilibrium measures.

**Main result.** In conserved active systems that break detailed balance, the stationary measure is generically nonlocal, leading to algebraic decay in correlation functions. This nonlocality prevents the use of a standard Landau–Ginzburg expansion for $P_\infty$.

**Method.** The analysis employs a perturbative expansion in nonlinearity ($\alpha$) around a Gaussian limit, utilizing Large Deviation Theory (LDT) and WKB ansatze to compute the quasipotential ($U = -\epsilon \ln P_\infty$).

**Model / system.** The study applies to various active matter models including Active Model A/B+, Nonreciprocal Cahn–Hilliard, and the Toner–Tu model. These systems are characterized by nonlinear dynamics that break detailed balance.

**Key observables.** Nonlocality of $P_\infty$, algebraic decay exponents ($eta$) of N-point correlation functions, and the structure of the quasipotential coefficients ($U^{(n)}_r$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Activity nonlinearity parameter ($\alpha$), noise strength ($\epsilon$), and dimensionality ($d$). The analysis focuses on the weak noise limit ($\epsilon 	o 0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are derived perturbatively, assuming small nonlinearities in $\alpha$ and $\epsilon$. The failure of the Landau–Ginzburg expansion is noted as a key limitation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that non-equilibrium features (like different interfacial tensions) require nonlocal $U$, contradicting simple local Gibbs decompositions for active systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the contrast between equilibrium and active systems, developing the perturbative machinery using LDT/WKB ansatz, calculating scaling laws in Fourier space ($\hat{u}^{(n)}_r$), and finally demonstrating how conservation laws enforce nonlocality and cause the failure of standard expansions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The stationary measure of equilibrium systems with detailed balance follows a Boltzmann distribution, so that for short-ranged interactions the measure is local, meaning that distant spatial domains are statistically independent. In contrast, active systems break detailed balance, and can have nonlocal stationary measure even for fully local dynamics. Here, by expanding in nonlinearity about a Gaussian-model limit, we construct the measure perturbatively deep in the disordered phase for a class of models that includes Active Model A, Active Model B+, Model AB, the Nonreciprocal Cahn--Hilliard model, and the Toner--Tu model. In this regime, nonlocality is linked to a dynamical conservation law. Our results generically preclude construction of a Landau--Ginzburg expansion of the stationary measure (as opposed to the dynamical equations) for conserved active field theories.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports on measuring the quantum projection noise limit in mesoscopic ensembles of NV spin defects at room temperature. By implementing an advanced readout technique combining polarization control and spin-to-charge conversion, they achieved high contrast necessary to perform accurate spin counting up to 43 spins. This demonstrates a powerful, scalable platform for sensitive magnetometry without extreme operating conditions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum optics/measurement theory with solid-state physics by providing a robust, room-temperature method to probe fundamental quantum noise limits (SQL) in a solid-state platform, which is highly relevant for developing practical quantum sensors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is measuring quantum projection noise (SQL) in mesoscopic solid-state spin ensembles, which is difficult due to overwhelming classical readout noise compared to atomic systems.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated direct measurement of quantum projection noise and performed spin counting on up to 43 spins at room temperature using NV centers. This protocol maintains high sensitivity without requiring cryogenic cooling or strong bias fields.

**Method.** They combine polarization-selective optical excitation with a spin-to-charge conversion (SCC) readout scheme to achieve high ODMR contrast ($>20\%$), enabling the measurement of noise variance ($\epsilon_V(p)$) and subsequent extraction of projection noise.

**Model / system.** The system is mesoscopic ensembles of Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) spin defects in diamond. The analysis involves modeling the total measured photon count variance, which combines fundamental quantum projection noise with measurable readout noise components.

**Key observables.** Quantum projection noise variance ($\epsilon_V^{	ext{proj}}$), ODMR contrast, Rabi oscillation decay, and the number of spins ($N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature operation; ensemble size up to $\sim 43$ spins; high readout contrast ($>20\%$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on separating the total measured variance into fundamental projection noise and measurable readout noise components, assuming controlled optical excitation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spin counting concepts (Stern-Gerlach analogy), optimized ODMR spectra showing contrast enhancement via polarization control and SCC, Rabi oscillations demonstrating high contrast, and plots comparing measured vs. expected projection noise variance as a function of the driving angle $\phi$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the fundamental difficulty (readout noise) to presenting an advanced readout protocol using NV centers in diamond, demonstrating spin counting capability, quantifying the achieved sensitivity improvement, and finally discussing the broad applicability for magnetometry and sensing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum projection noise is the fundamental noise source for the population measurement of spin ensembles. While projection-noise-limited measurements have been extensively studied in atomic systems, corresponding experiments on solid-state spin ensembles remain challenging due to dominant classical readout noise. Here, we report direct measurement of the quantum projection noise of mesoscopic ensembles of nitrogen-vacancy (NV) spin defects at room temperature. Our experiment is enabled by a high optically-detected magnetic resonance (ODMR) contrast of over 20% for a single crystallographic orientation of the defect spins, obtained by combining polarization-selective optical excitation with spin-to-charge conversion. We use our protocol to demonstrate projection noise measurements and spin counting from nanoscale NV ensembles of up to 43 spins. We further demonstrate that the protocol allows for significant gains in sensitivity for magnetometry applications without need for cryogenic operation or high bias magnetic fields.

</details>

**Authors:** Tomas Aguiar, Marcos Cesar de Oliveira

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel theoretical framework using a collective-spin approach to derive the effective Hamiltonian governing uniform magnon excitations in cavity magnonics. By treating the ferromagnet as a single large spin, the authors simplify the derivation significantly compared to site-resolved methods. The key finding is the prediction of an occupation-dependent reduction in the magnon-photon coupling when the system approaches finite-spin saturation under strong driving.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical tool—the collective spin description—to model magnon dynamics in cavity QED settings. The explicit inclusion of finite-size/finite-spin effects is crucial for accurately predicting nonlinear interactions under strong coherent driving, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and circuit QED.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to derive an effective Hamiltonian for uniform magnon dynamics in cavity magnonics using a novel collective-spin approach, bypassing the standard site-resolved bosonic operator method.

**Main result.** The collective formulation successfully yields the effective uniform-mode Hamiltonian and predicts that strong driving leads to an occupation-dependent reduction of the magnon-photon coupling due to finite-spin saturation effects.

**Method.** It employs a direct Holstein-Primakoff transformation applied to the total spin ($\mathbf{S}$) of the ferromagnet, treating it as a single macrospin, contrasting this with the conventional site-resolved approach.

**Model / system.** The system is a ferromagnetic sample modeled by a Heisenberg Hamiltonian coupled to external magnetic fields (static bias and cavity drives). The dynamics are analyzed in the long-wavelength limit, focusing on the uniform magnon mode.

**Key observables.** Effective magnon-photon coupling strength ($G_{	ext{eff}}$), occupation number ($ar{n}_M$), and the resulting nonlinear correction factor.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The total spin $S=Ns$, the low-excitation regime condition ($\langle\hat{M}^\dagger \hat{M}angle \ll 2N_s$), and the ratio of cavity wavelength to sample size.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system is near its fully polarized ground state, that dynamics are dominated by the uniform mode (long-wavelength limit), and neglects surface or non-uniform dipolar effects.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes; the focus is on deriving and comparing Hamiltonian forms (Eq. 9 vs Eq. 17/20) and showing the nonlinear correction formula (Eq. 35).

**Paper structure.** The paper contrasts the standard site-resolved approach with the proposed collective-spin derivation, demonstrating their equivalence for the uniform mode while highlighting the conceptual simplicity of the macrospin treatment.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a direct collective-spin derivation of the effective uniform-mode Hamiltonian used in cavity magnonics. Starting from a nearest-neighbor Heisenberg ferromagnet coupled to long-wavelength magnetic fields, we show that the relevant dynamics can be restricted to the fully symmetric spin sector, where the exchange interaction contributes only a constant energy shift and the ferromagnet behaves as a macrospin of length $Ns$. Applying the Holstein--Primakoff transformation directly to this total spin yields the usual uniform magnon mode and its leading nonlinear corrections without first introducing site-resolved bosonic operators. This collective formulation makes explicit the interpretation of the ferromagnet as a synthetic large-spin atom and provides a compact route to the effective Hamiltonians used in driven and Floquet cavity magnonics. As a physical consequence, the leading nonlinear correction produces an occupation-dependent reduction of the effective magnon--photon coupling, providing a simple signature of finite-spin saturation under strong uniform-mode driving.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proves that even when far from equilibrium and exhibiting high entropy production, the steady-state occupation probabilities on large dense networks resemble the predictions of the Boltzmann distribution. This finding establishes a robust link between local state dynamics (the 'low rattling' heuristic) and global statistical properties in non-equilibrium systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful generalization of equilibrium statistical mechanics concepts to non-equilibrium systems, which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems driven by external fields or dissipation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is extending the Boltzmann distribution concept from equilibrium statistical mechanics to nonequilibrium steady states (NESS). The authors investigate how occupation probabilities behave in such non-equilibrium settings.

**Main result.** They prove that NESS on large dense networks exhibit Boltzmann-like occupation, meaning $\pi_i / \pi_{eq, i} 	o 1$ asymptotically. Furthermore, this occurs even when the system produces extensive entropy.

**Method.** The analysis involves solving for the stationary distribution ($\mathbf{W}oldsymbol{\pi} = \mathbf{0}$) and employing asymptotic methods like random matrix theory and strong laws of large numbers to analyze convergence rates.

**Model / system.** The model is a Markov jump process on $N$ discrete states, defined by a transition rate matrix $\mathbf{W}$ whose elements depend on vertex parameters ($E_j$) and edge weights ($X_{ij}$).

**Key observables.** Steady-state probability ($\pi_i$), equilibrium distribution ($\pi_{eq, i}$), Entropy Production ($\sigma(\mathbf{p})$), and the relative error $\delta_i = \pi_i / \pi_{eq, i} - 1$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Network size $N$ (taken to infinity), edge weights ($X_{ij}$), and vertex energies ($E_j$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the network is large and dense, and requires finite moments (e.g., $	ext{Var}(X) \in (0, \infty)$) for convergence proofs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate relative errors ($\delta_i$) vanishing with $N$ under different weight assumptions, and show comparisons between entropy production rates ($\sigma_{NESS}$ vs. $\sigma_{min}$) that diverge in general cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the mathematical framework for NESS on dense networks, proving the asymptotic Boltzmann-like behavior of occupation probabilities, and then contrasting this result with expectations derived from minimal entropy production principles.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central problem in statistical physics is to extend the Boltzmann distribution to nonequilibrium steady states (NESS). We prove that NESS on large dense networks have Boltzmann-like occupation despite extensive entropy production. We further show that the active-matter heuristic of "low rattling" is asymptotically exact. Intuitively, these NESS spend a greater fraction of their time in states they leave more slowly. This explanation extends to the broader class of "equiaccessible" steady states, which play a role in our analysis akin to that of equilibrium in linear response.

</details>

**Summary.** The research presents an advanced hybrid protocol to deterministically prepare and measure specific long-range spin correlations in fermionic lattice quantum simulators. By combining analog resource state generation with digital gate sequences, the authors demonstrate precise control over entanglement patterns, enabling the study of strongly correlated matter beyond simple ground states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete experimental roadmap for engineering complex entanglement structures in ultracold atomic systems, moving beyond simple ground state preparation towards programmable quantum simulation of exotic phases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is the deterministic preparation of strongly correlated quantum states, such as those found in Heisenberg spin chains, using a hybrid analog-digital protocol.

**Main result.** The method successfully programs and measures non-local spin correlations, achieving high fidelity overlap with theoretical ground states even for larger chain sizes. The process demonstrates control over entanglement beyond the native Hamiltonian dynamics.

**Method.** A hybrid approach combining initial state preparation via analog methods (forming singlet chains) followed by digital programming using tunable collisional quantum gates and controlled atom rearrangement.

**Model / system.** The system is a fermionic lattice quantum simulator loaded with ultracold atoms, modeled by an effective Fermi-Hubbard type Hamiltonian. The resource state involves 'chained singlets' formed through adiabatic preparation from attractive interactions.

**Key observables.** Spin pair-correlators ($\langle S_z^i S_z^j angle$), global singlet fraction, and the overlap fidelity between the prepared state and the target ground state ($|GSangle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate exponent $\alpha$, gate error rate (robust up to $10^{-2}$), lattice depths, and magnetic field parameters for Feshbach resonance control.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The measurement relies on the assumption of SU(2) symmetry for correlator extraction, and long-distance correlations require deterministic atom rearrangement via shuttling/swapping.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate measuring spin pair correlators at various distances ($d=1, 2, 3$) as a function of gate parameters ($\alpha$), showing increased overlap with theory. Other figures detail the circuit protocol and fidelity dependence on error rates for increasing chain lengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the hybrid protocol: first, analog preparation to create singlet chains; second, digital programming using tunable gates (e.g., four-layer circuits) to encode specific correlations; and finally, measurement via quantum gate sequences followed by singlet-pair measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Analog quantum simulation provides a highly controlled platform to study diverse quantum many-body phenomena. However, current methods for state initialisation are limited to thermal ensembles or uncorrelated product states. Here we present a hybrid approach that complements analog preparation with a digital quantum-gate protocol. This approach enables the engineering of target states with specific, long-range spin-correlations from the same initial resource state. By applying collisional gates to adiabatically prepared and filtered four-fermion singlet chains, we program diverse spin-correlation patterns, including that of a Heisenberg chain. We measure the spin correlations using a sequence of quantum gates followed by singlet-pair measurements. Our method paves the way to the targeted preparation of strongly correlated states of matter.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an extension of the Numerical Renormalization Group (NRG) technique, termed OC-FDM, to model non-equilibrium quantum transport in interacting junctions like quantum dots. By coupling the NRG structure to reservoir dynamics via the Bloch-Redfield tensor, it accurately calculates steady-state currents and spectral functions under finite bias or temperature gradients. This method offers a powerful, computationally fast tool for studying strongly correlated physics far from equilibrium.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes advanced numerical techniques (NRG) into the complex domain of open quantum systems far from equilibrium. The ability to handle Kondo physics under bias/gradient using a computationally efficient method is valuable for modeling nanoscale devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates non-equilibrium quantum transport through interacting junctions, specifically addressing how to accurately determine the steady-state density operator when driven by finite bias or temperature gradients.

**Main result.** The proposed Open Chain Full Density Matrix (OC-FDM) approach successfully extends NRG techniques to the non-equilibrium regime, achieving high accuracy for Kondo physics while being computationally efficient compared to other state-of-the-art methods.

**Method.** The method combines the Numerical Renormalization Group (NRG) framework with the Bloch-Redfield master equation formalism. It constructs an effective single-lead description by solving the density matrix evolution iteratively across NRG shells.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a Quantum Dot (QD) or quantum impurity coupled to two leads, described by a Hamiltonian involving Coulomb repulsion and coupling terms. The transport is analyzed under non-equilibrium conditions like finite bias ($\mu_L 
eq \mu_R$) or temperature gradients ($T_L 
eq T_R$).

**Key observables.** Charge and spin currents ($I_\alpha$), differential conductance ($dI/dV$), spectral functions $A(\omega)$, and the steady-state density operator $ho_{	ext{steady}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite bias voltage ($eV$), temperature gradient ($\Delta T$), external magnetic field ($h_0$), and Kondo energy scales ($T_K$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The OC-FDM approach assumes that density matrix elements of discarded states remain unaltered across NRG iterations, which is necessary to solve the master equation in a manageable form.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spectral functions $A(\omega)$ and differential conductance ($dI/dV$) under varying bias and magnetic fields, demonstrating Kondo peak splitting and convergence with established benchmarks.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon NRG for equilibrium systems by augmenting Wilson sites with reservoirs. It introduces the OC-FDM approach to solve the steady-state density operator via the Bloch-Redfield Tensor (BRT) in the non-equilibrium setting, applying this to calculate transport properties like current and conductance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The numerical renormalization group (NRG) approach was developed to identify and quantify different equilibrium regimes of quantum impurity systems (QISs) with unprecedented accuracy by a tailored finite size representation. Out of equilibrium, the steady-state density operator is not of the Boltzmannian form but one that is determined by the imposed boundary conditions. We extend the NRG to the nonequilibrium setting by augmenting each Wilson site with a reservoir, whose coupling functions are calculated via a continuous fraction expansion in order to recover the continuum limit exactly. The nonequilibrium parameters such as a finite bias as well as a finite temperature gradient enters through the Bloch-Redfield tensor (BRT), whose zero eigenvector gives the steady-state density operator. We used the resulting open chain full density matrix (OC-FDM) approach with an effective single lead description to investigate the charge and spin transport through a quantum dot (QD) under finite bias and temperature gradient. The influence of lead asymmetry and an external magnetic field on the transport properties are also studied. For completeness, we have also investigated local properties of the QD, such as charge fluctuations and find excellent agreement with real-time quantum Monte Carlo (RT-QMC) data. The OC-FDM approach was able to explore Kondo energy scales as low as $T_K/D \approx 10^{-8}$ in the non-equilibrium regime, as well as show convergence with the established equilibrium benchmarks, such as a quantitative agreement with full density matrix numerical renormalization group (FDM-NRG) and Fermi-liquid scaling at small bias and temperature. Owing to the effective single lead description, a single OC-FDM data point takes orders of magnitude less time on a standard laptop, compared to other state-of-the-art numerical methods.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores how hypothetical quantum gravity corrections, modeled by GUP/MDR terms, modify the behavior of polarons in fermionic baths. By analyzing spectral and coherence measurements using both theoretical calculations and superconducting quantum processors, the authors demonstrate that these deformations cause measurable shifts in energy spectra and alter the stability of molecular states near crossover points.

**Why it may be interesting.** It bridges fundamental high-energy physics concepts (GUP/MDR) into a highly controllable, experimentally accessible platform (ultracold atoms/quantum simulators), providing testable predictions for deviations from standard condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how ultraviolet (UV) deformations, inspired by Generalized Uncertainty Principles (GUP) or Modified Dispersion Relations (MDR), affect the low-energy many-body physics of a polaron interacting with a molecular bath.

**Main result.** These UV deformations enhance the sensitivity of measurable observables like spectral and Ramsey coherence signals near the polaron-molecule crossover, providing a concrete route to probe quantum gravity effects in ultracold atomic systems.

**Method.** The authors employ both theoretical many-body techniques (e.g., ladder approximations, effective Hamiltonian construction) and advanced digital quantum simulation protocols on superconducting processors.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a polaron impurity interacting with a fermionic bath, described by an extended GUP-deformed Hubbard or polaron-molecule Hamiltonian. The physics spans the crossover between localized polaron states and delocalized molecular bound states.

**Key observables.** Spectral response (via FFT of Ramsey signal), coherence overlap $S(t)$, effective mass ($m^*$), and the shift/renormalization of resonance energies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The GUP deformation parameter ($eta$), low energy scales, and the polaron-molecule crossover regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is phenomenological, treating UV deformations as controlled corrections to an effective low-energy Hamiltonian; quantum simulations rely on approximations like Trotter-Suzuki decomposition and error mitigation techniques.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the systematic shift of spectral peaks towards higher energies with increasing $eta$, changes in Ramsey coherence contrast, and phase diagrams mapping out how the crossover point shifts based on the deformation strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from theoretical derivations of GUP-corrected propagators and effective interactions to concrete quantum simulation protocols (Ramsey interferometry) used to measure these effects on superconducting hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that impurity many-body observables can display enhanced sensitivity to ultraviolet deformations of generalized-uncertainty-principle and modified-dispersion-relation type at accessible energy scales. Using a deformed polaron-molecule Hamiltonian constructed to preserve the infrared sector, we quantify the impact of such deformations on spectral and Ramsey observables and implement the corresponding dynamics in a controlled quantum computing setting. We identify regimes near the polaron-molecule crossover where small ultraviolet deformations are strongly amplified, leading to experimentally resolvable changes in quasiparticle properties and spectral response. Our results establish a concrete sensitivity-based route to low-energy quantum-gravity phenomenology in a well-defined many-body platform and delimit the validity of the effective description. Furthermore, we report experimental validation on the QRed superconducting quantum processor (BSC-CNS).

</details>

The probability density consists of a continuous distribution and a delta...</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper develops a quasiprobability approach to extend covariant fluctuation theorems into the challenging realm of relativistic quantum systems. By combining Wigner and Margenau-Hill distributions, the authors successfully derive generalized Jarzynski equalities while demonstrating that the full Crooks theorem only holds for specific linear combinations of work and heat 4-vectors due to operator non-commutativity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical framework for applying non-equilibrium statistical mechanics concepts (fluctuation theorems) to relativistic quantum field theory, which is crucial for understanding energy exchange in high-energy or curved spacetime contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is extending covariant fluctuation theorems, which treat work as a 4-vector, into the quantum regime where operator non-commutativity complicates defining joint probability distributions.

**Main result.** While the standard covariant Crooks theorem fails for the joint distribution due to non-commutativity, the Jarzynski equality holds covariantly, and the fluctuation theorems can be recovered for specific linear combinations of work and heat 4-vectors.

**Method.** The authors introduce a novel quasiprobability distribution combining Wigner and Margenau-Hill formalisms to handle the joint characteristic function of the work 4-vector in a covariant manner.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on relativistic quantum fields ($\phi(x)$) driven by external classical sources, considering processes between initial and final spacelike hypersurfaces ($\Sigma^-$ to $\Sigma^+$).

**Key observables.** Work 4-vector ($W^\mu$), heat 4-vector ($Q^\mu$), joint characteristic functions ($\chi_{WMH}$), and the free energy difference ($\Delta F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature/Inverse Temperature ($eta$), Lorentz transformations (covariance), and coupling strengths for open systems.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on defining a mixed quasiprobability distribution, which is real but not positive-definite; the Crooks theorem only holds for specific linear combinations of observables.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate numerical results showing the real part of the characteristic function and quantifying the deviation from the standard Crooks relation, while also verifying the relation for specific rest-frame components.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for a quasiprobability approach due to non-commutativity. It then derives the unique mixed Wigner/MH distribution satisfying covariance. Finally, it applies this framework to derive and verify generalized covariant fluctuation theorems (Jarzynski and Crooks) for both closed and open quantum systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The covariant formulation of stochastic thermodynamics requires treating the stochastic work as a 4-vector, posing significant challenges for quantum systems due to the non-commutativity. We introduce a new quasiprobability distribution for the work 4-vector, which combines the Wigner and Margenau-Hill quasiprobabilities. This extends the covariant fluctuation theorems from classical to quantum regime. We illustrate our findings with a scalar field driven by classical particles with a generalized version of trace formula. Our work establishes a quasiprobability approach to studying relativistic quantum thermodynamics in a covariant way.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper finds a new set of degenerate solutions to the massless Dirac equation, which describe particles whose spin rotates precisely in sync with external electric and magnetic fields. This synchronization property is proposed as the physical mechanism for creating high-density optical memories using graphene or similar 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The coupling between particle spin dynamics (a quantum property) and classical electromagnetic field manipulation via a relativistic equation provides an excellent platform for studying light-matter interaction in 2D topological materials relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates a novel class of degenerate solutions to the massless Dirac equation and explores their potential applications for developing optical memory devices.

**Main result.** These solutions demonstrate that particle spin rotates synchronously with the electric and magnetic fields generated by circularly polarized electromagnetic waves. This property suggests a mechanism for writing/reading binary data using light on materials like graphene.

**Method.** The authors derived the degenerate spinors by imposing specific conditions ($\Psi^\dagger\gamma\Psi = 0$) on the massless Dirac equation, followed by calculating the resulting electromagnetic fields and spin components.

**Model / system.** The model is based on massless Dirac fermions governed by the Dirac equation in the presence of external electromagnetic potentials. The physical system of interest is graphene or similar materials supporting these massless fermions.

**Key observables.** Particle spin ($\mathbf{S}$), electric field ($\mathbf{E}$), and magnetic field ($\mathbf{B}$) components, which are shown to be synchronized.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis uses natural units ($\hbar=c=1$). The behavior is dependent on the structure of the electromagnetic 4-potential ($b_\mu$) and associated constants ($c_1, c_2, f_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the validity of describing the system using massless Dirac fermions. For localized solutions, the function $f$ must be chosen to ensure square integrability.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 proposes an experimental setup for optical memories in graphene, and Figure 2 illustrates the concept of recording binary data via spin manipulation on such a material.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem by presenting the governing Dirac equation. It then derives the general form of degenerate solutions using specific mathematical constraints. Subsequently, it calculates the associated electromagnetic fields and demonstrates the key physical result: synchronized spin rotation under external waves, concluding with potential applications in optical memory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this article, we present a novel class of degenerate solutions to the massless Dirac equation, corresponding to a wide variety of electromagnetic 4-potentials and fields, including both zero field and circularly polarized electromagnetic waves. An interesting property of these solutions is that the spin of the particles rotates in synchronization with the electric and magnetic fields of the electromagnetic waves. These results could be utilized for the development of optical memories based on materials supporting massless Dirac fermions, such as graphene.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies tools from statistical physics and complex systems theory to analyze a massive historical news corpus. By tracking semantic drift, word salience shifts, and informational entropy over time, the authors successfully identified major societal turning points, such as Italy's political transition in the mid-1990s, without needing prior knowledge of these events.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not physics-based, the methodology of detecting non-equilibrium transitions (regime shifts) in complex data streams—using diagnostics like entropy minima or sudden changes in correlation structure—is mathematically analogous to identifying critical points or phase transitions in physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to detect historical turning points or regime shifts within media discourse by treating the evolution of language use as a complex, non-equilibrium system.

**Main result.** Significant transitions in Italian media discourse were detected around the mid-1990s (First to Second Republic transition) and during major international conflicts, characterized by measurable changes in semantic structure and word salience.

**Method.** The study combines Natural Language Processing (NLP)—using techniques like TF-IDF and LSA—with tools from complex systems theory, such as changepoint detection (PELT) and entropy analysis.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the evolving discourse of a large diachronic corpus ($\approx 600,000$ articles from *La Repubblica*). The state space is mapped into a reduced semantic vector space via LSA, allowing tracking of the 'center of mass' trajectory over time.

**Key observables.** Word salience (lexical level), Semantic Trajectory (semantic level), Spectral Entropy $H(p)$, and the number/timing of statistical changepoints.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The corpus time span (1985–2000); the aggregation timescale for analysis (day, week, month, year).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The discourse evolution can be accurately captured by tracking changes in word co-occurrence statistics and semantic vector centroids; the single-source nature of the corpus limits generalizability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate document length distributions, time series of word salience (showing rising/falling political terms), the trajectory of media discourse on principal components, and similarity matrices showing block structures corresponding to distinct historical regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a progression from corpus description and basic statistics (token counts, Zipf's law) to advanced time-series analysis: first lexical dynamics using Fano factors/PELT, followed by semantic mapping using LSA trajectories and entropy calculations to pinpoint regime shifts.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The increasing availability of large-scale textual corpora has opened new possibilities for data-driven, quantitative approaches to historical analysis using Natural Language Processing (NLP). However, diachronic corpora with historical relevance from the pre-digital era remain scarce and often incomplete. We present a quantitative approach to historical analysis based on the reconstruction and exploration of a diachronic corpus of around 600,000 articles from the Italian newspaper "La Repubblica", covering all the articles published from the 1st of January 1985 to the 31st of December 2000 - a period of major political, social, and geopolitical change in Italy and globally. Using NLP techniques, we analyze the text at both lexical and semantic levels; we then apply tools from complex systems and statistical physics to trace shifts in media discourse over time. This allows us to detect key transition periods, such as the transition from the First Republic to the Second Republic in Italy, or major international conflicts like the Gulf War or the Kosovo War, without relying on prior labeling. The results show how combining computational linguistics with ideas from complex systems can offer new quantitative insight into historical changes, opening up new paths for studying the dynamics of media and society through large-scale textual data.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes autocatalytic dynamics where particles diffuse outside a sphere while replicating upon reaching the boundary. The competition between diffusion escape and self-catalysis leads to three distinct long-time regimes: extinction, steady-state maintenance, or explosive growth. By solving moment equations using advanced mathematical techniques like Laplace transforms, the authors characterize the precise scaling laws governing these transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical diffusion and reaction kinetics, the concept of phase transitions (subcritical/critical/supercritical) based on a control parameter ($q_c$) is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and pattern formation in complex systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the collective dynamics of independent particles diffusing outside a spherical surface where they undergo autocatalytic replication at a prescribed rate.

**Main result.** The system exhibits three distinct long-time regimes (subcritical, critical, supercritical) characterized by different behaviors for population moments and mean size, including power-law or exponential growth/decay.

**Method.** The analysis uses generating functions and integral equations to track the time evolution of population moments and probability distributions. Asymptotic behavior is determined using Laplace transforms in the long-time limit.

**Model / system.** The model describes diffusion in an exterior domain (outside a ball) with autocatalytic reactions occurring on the boundary surface ($\partial\Omega$). The dynamics are governed by the competition between particle escape to infinity and local branching/replication events.

**Key observables.** Mean population size $\langle N(t) angle$, variance, higher-order moments ($N_k$), and the full probability distribution $Q_k(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The catalytic rate ($q_c$) relative to a critical value ($q_{crit}$), which determines the system's long-term fate.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes rotational symmetry of the domain (exterior of a ball) and relies on explicit forms for diffusion propagators. The asymptotic results are noted to be expected to hold for generic exterior domains.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of population moments ($N_k$) across subcritical, critical, and supercritical regimes, comparing numerical integration against derived asymptotic formulas.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the dynamics via propagators in an unbounded domain to deriving integral equations for moments. It then performs a detailed analysis of the long-time asymptotics by examining different parameter regimes ($q_c$ vs $q_{crit}$) using Laplace transforms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the collective dynamics of independent particles that diffuse outside a spherical surface, on which they are replicated with a prescribed catalytic rate. In spatial dimensions three and higher, the transient nature of diffusion creates the competition between autocatalytic and escape events, thus leading to a rich phase diagram between subcritical (extinction), critical (steady-state), and supercritical (growth) regimes at long times. The rotational symmetry of the domain and an explicit form of the single-particle diffusion propagator allow us to obtain the statistics of the population size (i.e., the number of particles). In this way, we analyze the mean population size, its variance and higher-order moments, as well as the full distribution. In particular, we obtain a fully explicit form of the distribution at long times and describe a slow, power-law approach to this steady-state limit.

</details>

### [Generic long-range correlations in nonequilibrium mixtures](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14651v1)

**Authors:** Jessica Metzger, Yariv Kafri, Mehran Kardar, Julien Tailleur

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that unlike equilibrium systems, non-equilibrium mixtures can spontaneously develop long-ranged three-point correlations due to the presence of nonlinear terms. These effects are predicted theoretically using renormalization group methods and confirmed by simulating Brownian colloids in multi-temperature baths. This suggests that long-range order is a generic feature of many out-of-equilibrium physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful mechanism—the role of 'dangerously irrelevant' nonlinear terms—to generate robust, non-trivial long-range correlations in systems driven out of equilibrium, which is highly relevant for understanding active matter and complex fluids.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the origin of long-range correlations in non-equilibrium mixtures, contrasting them with equilibrium systems where such correlations typically require fine-tuning to a critical point.

**Main result.** Nonlinearities, even those formally 'dangerously irrelevant' in renormalization group theory, can generate genuinely long-ranged three-point correlations in the isotropic disordered phase of non-equilibrium mixtures.

**Method.** The analysis combines theoretical predictions using renormalization group arguments and perturbative calculations of correlation functions with numerical simulations of Brownian colloids.

**Model / system.** The system models generic dynamics of conserved, isotropic fields (applicable to multi-temperature or non-reciprocal mixtures) and is simulated using overdamped Brownian particles interacting via a soft repulsive potential.

**Key observables.** Two-point and three-point correlation functions ($\langle \phi_\alpha \phi_eta \phi_\gamma angle$), analyzed in both real space (decaying as $r^{-2d}$) and Fourier space, particularly focusing on the low-wavenumber limit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative temperatures ($T_A 
eq T_B$) of the components are crucial; long-range correlations appear when temperatures are unequal.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on treating nonlinearities perturbatively, justified by their irrelevant nature in the renormalization group sense.

**Figures summary.** Figures show evidence of long-range three-point correlations ($\sim r^{-2}$ decay) in both 1D and 2D simulations, comparing numerical results against theoretical predictions in real and Fourier space.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general theory for non-equilibrium mixtures, showing that linear theory yields short-range correlations. It then calculates three-point functions perturbatively, demonstrating the emergence of long-range behavior due to nonlinearities, which is confirmed by simulations using Brownian colloids.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study correlation functions in generic non-equilibrium mixtures, including multi-temperature systems and non-reciprocal field theories. The corresponding linear theory is short-ranged, and nonlinearities are irrelevant in the renormalization-group sense. Nonetheless, we find that these nonlinearities generate long-ranged three-point correlations in the isotropic disordered phase. Our analytical predictions, which are based on a phenomenological theory, are confirmed by numerical simulations of Brownian colloids in contact with thermal baths at different temperatures. Dangerously irrelevant nonlinearities in non-equilibrium mixtures thus offer a new route to long-range correlations, supporting the hypothesis that such correlations are not the exception but the rule out of equilibrium.

</details>
