**Summary.** The paper presents an advanced quantum sensing technique using Hong-Ou-Mandel interference on polarization-entangled photons. By controlling the exchange symmetry between bosonic and fermionic Bell states via a geometric phase imprinted on the pump beam, the method achieves high sensitivity for measuring physical parameters like birefringence. This approach overcomes limitations of conventional setups by keeping the sample outside the main interferometer arms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel platform for quantum metrology by engineering the fundamental symmetries of the two-photon state, offering robustness against common experimental noise sources that plague standard interferometers.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Conventional quantum sensing schemes based on Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interference are susceptible to optical loss and instability when a sample is placed in one interferometer arm.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate a robust, symmetry-controlled quantum sensing scheme where the exchange symmetry of two-photon states can be continuously tuned using geometric phase, allowing for high-resolution measurement of birefringence changes by placing the sample in the pump beam.

**Method.** The technique utilizes polarization-entangled photon pairs generated via SPDC and measures coincidence counts as a function of an engineered relative phase ($\phi$) to probe the transition between bosonic (bunching) and fermionic (anti-bunching) two-photon states.

**Model / system.** The system involves two-photon interference in a setup where the geometric phase imprinted on the classical pump beam controls the exchange symmetry of the entangled Bell state. The sensing element is typically a birefringent crystal placed directly in the pump beam path.

**Key observables.** Coincidence probability $P_c(\phi, \delta)$, which varies sinusoidally with the relative phase $\phi$, and the resulting change in coincidence counts as a function of temperature ($\Delta T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The phase-modulation linewidth remains fixed at $\pi/2$ independent of photon bandwidth; birefringence resolution achieved down to $10^{-6}$ or better.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method avoids limitations by measuring the sample effect on the pump beam rather than placing it in the main interferometer arms, and relies on established thermo-optic dispersion formulas for analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup schematic, reconstruction of Bell state density matrices, coincidence counts vs. optical delay (showing bunching/anti-bunching), and crucially, coincidence count variation as a function of temperature-induced birefringence changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of conventional HOM sensing. It then introduces the symmetry-controlled scheme using geometric phase to tune Bell states. The core is demonstrating this control via coincidence measurements, culminating in applying this robust method to measure material properties like thermo-dispersive birefringence with high resolution.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interference has become a central resource for quantum sensing and metrology owing to its sensitivity to temporal delay and photon indistinguishability. However, existing HOM-based sensing schemes generally rely on inserting a sample into one arm of the interferometer, making the measurement vulnerable to optical loss, alignment instability, and bandwidth-dependent distortion of the interference profile. Here, we demonstrate a symmetry-controlled quantum sensing scheme based on continuous transitions between symmetric (bosonic-like) and antisymmetric (fermionic-like) Bell states in two-photon interference. By imprinting a geometric phase onto the classical pump beam and transferring it to polarization-entangled photons generated via spontaneous parametric down-conversion, we coherently tune the exchange symmetry of the entangled state without altering the temporal or spectral indistinguishability of the photons. The HOM response evolves continuously from bunching to antibunching with a sine square phase dependence, producing a coincidence modulation of approximately 10 * 10^4 counts s^-1 counts/s. In contrast to conventional HOM sensing, the phase-modulation linewidth remains fixed at pi/2, independent of photon bandwidth. Using a birefringent crystal placed directly in the pump beam, we measure thermo-dispersive birefringence with a resolution of the order of 10^{-6} over a broad temperature range. Our results establish exchange symmetry as a controllable resource for robust quantum sensing and symmetry-engineered photonic quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a novel method to synchronize remote quantum nodes using only independent miniature atomic clocks rather than dedicated timing links. By applying advanced computational post-processing techniques, the authors achieved timing stability comparable to state-of-the-art systems. This breakthrough establishes a scalable and topology-independent pathway for building large-scale quantum networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides an experimental blueprint for realizing scalable quantum networks by circumventing major infrastructure bottlenecks associated with timing distribution, which is crucial for future terrestrial and space-based quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Achieving precise timing synchronization for distributed quantum networking nodes without relying on dedicated, infrastructure-heavy timing distribution links.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated high-visibility Hong-Ou-Mandel interference across a metropolitan-scale fiber network using only independently operating atomic clocks and computational post-processing, matching benchmarks set by White Rabbit systems.

**Method.** They employed a two-stage coincidence peak search algorithm combined with piecewise linear drift compensation applied to raw time-tagging data from miniature rubidium atomic clocks (MACs).

**Model / system.** The experiment utilized a deployed metropolitan-scale telecom fiber network connecting three nodes, using MACs as the primary timing reference source for quantum state distribution.

**Key observables.** Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interference visibility, coincidence peak Full Width at Half Maximum (FWHM), and timing stability over 8 hours.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Metropolitan scale deployment; rubidium atomic clock precision; $70\%$ HOM visibility threshold; $	ext{sub-nanosecond}$ timing performance goal.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The residual frequency drift between independent MACs is slow and deterministic enough to be accurately modeled and corrected via post-processing algorithms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the two-stage coincidence peak identification algorithm (coarse vs. fine search) and compare HOM visibility/FWHM performance across WRS, uncorrected MAC, and drift-corrected MAC in both lab and deployed settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the synchronization challenge, detailing the computational method for handling large time offsets, validating the technique on local fiber spools (comparing FWHM), and culminating in the primary demonstration of high-visibility quantum interference across a real, deployed metropolitan network.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Precise timing synchronization is essential for distributed quantum networking, enabling entanglement distribution, quantum teleportation, and entanglement swapping across remote nodes. Existing synchronization architectures rely on dedicated timing-distribution infrastructure, most notably White Rabbit networks, which constrain topology, scalability, and deployment in free-space and satellite environments. Here we demonstrate link-free synchronization of quantum network nodes using independently operating miniature rubidium atomic clocks and computational post-processing. We validate the approach on a deployed metropolitan-scale telecom fiber network spanning three geographically separated nodes. Following drift correction, atomic-clock-based synchronization achieves timing performance approaching that of a White Rabbit benchmark and remains stable over continuous 8-hour operation. As a stringent test of quantum-network functionality, we observe Hong-Ou-Mandel interference across spatially separated nodes with visibility exceeding 70%, statistically equivalent to that obtained using dedicated White Rabbit timing links. To the best of our knowledge, this represents the first observation of quantum interference across a deployed metropolitan-scale telecom fiber network synchronized entirely without dedicated timing-transfer infrastructure. These results establish atomic-clock-based synchronization as a scalable, topology-independent alternative to conventional timing-distribution architectures and a practical pathway toward terrestrial, airborne, and space-based quantum networks where dedicated timing links are unavailable.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates an advanced method for generating high-quality, polarization-entangled photon pairs using periodically poled van der Waals semiconductors. By engineering the nonlinearity via quasi-phase matching across multiple thin layers, they overcome material coherence length limitations inherent to these nanoscale sources. The combination of theory and experiment establishes a powerful platform for scalable quantum light source development.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes quantum light sources into nanoscale solid-state platforms, providing a pathway to engineer entanglement properties (like polarization) in systems where traditional bulk optics are impractical or inefficient.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to efficiently generate high-fidelity, polarization-entangled photon pairs using Spontaneous Parametric Down-Conversion (SPDC) in ultrathin van der Waals semiconductors.

**Main result.** By employing periodically-poled structures (PPTMDs), the pair-production rate can be scaled up significantly while maintaining entanglement fidelities exceeding 99% by utilizing quasi-phase matching.

**Method.** The work combines rigorous theoretical modeling based on canonical quantization with detailed experimental characterization using quantum state tomography and coincidence measurements.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes periodically poled TMDs (like $3R$-MoS$_2$) which act as nonlinear media. The theory models the interaction Hamiltonian derived from the second-order nonlinearity ($\chi^{(2)}$) across multiple stacked, sign-inverted slabs to achieve quasi-phase matching.

**Key observables.** Entanglement fidelity, pair-production rate enhancement (ENH), and state purity ($P$) measured via two-photon interference visibility ($V$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The material coherence length ($L_c$), the periodicity of poling, and the relative phase/polarization angle ($	heta, \phi$) controlling the Bell state mixture.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include the undepleted pump approximation and using first-order Dyson perturbative expansion in the theoretical model. Experimentally, high alignment precision is crucial for scaling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup (CW laser on PPTMDs), measured density matrices confirming Bell states ($\vert\phi^-angle, \vert\psi^+angle$), and quantitative plots showing entanglement dependence on pump polarization angle and pair generation rate enhancement vs. slab number.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the limitations of bulk SPDC to proposing PPTMDs for QPM. It details the theoretical framework using Hamiltonian quantization, presents experimental measurements confirming state generation and fidelity, and concludes by analyzing the interplay between crystal symmetry and engineered periodicity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Efficient generation of entangled photons typically relies on spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) in phase-matched macroscopic nonlinear media. However, generating entanglement under phase-matching constraints requires additional bulk optics or interferometers. In contrast, ultrathin van der Waals semiconductors - such as transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) - exhibit strong enough optical nonlinearities for SPDC to be observed from subwavelength-thick media, thereby bypassing conventional phase-matching constraints. In this microscopic domain, the intrinsic crystal symmetry governs the nonlinear optical response, enabling the native generation of polarization-entangled photon pairs. However, generating these states efficiently has been fundamentally restricted by the material's coherence length ($L_c$), which limits the attainable conversion efficiency. Here, we investigate periodically-poled TMDs (PPTMDs) designed to scale up this interaction via quasi-phase matching. We demonstrate that mechanically flipping the sign of the nonlinearity at precise intervals of $L_c$ introduces quasi-phase matching, that scales the pair-production rate while preserving the pristine, symmetry-generated polarization entanglement, with fidelities exceeding 99%. Backed by a rigorous theoretical model, our work clarifies the interplay between crystal symmetry and propagation effects in thin nonlinear media, providing a new avenue for engineering quantum light in nanophotonic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the interaction dynamics of spinor solitons in $F=1$ BECs, examining configurations like dark-bright-dark. The research develops an effective Lagrangian model whose predictions are tested against numerical simulations. It reveals that while attraction can form bound states, spin interactions cause these to break via particle exchange, highlighting the complexity beyond simple non-spinor models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles complex non-linear dynamics in ultracold atomic systems, specifically addressing how internal spin degrees of freedom modify classical soliton interactions and lead to particle redistribution.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the spin-mixing-induced dynamics of spinor solitons within $F=1$ Bose Einstein Condensates (BECs), focusing on interactions between dark and bright soliton configurations.

**Main result.** While initial attraction can lead to bound states, these states break down due to particle exchange dynamics induced by spin interactions. An effective classical model successfully captures the essential features of soliton behavior in most cases.

**Method.** The authors employ numerical simulations using Runge-Kutta integrators and develop an effective classical model based on a Lagrangian approach to describe the time evolution of variational parameters.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a 1D spinor $F=1$ BEC governed by dimensionless spinor Gross-Pitaevskii equations (SGPEs) for three hyperfine states. The dynamics are analyzed in terms of dark and bright solitons interacting via spin-mixing terms.

**Key observables.** Soliton densities ($|\Psi_m|^2$), interspecies particle exchange, effective interaction strength (repulsive/attractive), and variational parameters ($\xi(t)$, $\varphi(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spin-dependent coefficient $g_s$ (determining F or AF interactions), initial phase difference ($\Delta\phi$), and the quadratic Zeeman term ($q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The effective model relies on the adiabatic approximation, which breaks down when large soliton overlaps occur, particularly in out-of-phase (OP) configurations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the predictions of the effective classical model against numerical simulations for both In-Phase (IP) and Out-of-Phase (OP) interactions, showing fidelity metrics and particle number evolution.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the governing SGPEs and analyzes basic soliton interactions. It then develops an effective Lagrangian model to describe two-soliton dynamics, comparing its predictions against detailed numerical simulations for various phase configurations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We explore soliton interactions in a homogeneous spinor $F=1$ Bose Einstein Condensate (BEC) in the presence of a magnetic field, focusing on dark bright dark and bright dark bright configurations. We investigate how these interactions depend on the phase differences among bright solitons and their influence during the dynamics. Our findings align with prior non spinor results, i.e., repulsion among in phase bright solitons and attraction among out of phase pairs in self repulsive atomic BECs. The potential bright soliton attraction, added to the short range repulsion of dark dark soliton interactions, can lead to bound states. However, we find that these bound states break in the presence of spinor interactions due to the particle exchange dynamics between the hyperfine states of the components. Additonally, we develop an effective classical model to describe the soliton dynamics, using a Lagrangian approach. The accuracy of the model is tested by comparing it against numerical simulations. Our results suggest that the proposed model captures the essential features of soliton behavior in the presence of spin interactions, and provides congruent soliton trajectories and interspecies particle exchange dynamics in most of the cases.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an operational framework to certify the genuine photon-number resolving capability of PNR detectors. By employing a 'guessing game' protocol with coherent state probes, the authors developed a scalable method to quantify detector performance without full tomography. They successfully applied this to test a superconducting nanowire detector, establishing critical benchmarks for efficiency and resolution.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, scalable diagnostic tool for characterizing fundamental quantum hardware components (PNR detectors) that are crucial building blocks for quantum communication and computation protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is establishing a practical, operational metric to certify the genuine resolution (the ability to distinguish between different photon numbers) of Photon-Number-Resolving (PNR) detectors.

**Main result.** The authors developed a scalable protocol using coherent state probes that successfully certified genuine four-outcome resolution for a tested PNR-SNSPD, while also quantifying the critical dependence of this resolution on detector efficiency.

**Method.** They introduced an operational framework based on quantum measurement theory and utilized a 'guessing game' protocol involving weak coherent states to derive lower bounds on the genuine resolution by comparing measured guessing probabilities against theoretical witness inequalities.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on characterizing PNR detectors, specifically testing a 28-pixel superconducting nanowire single-photon detector (PNR-SNSPD). The analysis is framed within the context of efficiency-limited quantum channels acting on Fock states.

**Key observables.** Genuine Resolution ($R$), Guessing Probability ($P_{	ext{guess}}$), Effective Efficiency ($\eta^*_m$), and statistical significance ($Z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detector efficiency ($\eta$), the maximum photon number subspace limit ($m$), and the intensity bound ($I$) used in untrusted source models.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol avoids full measurement tomography by relying only on measurable input-output statistics from coherent probes; statistical analysis uses conservative bounds like Azuma–Hoeffding inequality.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the conceptual framework of quantifying photon counting ability, show data acquisition workflows using oscilloscope traces and histogram analysis, and present tables summarizing threshold efficiencies ($\eta_{	ext{th}}$) and certified effective efficiencies ($\eta^*_m$) for various subspaces ($m$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the theoretical gap (lack of certification metric) to proposing an operational framework using coherent state probes. It then details the mathematical derivation of witness inequalities, presents experimental data analysis comparing measured $P_{	ext{guess}}$ against trusted/untrusted bounds, and concludes by reporting certified resolution levels and effective efficiencies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photon-number-resolving (PNR) detectors are essential components of photonic quantum technologies, yet thus far, no practical metric exists to certify how many photons they can genuinely resolve in a single measurement. Here we introduce an operational framework for quantifying the capability of a PNR detector to distinguish between different numbers of photons, i.e. its genuine resolution. In turn, we develop a practical and scalable protocol for certifying the genuine resolution of a detector, which is based on coherent state probes. We apply the method to a 28-pixel photon-number-resolving superconducting nanowire single-photon detector (PNR-SNSPD) and certify genuine four-outcome resolution. Our work highlights the critical requirements in terms of detector efficiency towards achieving high genuine resolution. This approach provides an operational benchmark for PNR detectors and fills a crucial gap in the characterization of photonic quantum devices.

</details>

B. **Summary.** This paper rigorously calculates the Stabilizer Rényi entropy for solvable spin chains across various physical geometries. By deriving exact finite-size formulas using Pfaffian methods, it establishes a lattice benchmark that precisely maps out how entanglement measures behave when moving away from critical points into massive or crossover regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides rigorous, exact lattice results for a complex entanglement measure (SRE) in critical/crossover regimes, offering crucial benchmarks for theoretical models derived from Quantum Field Theory and CFT.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is understanding how the scaling structure of Stabilizer Rényi entropy (SRE) behaves in quantum many-body systems when moving away from critical points into massive or crossover regimes.

**Main result.** Exact finite-size formulas are derived for SRE in both periodic and subsystem geometries, providing a lattice benchmark that extends QFT descriptions beyond isolated conformal points.

**Method.** The authors use exact solvability techniques applicable to free-fermion spin chains, deriving results via Pfaffian methods and analyzing the factorization properties of correlation matrices across different boundary conditions (periodic vs. interval).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on a broad class of finite-range spin chains, particularly the anisotropic XY chain, analyzed in both full periodic geometry (ring) and as an embedded subsystem (interval).

**Key observables.** Stabilizer Rényi entropy ($	ext{SRE}$), $	ext{Det}_1(G)$ (absolute minor determinant of the correlation matrix), and various scaling functions ($\Phi_{\chi}(\sigma)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The anisotropy parameter ($h$), the coupling strength ($\gamma$), system size ($L$ or $\ell$), and the Rényi index ($\alpha=1/2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on specific algebraic structures (Toeplitz/periodic matrices) that allow the intractable absolute-minor problem to be reduced to solvable determinant/Pfaffian forms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate parameter spaces showing critical lines ($h^2 + \gamma^2 = 1$) and compare exact formulas derived for periodic vs. interval geometries, highlighting boundary effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes SRE in different physical settings: first the full periodic chain (volume law, cusp), then the subsystem geometry (boundary contributions via Pfaffians), and finally comparing three specific block representations ($	ext{XY}$, $	ext{XYR}$, $	ext{XYL}$) to establish universal scaling laws across regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Stabilizer Rényi entropy has emerged as a probe of nonstabilizerness in quantum many-body systems, but its scaling structure beyond critical points remains poorly understood compared with entanglement entropy. Recent field-theory approaches indicate that stabilizer entropy contains universal critical data and boundary-sensitive terms, raising the question of how these structures extend into massive and crossover regimes. We address this problem for a broad class of finite-range spin chains at Rényi index one-half. We derive exact finite-size formulas for both full periodic chains and finite intervals of the infinite chain, making the universal crossover from critical to noncritical behavior analytically accessible. In periodic geometry, the entropy obeys a volume law away from criticality and exhibits a universal finite-size crossover controlled by the competition between system size and correlation length. We also show that the large-scale SRE density develops a cusp across the field-tuned critical line, while the XX endpoint is governed by a distinct scaling regime associated with the saturation point. In the subsystem geometry, the interval entropy separates bulk critical behavior from boundary contributions generated by the way the finite region cuts the infinite chain. The crossover from critical to massive behavior is then encoded in boundary constants and universal functions controlled by the correlation length. Through exact stabilizer-entropy correspondences, the scaling theory extends to internal XY reductions, Finite-range spin chains, and Cluster--Ising representatives. Our results provide an exact lattice benchmark for the emerging QFT description of stabilizer entropy beyond isolated conformal points.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors designed and utilized an integrated Ultrahigh Vacuum cluster tool to study Nitrogen-Vacancy centers in diamond. This platform uniquely combines surface science tools like XPS/LEED with cryogenic NV spin measurement capabilities, allowing researchers to directly correlate controlled surface chemistry changes with the resulting quantum coherence of the defect center.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly sophisticated experimental realization of controlling solid-state quantum emitters by engineering their immediate physical environment at the atomic scale. The ability to correlate chemical state (XPS) directly with quantum coherence ($T_2$) is crucial for building robust quantum memories and sensors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is correlating diamond surface chemistry/structure with the spin and charge properties of shallow Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers, as surface contaminants cause decoherence and instability.

**Main result.** An integrated UHV cluster tool was developed that allows for direct, in situ correlation between controlled diamond surface modifications and measurable NV spin dynamics, establishing a pathway for reproducible quantum sensing.

**Method.** The authors utilized an ultrahigh vacuum (UHV) cluster tool integrating separate chambers for surface science (XPS, LEED, annealing) and cryogenic confocal microscopy for single-NV measurements.

**Model / system.** The system is a complex experimental platform comprising three interconnected modules: a load-lock, a surface science chamber capable of thermal/gas treatment, and a cryogenic microscope chamber. This setup allows atomic-scale control over the diamond surface while simultaneously performing quantum optical measurements on NV centers.

**Key observables.** NV spin coherence times ($T_1$, $T_2$), charge state stability, background Photoluminescence (PL) intensity, XPS core-level spectra ($	ext{C}1s$, $	ext{O}1s$), and LEED patterns.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultrahigh vacuum base pressures ($\sim 5 	imes 10^{-10}$ mbar); cryogenic temperatures (down to $4	ext{ K}$); annealing temperatures up to $>1000^\circ	ext{C}$; magnetic field alignment precision better than $0.5^\circ$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system's ability to maintain vacuum integrity while performing complex mechanical and optical manipulations is assumed; the observed PL changes are attributed to surface charging effects mediated by adsorbates.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the schematic layout of the three-module UHV cluster tool, the optical setup for confocal microscopy, XPS/LEED results showing surface termination, and PL maps illustrating background signal changes upon annealing or exposure.

**Paper structure.** The paper describes the necessity of integrating surface science (XPS/LEED) with quantum measurement (NV spectroscopy) under UHV. It details the custom hardware design (external scanning optics, specialized sample mount) to achieve this integration, followed by presenting results correlating surface cleanliness metrics with improved NV spin coherence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a custom-designed ultrahigh vacuum (UHV) cluster tool developed for studying shallow nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond, enabling in situ diamond surface preparation, characterization, and single NV center dynamics measurements within a single connected platform. The system combines a surface science chamber for controlled surface modification and analysis with a cryogenic confocal microscope chamber dedicated to NV spin and optical measurements. This integrated approach enables a direct correlation between diamond surface chemistry and the resulting NV spin and charge properties. The instrument provides a versatile platform for systematic studies of surface-induced decoherence mechanisms and charge dynamics for shallow NV centers, and establishes a pathway toward reproducible surface engineering for quantum sensing applications.

</details>

### [Ferromagnetic Order of Reduced Magnetic Moments in a Frustrated Sawtooth Chain of the Magnetic Semiconductor ZnYb$_2$S$_4$](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14196v1)

**Summary.** The study investigates $	ext{ZnYb}_2	ext{S}_4$, a material featuring Yb ions arranged in a frustrated sawtooth chain. Through detailed measurements of specific heat and magnetization, the authors demonstrate that magnetic frustration suppresses the expected long-range antiferromagnetic order below $T_m = 1.4 	ext{ K}$. The results suggest the ground state features ferromagnetically aligned but significantly reduced moments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an excellent example of how geometric frustration in a low-dimensional quantum magnet can suppress expected long-range order, leading to novel ground states that are sensitive to external fields.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating how magnetic frustration within a sawtooth chain structure affects the ground state magnetic order of $	ext{Yb}^{3+}$ moments in the semiconductor $	ext{ZnYb}_2	ext{S}_4$.

**Main result.** The system exhibits evidence of magnetic frustration, leading to reduced spontaneous magnetization despite underlying antiferromagnetic correlations; the ground state is characterized by ferromagnetically aligned but suppressed Yb moments.

**Method.** A combination of bulk measurements including specific heat ($C(T)$), isothermal magnetization ($M(B)$), and powder neutron diffraction were employed.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the orthorhombic semiconductor $	ext{ZnYb}_2	ext{S}_4$, where $	ext{Yb}^{3+}$ ions form a sawtooth spin chain along the $b$-axis, modeled by competing nearest- and next-nearest-neighbor exchange interactions.

**Key observables.** Specific heat peak at $T_m = 1.4 	ext{ K}$, magnetic entropy release ($27\%$ of $R\ln2$ at $T_m$), hysteresis in $M(B)$, and absence of superlattice reflections in neutron diffraction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $T_m = 1.4 	ext{ K}$ (transition temperature); $	heta_p < 0$ (indicating AFM interactions); spontaneous magnetization $\approx 0.1 \mu_B/	ext{Yb}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The magnetic behavior is governed by the effective spin-1/2 Kramers doublet ground state derived from Crystal Electric Field theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $C(T)$ peaks, $M(B)$ hysteresis loops, and neutron diffraction patterns confirming the nature of the magnetic ordering below $T_m$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework using a sawtooth chain Hamiltonian and CEF analysis. It then presents experimental results from specific heat and magnetization measurements to identify the transition temperature ($T_m$) and quantify frustration via entropy release. Finally, neutron diffraction confirms the nature of the magnetic order.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a sawtooth spin chain, competing nearest- and next-nearest-neighbor interactions suppress long-range order, yielding novel quantum states such as a spin-dimer singlet, 1/2 magnetization plateau, and spin contraction. Here, we investigate the magnetic properties of the orthorhombic semiconductor ZnYb$_2$S$_4$, in which Yb$^{3+}$ ions with an effective spin-1/2 form a sawtooth chain along the $b$-axis. The specific heat exhibits a sharp peak at ${T}_{\rm m}$ $=$ 1.4 K, at which the magnetic entropy $S_{\rm m}$ reaches only 27% of $R$ln2. This reduced $S_{\rm m}$ at $T_{\rm m}$ indicates the entropy release of the ground state doublet of Yb$^{3+}$ even for $T$ $>$ $T_{\rm m}$. The isothermal magnetization $M(B)$ at 0.28 K exhibits hysteresis for $\left|B\right| \leq 0.2$ T and increases monotonically for $B > 0.2$ T. The spontaneous magnetization is only 0.1 ${\it μ}_{\rm B}$$/$Yb, an order of magnitude smaller than that expected for the ground state doublet of Yb$^{3+}$. Moreover, in powder neutron diffraction measurements, no superlattice reflections due to antiferromagnetic order are observed for $T$ $<$ $T_{\rm m}$. Therefore, in the ground state, the Yb moments are ferromagnetically aligned, but their amplitude is reduced by magnetic frustration in the sawtooth Yb chain.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates mass-imbalanced two-dimensional Bose-Fermi mixtures interacting via tunable attraction. Using a diagrammatic T-matrix approach, the authors calculate key thermodynamic properties and predict that mass imbalance introduces qualitative changes in the boson momentum distribution. These findings offer new experimental signatures for probing strongly correlated quantum gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced theoretical tools (T-matrix formalism) to predict novel observable signatures (finite momentum peaks) in ultracold quantum gas mixtures, which is highly relevant for experimental realization using modern cold atom or semiconductor platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes mass-imbalanced two-dimensional Bose-Fermi (BF) mixtures with tunable boson-fermion attraction at zero temperature.

**Main result.** Mass imbalance acts as a new control parameter that qualitatively modifies the bosonic momentum distribution, potentially allowing for the observation of a peak at finite momentum.

**Method.** The analysis employs a diagrammatic T-matrix approach and utilizes weak-coupling perturbative expansions to calculate thermodynamic quantities.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a 2D homogeneous mixture of spinless bosons and fermions, governed by an effective Hamiltonian including BF attraction and optional BB repulsion. The study extends theoretical treatments from mass-balanced to mass-imbalanced cases.

**Key observables.** Chemical potentials ($\mu_B, \mu_F$), boson momentum distribution function ($n_B(k)$), condensate density, and Tan's contact parameter ($C_{BF}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mass ratio ($\gamma_m = m_B/m_F$), boson concentration parameter ($x = n_B/n_F$), and the effective coupling strength ($g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a dilute, homogeneous mixture at zero temperature ($T=0$) and relies on diagrammatic approximations (T-matrix formalism).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Feynman diagrams for T-matrices and self-energies; specific plots show the dependence of chemical potentials ($\mu_F, \mu_B$) and the bosonic momentum distribution $n_B(k)$ on mass ratio and coupling strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theoretical framework by first establishing the T-matrix formalism in the normal phase, then calculating self-energies for both species, deriving perturbative expansions for chemical potentials ($\mu_F, \mu_B$), and finally analyzing the resulting momentum distributions and condensate fractions as functions of mass imbalance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We analyze a two-dimensional Bose-Fermi mixture at zero temperature in the presence of a tunable Bose-Fermi attraction. We adopt a diagrammatic T-matrix approach and study the behavior of several thermodynamic quantities for the two species as functions of density, mass ratio, and coupling strength. These include the chemical potentials, the boson momentum distribution function, the condensate density, and Tan's contact parameter. We analytically demonstrate that the present T-matrix formalism recovers the correct second-order perturbative expansion of the chemical potentials in the weak-coupling regime, and test it numerically. The near-universal behavior already found in prior work for the mass-balanced case is confirmed for different masses and becomes even more accurate when the boson mass is large. The mass imbalance emerges as an additional control parameter that qualitatively affects the bosonic momentum distribution. In particular, we found that it can be used to allow for the experimental observation of a peculiar peak in the boson momentum distribution at finite momentum.

</details>

**Summary.** The study reports on achieving deep degeneracy in fermionic polar molecular gases in both 3D and quasi-2D traps. By measuring density fluctuations and analyzing collision rates, the authors confirm that the Pauli exclusion principle imposes a fundamental limit on how cold these quantum systems can be cooled. This work is crucial for designing future experiments aiming to explore exotic many-body physics beyond simple thermal equilibrium.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial experimental benchmarks for understanding how quantum statistics constrain evaporative cooling in dipolar systems, which is vital for realizing highly correlated quantum phases like superfluidity or novel magnetic states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates achieving deep degeneracy in quantum gases of polar molecules while quantifying the fundamental limitations imposed by the Pauli exclusion principle during evaporative cooling.

**Main result.** The authors successfully produced degenerate Fermi gases in both 3D and quasi-2D geometries, confirming that Pauli blockade significantly limits the lowest achievable temperatures, especially in lower dimensions.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements of density fluctuations and cross sections with advanced theoretical modeling (Monte Carlo simulations and solving radial Schrödinger equations) to quantify these quantum statistical effects.

**Model / system.** The system is a Fermi gas of fermionic polar molecules ($	ext{KRb}$) trapped in harmonic or quasi-2D optical potentials. Interactions are dominated by engineered repulsive dipole-dipole collisions, allowing for precise control over scattering cross sections.

**Key observables.** Reduced temperature ($T/T_F$), Pauli suppression factor ($\eta$), elastic and inelastic collision cross sections ($\sigma_{el}, \sigma_{in}$), and density fluctuations ($	ext{Var } N/N_{	ext{;}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The primary limiting factors are the Pauli exclusion principle, which restricts scattering rates, and one-body heating from the trap potential.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include using the Local Density Approximation (LDA) for thermometry and assuming adiabaticity during electric field ramping for cooling analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate evaporative cooling trajectories ($T$ vs $N$, $T/T_F$ vs $N$) comparing experimental data to simulations incorporating Pauli suppression. Other figures detail density fluctuation measurements and the temperature dependence of cross sections derived from thermalization rate analyses.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the platform (polar molecules in 3D/2D), then detailing multiple diagnostic techniques (density fluctuations, CDT) to measure degeneracy and interaction rates. The core argument builds by comparing cooling trajectories across dimensions, culminating in a quantitative assessment of Pauli blockade as the ultimate limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum gases of polar molecules have recently emerged as a powerful platform for exploring exotic many-body dynamics and correlated quantum behavior. To achieve the full potential of this platform, the production of deeply degenerate quantum gases of molecules in arbitrary confinement geometries is necessary. Here, we successfully evaporate fermionic KRb molecules in both 3D and quasi-2D geometries to well below their Fermi temperatures utilizing dipolar collisions. As we evaporate deeper into degeneracy in both geometries, we enter the Pauli-blocked regime with polar molecules, which we independently confirm for the first time by measuring the Pauli suppression of elastic collisions. Moreover, the Pauli suppression of collisions contributes to the limitation of our final molecular temperature to about 25% of the Fermi temperature in both geometries, particularly limiting quasi-2D evaporation where the Pauli blockade drastically reduces an otherwise large elastic to inelastic scattering ratio. This work demonstrates the production of degenerate Fermi gases of polar molecules both in a 3D harmonic trap and in mono- and bi-layer 2D configurations. Further, our work explores the fundamental limits on evaporation of molecular Fermi gases set by the Pauli-exclusion principle, which could be overcome in the future by introducing distinguishable scattering partners.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper resolves the quantum pyramids conjecture by rigorously proving the optimal measurement strategy for specific ensembles of pure quantum states. It achieves this by establishing necessary entropy inequalities that certify optimality across different geometric regimes (obtuse and flat). The methodology is highly technical, combining advanced optimization theory with algebraic analysis to determine fundamental information-theoretic bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical tools—such as advanced inequality proofs and structural analysis of optimizers—that are highly relevant to understanding fundamental limits in quantum information theory, particularly those related to measurement capacity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper resolves the quantum pyramids conjecture by determining the globally information-optimal measurement for an ensemble of equiangular equiprobable pure states.

**Main result.** Optimal measurements are confirmed by proving necessary entropy inequalities for both obtuse and flat pyramid configurations, establishing bounds on accessible information $A(E)$.

**Method.** The proof combines techniques from convex optimization, analyzing structural properties of minimizers using Lagrange multipliers, and applying specialized methods like the 'equal variables method' to establish tight $\ell^p$ inequalities.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum information processing via an ensemble $E = \{\pi_j, ho_j\}_{j=1}^m$ of pure states forming a geometric structure (the 'quantum pyramid') defined by the angles between them. The core mathematical object is the accessible information $A(E)$.

**Key observables.** Accessible information $A(E)$; entropy inequalities; $\ell^p$ norms for zero-sum vectors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of states ($m$), and geometric parameters defining the pyramid (acute, obtuse, flat regimes). The exponent $\alpha$ is crucial in the flat regime analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on reducing complex optimization problems to real-variable minimization problems and systematically eliminating candidate minimizers with three or more distinct coordinate values.

**Figures summary.** Not specified (Notes reference tables summarizing pyramid types and associated theorems).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by analyzing the problem in different geometric regimes (acute, obtuse, flat), proving necessary entropy inequalities for each case. Key steps involve rigorous optimization proofs: first eliminating complex families of minimizers, then establishing tight $\ell^p$ bounds via specialized methods.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Standing on the shoulders of giants, we resolve the quantum pyramids conjecture, confirming the globally information-optimal measurement for an ensemble of equiangular equiprobable pure states, as conjectured by Englert and Řeháček (arXiv:0905.0510). We do so by proving the remaining entropy inequalities of Holevo and Utkin (arXiv:2506.06700), which certify optimality for obtuse and flat pyramids. For obtuse pyramids, our key contribution is a rigorous proof that local minimizers of the corresponding entropy inequality cannot have three distinct coordinate values. We show that eliminating this family can be reduced to a neat algebraic reciprocal inequality relating branches of the Lambert $W$ function, which may be of independent interest. For flat pyramids, we prove a tight $\ell^p$ inequality for zero-sum vectors that was recently conjectured, proved analytically in dimension $d=3$, and computationally verified for $d\leq 200$ by Holevo and Utkin (arXiv:2603.24017). We prove this bound for all $d\geq 2$ via a technique in symmetric inequalities known as the equal variables method.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously investigates the bipartite entanglement entropy of Bethe states across various integrable spin chains, including $	ext{XXX}_{1/2}$ and $SL(2,\mathbb{R})$. Using sophisticated numerical optimization and analytical techniques like Schmidt decomposition, the authors map out how entanglement depends on the state's energy and root configuration. The findings establish systematic patterns for extremal entropy values while developing robust mathematical tools to handle complex singular solutions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep insights into the geometric structure of entanglement within highly constrained quantum systems, linking spectral properties (Bethe states) directly to information-theoretic measures like entropy. The comparison between compact and non-compact chains is particularly relevant for understanding boundary effects in condensed matter models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum entanglement, quantified by bipartite entanglement entropy, behaves across different Bethe states in a family of integrable spin chains.

**Main result.** The minimum entanglement entropy often coincides with the ground state for simple models (XXX$_{1/2}$), but this is not guaranteed for higher-spin generalizations. The analysis reveals distinct scaling behaviors for compact versus non-compact chains like $SL(2, \mathbb{R})$.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced analytical and numerical techniques, including Schmidt decomposition, variational optimization over rapidities (both on-shell and off-shell), and regularization procedures to handle singular solutions.

**Model / system.** The research focuses on integrable quantum spin chains: the XXX$_{1/2}$ model, higher-spin generalizations ($	ext{XXX}_s$), and the non-compact $SL(2,\mathbb{R})$ chain. The states analyzed are Bethe eigenstates characterized by rapidities $\{u_j\}$.

**Key observables.** Bipartite entanglement entropy ($S_\ell$), minimum entropy ($S_{\min}$), maximum entropy ($S_{\max}$), and the corresponding sets of Bethe quantum numbers ($\{I_j\}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($L$), subsystem size ($\ell$), magnon number ($M$), and the nature of the chain (compact vs. non-compact).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on developing complex regularization procedures to handle singular rapidities and assumes that specific limits (e.g., $\epsilon 	o 0$) yield physically meaningful product states with zero entropy.

**Figures summary.** Multiple tables systematically map out the dependence of $S_{\min}$ and $S_{\max}$ on subsystem size ($\ell$) for fixed system parameters ($L, M$), detailing specific extremal rapidities found in various spin sectors (Spin 1, Spin 3/2).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the entanglement calculation via Schmidt decomposition to applying optimization algorithms for off-shell states. It then systematically analyzes different models ($	ext{XXX}_{1/2}$, $	ext{XXX}_s$, $SL(2,\mathbb{R})$), culminating in detailed mathematical derivations concerning singular roots and regularization techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the quantum entanglement of Bethe states across a family of integrable spin chains, including the XXX$_{\frac{1}{2}}$ model, its higher-spin generalizations (XXX$_s$), and the non-compact $SL(2,\mathbb{R})$ chain. For on-shell eigenstates, we perform a comprehensive scan of the bipartite entanglement entropy across the entire spectrum of finite chains with periodic boundary conditions, and identify the Bethe solutions that minimize and maximize the entanglement. These extremal solutions follow systematic, spin-dependent patterns in the Bethe quantum numbers. In the XXX$_{\frac{1}{2}}$ spin chain, for the antiferromagnetic chain, the state with minimal entropy always coincides with the lowest-energy state (the ground state) within a given fixed-magnon sector. For the higher-spin XXX$_s$ model, however, the lowest-entropy state is not always identical to the ground state, and can even be the state of highest energy. By contrast, the Bethe roots that maximize entropy exhibit considerably more intricate structure. Our analysis further reveals how special Bethe root configurations, such as singular and strange solutions, affect entanglement, and it uncovers characteristic entanglement features in the non-compact $SL(2,\mathbb{R})$ chain that are absent from compact spin chains. For off-shell Bethe states, we develop an optimization algorithm that extremizes the entanglement entropy over rapidity distributions, enabling us to explore the maximum entanglement achievable by a Bethe state without imposing the Bethe ansatz equations.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper characterizes the minimum number of quantum copies required to simultaneously estimate a sequence of partial-transpose moments for an unknown bipartite quantum state. By developing resource-efficient quantum circuits and proving matching lower bounds, it establishes the fundamental scaling laws for this type of nonlinear functional estimation under active memory constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental resource characterizations for estimating non-local properties (like entanglement via PT moments) of quantum states, which is crucial for designing efficient quantum measurement protocols in quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on developing efficient protocols for simultaneously estimating multiple partial-transpose moments of an unknown bipartite quantum state under strict active-memory constraints.

**Main result.** The authors achieve a total copy complexity scaling of $O(K \log K / \epsilon^2)$ while maintaining an active memory cost independent of the moment order $K$.

**Method.** They employ sequential qubit-reuse quantum circuits to realize the partial-transpose permutation, comparing the achievable upper bound with established minimax lower bounds.

**Model / system.** The system is a bipartite $n$-qubit quantum state $ho_{AB}$. The analysis revolves around estimating Partial-Transpose (PT) moments, which are related to entanglement measures like negativity.

**Key observables.** Partial-transpose moments $\{p_j(ho_{AB})\}_{j=2}^K = 	ext{Tr}[(ho^{T_B})_{AB}^j]$, and the resulting estimate of quantum negativity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $K$ (target moment order), $\epsilon_{	ext{mom}}$ (desired uniform additive error for all moments simultaneously).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an active-memory constraint where memory usage must not scale with $K$. A logarithmic gap exists between the upper and lower bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the topological view of the PT permutation circuit, the sequential qubit-reuse circuit realizing the moments, and comparisons of estimated moments against exact theory using noise mitigation techniques.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by defining the PT moment hierarchy, presents an achievable quantum protocol with complexity $O(K \log K / \epsilon^2)$, and rigorously proves lower bounds ($\Omega(K/\epsilon^2)$) using hard state families to characterize the necessary resource scaling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the simultaneous estimation of partial-transpose moments $p_j(ρ_{AB})=\mathrm{Tr}[(ρ_{AB}^{T_B})^j]$, $j=2,\ldots,K$, of an unknown bipartite $n$-qubit state from independent copies under an explicit active-memory constraint. We give a sequential qubit-reuse realization of the partial-transpose permutation that uses at most $2n+1$ active qubits, independent of $K$, and estimates all moments $p_2,\ldots,p_K$ to uniform additive error $ε$ with total copy complexity $O(K\log K/ε^2)$. We also prove two converse bounds. First, any uniformly accurate simultaneous estimator requires $Ω(K/ε^2)$ copies in the worst case. Second, the same scaling holds on an explicit isospectral two-qubit negative-partial-transpose (NPT) family whose ordinary moments are constant while the partial-transpose moments vary. These results characterize the copy complexity of the partial-transpose moment hierarchy up to a logarithmic factor and extend simultaneous nonlinear-functional estimation from ordinary state powers to partial-transpose spectral data under active quantum memory independent of the target moment order.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops collision models for open quantum systems coupled to finite environments, analyzing the complex dynamics arising from repeated interactions. By introducing controlled environmental resets, the authors map out distinct dynamical regimes—ranging from fully Markovian to highly non-Markovian—using advanced mathematical tools like divisibility witnesses. It provides a detailed understanding of how memory effects are quantified and suppressed in realistic quantum scenarios.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical toolkit to analyze open quantum systems when environmental memory effects are crucial, offering quantitative diagnostics (BLP, divisibility) beyond simple Markovian assumptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates non-Markovian dynamics in open quantum systems where the environment is finite and repeatedly interacts with the system, moving beyond standard Born-Markov approximations.

**Main result.** The authors establish a clear classification of dynamical regimes (e.g., CP-divisible Markovian vs. non-P-divisible non-Markovian) based on environmental reset strength, showing that perfect correlation erasure requires a full environmental reset.

**Method.** The analysis employs collision models and utilizes advanced diagnostics like the Breuer–Laine–Piilo (BLP) measure and Choi-matrix divisibility witnesses to characterize memory effects.

**Model / system.** The model consists of a system qubit interacting repeatedly with a finite environmental qubit, where the environment's state is modulated by an intermediate reservoir-induced CPTP map between collisions.

**Key observables.** System-environment correlations ($\chi_{SE}$), Non-Markovianity (BLP measure), and Divisibility measures (CP/P-divisibility).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless relaxation parameter ($\lambda$ or $\kappa$) controlling the degree of environmental reset, and interaction strengths ($s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis builds upon collision models and assumes specific forms for the reservoir action (e.g., generalized depolarizing/amplitude damping channels) to control the environment's state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase boundaries in parameter space ($\lambda$ vs $s$, or $\kappa$ vs $s$) separating Markovian from non-Markovian regimes, and show the decay of map determinants over time steps.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the collision model framework, contrasts it with standard assumptions, derives explicit forms for the n-step dynamical maps under partial reset, and finally classifies the resulting dynamics into distinct mathematical regimes based on memory retention and CP properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a system qubit repeatedly interacting with the same environmental qubit, with a reservoir acting on the environment between collisions via a completely positive, trace-preserving map. We show that complete suppression of system--environment correlations uniquely requires a full environmental reset, recovering a semi group dynamics with a time-independent Gorini--Kossakowski--Sudarshan--Lindblad generator, whereas a partial reset yields a continuous transition between Markovian and non-Markovian regimes governed by a single dimensionless relaxation parameter. For a resonant excitation-exchange interaction, we obtain exact closed-form expressions for the Bloch-vector dynamics for both a generalized depolarizing channel and a generalized amplitude-damping channel acting as the reservoir-induced map. Using the Breuer--Laine--Piilo measure and a Choi-matrix CP-divisibility witness, we identify three distinct dynamical regimes across the parameter space: CP-divisible Markovian dynamics, CP-indivisible but P-divisible dynamics, and non-P-divisible non-Markovian dynamics. The boundaries between these regimes, and the structural differences between uniform and anisotropic environmental relaxation, are characterized numerically.

</details>

### [Local correlations in long-range dual-unitary kicked Hamiltonian chains](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13857v1)

**Authors:** Vladimir Al. Osipov, Marc Cedric Spyra, Jana Carolina Schumann, Thomas Guhr, Boris Gutkin  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.13857v1>

**Summary.** The paper constructs an extensive family of dual-unitary kicked spin chains featuring long-range interactions. It analytically proves that local correlations are strictly confined to specific light-cone edges defined by the interaction range. This provides deep insights into the spatial and temporal structure of quantum dynamics in highly symmetric, non-local many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for analyzing dynamics in complex quantum many-body systems exhibiting long-range interactions while preserving special symmetries (dual unitarity), which is crucial for understanding transport and correlation spreading in engineered quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how local correlations propagate within long-range dual-unitary kicked Hamiltonian chains.

**Main result.** Local two-point correlations are found to propagate exclusively along specific light-cone edges, $|k| = r|t|$, and these correlations can be derived analytically for a wide family of such models.

**Method.** The analysis employs techniques based on establishing spatio-temporal duality using Hadamard matrices and calculating correlation functions via transfer operators or direct trace manipulations.

**Model / system.** The system is a generalized Kicked Ising Chain (KIC) extended to include long-range interactions ($r>1$) while maintaining the dual-unitary symmetry. The dynamics are governed by time evolution operators $U = \hat{U}_K \hat{U}_I$.

**Key observables.** Local two-point correlation function $C(n, m, t)$, and spectral statistics diagnostics like the ratio of consecutive level spacings ($r$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction range ($r$), coupling parameters ($J, h$), and time/space indices ($t, n, k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on adding fine-tuned pairwise interactions to maintain dual unitarity with long-range couplings; periodic boundary conditions are assumed for some calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the vanishing of correlations outside light cone edges $|k| = r|t|$ and show spectral statistics matching COE predictions for generic parameters, deviating at integrable points.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the construction of long-range dual-unitary models, proves spatio-temporal duality relations, derives the structure of the correlation function $C(t)$ using transfer operators, and analyzes its decay rates and spectral statistics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many-body Floquet models with exact space--time symmetry, such as the kicked Ising spin chain (KIC), provide natural examples of systems with dual-unitary dynamics. The requirement of exact space--time symmetry is, however, highly restrictive, as it permits only nearest-neighbor interactions. Based on a pair of Hadamard matrices, we construct a wide family of dual-unitary kicked spin chains with long-range interactions. We show that local two-point correlations in such models propagate along the light-cone edges \( |n| = r|t| \), where \(r\) is the interaction range, and can be derived analytically for operators with local support. This approach is illustrated using the example of a kicked Ising spin chain with next-to-next-neighbor interactions.

</details>

The dashed line marks the theoretical deci- sion boundary at ˆy(x) = 0.5. Error bars indicate the 1σ statis- tical dispersion of three independent runs with Nshots = 1024 each.</figcaption>

**Summary.** The authors propose and implement a Physics-Informed Variational Quantum Classifier (VQC) to detect topological phase transitions in strongly correlated fermionic systems, such as the BEC-BCS crossover. By encoding the system dynamics via Trotterized time evolution, the VQC achieves linear scaling complexity ($\mathcal{O}(N)$). This approach successfully demonstrates robust phase classification on superconducting quantum hardware despite inherent noise.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum computation with fundamental condensed matter physics problems (BEC-BCS crossover), providing a scalable quantum sensing tool that circumvents the memory wall of classical simulations for many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is characterizing quantum phases, specifically detecting the topological phase transition between the Fermi polaron quasiparticle and the molecular bound state (BEC-BCS crossover) in strongly correlated matter.

**Main result.** A Physics-Informed Variational Quantum Classifier (VQC) was developed that successfully distinguishes these phases on both simulated and noisy superconducting quantum hardware, demonstrating linear scaling complexity.

**Method.** The VQC is constructed using the Trotterized time-evolution of an effective Hamiltonian derived from Ramsey interferometry protocols, ensuring physical interpretability in its parameters.

**Model / system.** The system models ultracold Fermi gases involving an impurity coupled to a bath of fermions. The dynamics are governed by an effective Hamiltonian whose evolution is mapped onto quantum gates for classification.

**Key observables.** Ramsey fringes visibility, the expectation value of Pauli-Z on the ancilla qubit ($\langle Z_0 angle$), and the resulting phase probability $\hat{y}(x)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system is analyzed across BEC and BCS regimes, with key learned parameters being the optimal Trotter step size ($\delta t$) and effective bath-bath interaction ($U_{ff}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the classification can be achieved by measuring the interferometric correlation $\langle Z_0 angle$, and it relies on the VQC structure to enforce physical constraints.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the VQC architecture, the learned quantum phase diagram showing decision boundaries governed by Ramsey fringes for $N=4$ and $N=10$, and comparisons between ideal simulation versus noisy hardware measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical problem (phase detection), details the physics-informed VQC methodology based on Trotterization, presents results validating phase separation on superconducting hardware, and concludes by comparing its linear scaling advantage ($\mathcal{O}(N)$) against classical exponential simulation limits ($\mathcal{O}(2^{2N})$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The characterisation of quantum phases in strongly correlated systems is a crucial milestone for the deployment of quantum sensors. In this work, we present a Physics-Informed Variational Quantum Classifier (VQC) designed to detect the topological phase transition between the Fermi polaron quasiparticle and the molecular bound state. Unlike conventional Machine Learning approaches, our quantum architecture is constructed via the Trotterised time-evolution of an effective Hamiltonian, ensuring that the learnable parameters correspond to interpretable physical quantities. We show that the VQC efficiently discovers the optimal interferometric protocol, specifically the evolution time and effective bath interactions required to maximise the visibility of Ramsey fringes, thereby clearly distinguishing the Bose-Einstein Condensate (BEC) and Bardeen-Cooper-Schrieffer (BCS) regimes. Furthermore, we report the validation of this classifier on the QRed superconducting quantum processor (BSC-CNS). Despite the intrinsic hardware noise and decoherence, the VQC preserves the relative ordering of the topological phases. We demonstrate that the physics-informed architecture achieves a linear gate complexity $\mathcal{O}(N)$, bypassing the exponential memory wall of classical simulation and ensuring scalability to many-body regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper constructs a generalized thermodynamic framework using Rényi and Tsallis entropies to define $q$-affinities for non-equilibrium processes. It successfully extends the second law of thermodynamics to systems exhibiting strong correlations or non-Gibbsian statistics by deriving non-negative entropy production rates. This provides essential theoretical tools for analyzing complex, far-from-equilibrium physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful mathematical tools (generalized affinities and entropies) applicable to open quantum systems where non-Markovian or long-range effects invalidate standard thermodynamic descriptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a generalized thermodynamic framework for non-equilibrium affinities, extending classical concepts to systems with long-range interactions or strong correlations.

**Main result.** It successfully derives $q$-affinities based on Rényi and Tsallis entropies, establishing a generalized second law structure ($\dot{S}_i \ge 0$) for these non-extensive systems.

**Method.** The authors use master equation approaches and thermodynamic identities involving generalized entropies (Rényi/Tsallis) to define $q$-deformed entropy balance laws and affinities.

**Model / system.** The framework applies to general non-equilibrium systems, particularly those undergoing Markov jump processes or described by reaction equilibria, moving beyond the standard Gibbsian assumptions.

**Key observables.** Generalized thermodynamic force ($q$-affinity, $A^q$), Entropy Production Rate ($\dot{S}_i(T)_q$), and generalized free energy potentials ($\Phi_q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The non-extensivity parameter $q$, which governs the deviation from standard Boltzmann/Shannon statistics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework relies on extending classical irreversible thermodynamics using non-extensive generalizations, assuming a Markovian embedding for local stochastic definitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the generalized affinity for Rényi entropy via free energy differences, then tackling Tsallis entropy using master equations to derive the entropy production rate. Finally, it consolidates these findings into a general $q$-deformed entropy balance law.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a thermodynamic framework for non-equilibrium affinities based on generalized entropies. In particular, we extend the classical concept of De Donder by introducing $q$-affinities associated with Rényi and Tsallis entropies. This in turn allows us to generalize thermodynamic driving forces to systems with long-range interactions and/or strong correlations. For Rényi entropy, we build on a thermodynamic interpretation due to Baez, where the entropy is expressed through finite differences of the Helmholtz free energy at two temperatures. This leads to a generalized thermodynamic potential whose derivative with respect to a reaction coordinate defines the Rényi $q$-affinity. The resulting expression admits a representation in terms of exponential work averages, establishing a connection to Jarzynski-type fluctuation relations. For Tsallis entropy, we consider Markov jump processes using a master-equation-based approach. We derive a $q$-deformed entropy balance law and obtain an explicit expression for the Tsallis entropy production rate, proving its non-negativity and thus recovering a generalized second-law structure. This allows to identify a local stochastic $q$-affinity with the generalized thermodynamic force entering the entropy production rate.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes how higher-order fermionic interactions evolve under renormalization group flow near a Fermi surface. It demonstrates that while these couplings are driven toward strong coupling by the BCS mechanism, they maintain a hierarchical suppression relative to the primary pairing interaction. The findings provide insights into the stability of non-Fermi liquid states and superconducting behavior in strongly correlated metals.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on fermionic interactions, the RG flow techniques used—analyzing how irrelevant operators become relevant or suppressed relative to a primary pairing interaction—are highly relevant to understanding quantum criticality and emergent order in strongly correlated systems studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the renormalization-group (RG) flow of higher-dimensional fermionic interactions ($\psi^\dagger\psi)^{2n}$ in the presence of a Fermi surface.

**Main result.** A 'drag mechanism' is shown to drive all higher-order couplings toward strong coupling, yet they remain parametrically suppressed relative to the BCS interaction. In NFL regimes, this enhancement does not destabilize the stable IR fixed point for sufficiently large N.

**Method.** The analysis relies on calculating one-loop beta functions and solving coupled differential equations describing the running of various fermionic couplings under RG flow.

**Model / system.** The models include non-relativistic $2+1$ dimensional fermions interacting via higher-order terms, initially analyzed near a Fermi surface (BCS context) and later extended to quantum critical metallic systems coupled to a boson.

**Key observables.** Running couplings ($	ilde{g}_4(\mu)$, $	ilde{g}_8(\mu)$), stability of fixed points in coupling space $(\alpha, \lambda, \kappa)$, and the relationship between superconducting gap and critical temperature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of fermions ($N$), the chemical potential ($\mu$), and the critical threshold $N_{cr}=8$ are crucial parameters determining the system's stability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses one-loop approximations, relies on specific kinematic corners for the drag effect, and assumes large-$N$ limits in some sections.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate RG flow trajectories in coupling planes (e.g., $(\alpha, \lambda)$) showing how couplings evolve towards stable or unstable fixed points depending on system parameters like N.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing the basic BCS drag effect using 4- and 8-fermion interactions near a Fermi surface to investigating this mechanism in $2+1$ dimensional Non-Fermi Liquid (NFL) models, concluding with stability analysis for superconducting applications.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the renormalization-group flow of higher-dimensional fermionic interactions $(ψ^\dagger ψ)^{2n}$ in the presence of a Fermi surface. We show that the growth of the BCS four-fermion coupling induces a drag mechanism whereby higher-order fermionic couplings are driven to strong coupling in the infrared. We derive the corresponding beta functions and show that the drag effect applies generically to the whole tower of fermionic operators. Remarkably, although all couplings are driven toward the same strong-coupling scale, the renormalization-group flow preserves a hierarchy in which higher-dimensional operators remain parametrically suppressed relative to the BCS interaction. We then investigate this mechanism in a $2+1$-dimensional non-Fermi liquid coupled to a critical boson. While higher-order fermionic interactions are similarly enhanced along the flow, we show that they do not destabilize the IR stable non-Fermi-liquid fixed point, present for sufficiently large $N$. We briefly discuss possible implications for multicomponent superconductors and other strongly correlated metallic systems.

</details>
