**Summary.** This work develops a mean-field theory for myopic self-avoiding fractional Brownian motion, modeling particles repelled by their own density distribution. The theory predicts that the system's long-time dynamics are governed by two distinct regimes depending on the noise exponent $\alpha$. By solving the associated Fokker-Planck equation analytically in multiple dimensions, the authors confirm scaling laws crucial for understanding self-organization processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on statistical mechanics, the use of anomalous diffusion and non-equilibrium dynamics is relevant to understanding complex systems where memory effects or long-range correlations govern particle movement, which has parallels in many AMO contexts like quantum transport or interacting cold atoms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops an analytical mean-field theory for myopic self-avoiding fractional Brownian motion (FBM). This process models particle movement influenced by both anomalous noise and repulsive interactions from the ensemble's time-integrated density.

**Main result.** The theory successfully predicts distinct dynamical regimes: in the interaction-dominated regime, the mean-squared displacement scales as $\langle r^2(t) angle \sim t^{4/(d+2)}$, independent of the noise exponent $\alpha$.

**Method.** The approach combines a Fokker-Planck formalism with scaling theory analysis to derive closed-form polynomial solutions for the probability density in various dimensions.

**Model / system.** The model is myopic self-avoiding FBM, where particles are driven by fractional Gaussian noise and experience a mean-field repulsion proportional to the gradient of the time-integrated ensemble density. This framework is applied across 1D, 2D, and 3D.

**Key observables.** Mean-squared displacement ($\langle r^2(t) angle$), instantaneous probability density $P(r, t)$, and time-integrated density $P_{	ext{int}}(r, t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The anomalous diffusion exponent $\alpha$, the critical exponent $\alpha_c = 4/(d+2)$, and spatial dimension $d$ are crucial parameters defining the system's behavior.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is working within the mean-field limit ($N 	o \infty$), which neglects ensemble fluctuations. The analysis also assumes rotational symmetry in space.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare analytical solutions (solid lines) against numerical simulations (symbols) across different dimensions and exponents, confirming predicted scaling laws like parabolic profiles in 1D or annular structures in 2D.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical model and its known scaling theory ($\alpha_c$), then develops the mean-field Fokker-Planck approach. It proceeds to derive analytical solutions for specific dimensions (1D, 2D, 3D) by solving nonlinear ODEs derived from consistency relations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Myopic self-avoiding fractional Brownian motion (FBM) is a stochastic process in which an ensemble of particles is driven by fractional Gaussian noise while being repelled by the gradient of the time-integrated ensemble density [J. House, R. Bakhshizada, S. Janušonis, R. Metzler, and T. Vojta, Phys. Rev. E 112, 034119 (2025)]. Depending on the anomalous diffusion exponent $α$ characterizing the noise, the process features two dynamical regimes: an interaction-dominated regime ($α< α_c=4/(d+2)$) where the mean-density interaction governs long-time dynamics, and a noise-dominated regime ($α> α_c$) where FBM correlations prevail. In the interaction-dominated regime, the mean-squared displacement grows as $\langle r^2(t) \rangle \sim t^{4/(d+2)}$ regardless of $α$, while for $α> α_c$ the standard FBM scaling $\langle r^2(t) \rangle \sim t^α$ is recovered. Here, we develop an analytical mean-field theory of myopic self-avoiding FBM, based on a Fokker-Planck approach to the interaction-dominated regime. This allows us to derive closed-form polynomial solutions for the probability density. To compare with computer simulations, we develop an efficient radial binning algorithm that significantly reduces the computational complexity, making large-scale three-dimensional simulations feasible. Extensive simulations in one, two, and three dimensions confirm the analytical predictions. We also discuss the application of the process to the self-organization of serotonergic axons (fibers) in vertebrate brains, where FBM paths with self-avoidance provide a natural framework for understanding spatial heterogeneities of fiber densities.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces 'pseudo entropy,' a complex generalization of entanglement entropy used to track the directional flow of information during real-time quantum evolution. The key finding is that this pseudo entropy's short-time change decomposes into two parts: an imaginary component driven by modular covariance and a real component driven by commutators. This framework extends naturally to non-Hermitian physics, providing a powerful diagnostic for time-asymmetric quantum processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel, amplitude-level diagnostic tool for quantum dynamics that intrinsically carries an arrow of time, offering insights beyond standard entropy production theorems relevant to open and non-equilibrium quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To derive a microscopic, time-oriented entropic quantity (pseudo entropy) that captures directional information about quantum evolution before standard thermodynamic irreversibility is established.

**Main result.** The short-time change in real-time pseudo entropy splits into an imaginary part governed by the modular covariance of the Hamiltonian and initial state, and a real part governed by the commutator of these operators.

**Method.** The analysis relies on calculating the time derivative of the pseudo entropy using the reduced transition matrix ($	au$) and decomposing this variation into covariance and commutator terms.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to general quantum systems undergoing real-time unitary evolution (quenches), with specific illustrations in two-qubit models, transverse-field Ising chains, and non-Hermitian PT-symmetric toy models.

**Key observables.** Real-time pseudo entropy ($S_A(t, 0)$), modular covariance response ($\propto \langle \{K_A, H\} angle$), and the commutator expectation value ($\langle [K_A, H] angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time $t$ (especially short-time expansion), temperature/inverse temperature $eta$, and system size $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a definition of pseudo entropy via the reduced transition matrix and focuses on the leading order ($O(t^2)$) time dependence.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the real ($	ext{Re } S_A(t)$) and imaginary ($	ext{Im } S_A(t)$) components of the pseudo entropy after an Ising-chain quench, showing the linear imaginary response fixed by modular covariance. A finite-size study shows the modular susceptibility peaking near criticality.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first defining the complex pseudo entropy and deriving its short-time expansion. It then rigorously separates the time evolution into distinct contributions (covariance/commutator). The methodology is extended to non-Hermitian systems, culminating in discussions relating this oriented response to holographic principles.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pseudo entropy is a complex-valued generalization of entanglement entropy defined from a reduced transition matrix. We study the pseudo entropy associated with a real-time transition matrix between an initial pure state and its unitary time evolution. For a subsystem $A$, we show that the short-time behavior of real-time pseudo entropy is governed by the correlation between the physical Hamiltonian $H$ and the modular Hamiltonian $K_A=-\logρ_A$ of the initial reduced state, $ S_A(t,0)=S_A(0)-it \langle K_A(H-\langle H\rangle)\rangle + \mathcal{O}(t^2)$. For Hermitian dynamics, the initial imaginary response is controlled by the symmetrized covariance of $H$ and $K_A$ with an overall minus sign, while the initial real response is governed by their commutator. Thus the imaginary part of real-time pseudo entropy is not merely a branch artifact: it is a time-oriented modular response generated by the correlation between microscopic time evolution and subsystem coarse graining. We clarify the relation of this result to the known first law of pseudo entropy, derive an all-order expression in a Schmidt-diagonal model, recover thermal pseudo entropy as a special case, illustrate the covariance/commutator decomposition in a two-qubit model, and confirm the covariance response in transverse-field Ising-chain quenches, including a finite-size study of a modular susceptibility near the Ising critical region. We discuss how this amplitude-level oriented response can be related to ordinary entropy production, and also give a concrete $\mathcal{PT}$-symmetric toy-model illustration of the non-Hermitian extension.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses an Agent-Based Model to explore how wealth inequality impedes the transition to a sustainable 'Green' economy. It finds that high levels of inequality can lock economies into polluting regimes, even when environmental risks are known. The results highlight that no single policy is universally optimal; effective intervention requires tailoring mechanisms (taxation vs. incentives) to the specific level of existing economic disparity.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly quantum mechanical, the use of complex non-equilibrium dynamics, phase transitions (Brown to Green regime), and agent heterogeneity mirrors concepts found in statistical physics models of critical phenomena or pattern formation far from equilibrium.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether wealth inequality acts as a structural obstacle preventing an economy from undergoing an ecological transition toward sustainability, rather than merely being a consequence of environmental damage.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that beyond certain thresholds of wealth inequality, the economy can become locked in a high-emission 'Brown' regime, regardless of agent sensitivity to climate externalities. The effectiveness of policy interventions depends critically on the existing level of inequality and the regressivity embedded in the fiscal mechanism.

**Method.** The authors employ a stylized Agent-Based Model (ABM) simulating heterogeneous agents allocating income between environmentally damaging ('Brown') and sustainable ('Green') sectors based on utility maximization.

**Model / system.** The model features agents with initial wealth following a Pareto distribution, making decentralized investment decisions influenced by short-term returns versus long-term systemic environmental risks. The dynamics are governed by equations describing income rates and agent utility differences related to externalities.

**Key observables.** Gini coefficient, Time to Transition ($T_{2T}$), cumulative environmental destruction ($\langle\omega_{	ext{loss}}(t)angle$), aggregate Green/Brown wealth shares, and policy performance metrics (e.g., reduction in $T_{2T}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Initial wealth heterogeneity ($	ext{Gini}_0$), externality awareness weight ($\lambda$), time scales for tech inertia ($	au$) and external impact decay ($	heta$), and parameters defining fiscal mechanisms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Agents are assumed to be rational utility maximizers; the model is stylized, representing an exploratory framework rather than a precise forecast of reality. A key assumption is that wealthier agents discount long-term environmental risks more heavily.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing transition possibility thresholds based on inequality, and radar plots comparing the multidimensional performance trade-offs (e.g., speed vs. cost) across various policy combinations.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops an ABM framework to model wealth allocation between two sectors; it establishes that high initial inequality can trap the system in a poor state; subsequently, it compares multiple fiscal policies (BI, carbon tax, incentives) by analyzing their impact on transition speed and environmental outcomes across different inequality regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

At the intersection of rising wealth inequality and intensifying environmental pressures, we investigate a reverse causal relationship that has received comparatively little attention: wealth inequality may not only be a consequence of environmental crises, but also act as a structural obstacle to the ecological transition itself. We develop a stylized agent-based model in which heterogeneous agents, whose initial wealth follows a Pareto distribution, allocate their income between either a Brown or a Green sector through a utility function. The function is designed to capture the trade-off between short-term returns and exposure to long-term systemic risks. A central ingredient is that wealthier agents perceive themselves as less vulnerable to environmental shocks, thereby reducing the amount of resources available for the transition. We show that, beyond inequality thresholds compatible with those observed in most developed countries, the economy remains locked in a Brown regime, even when a substantial share of agents is sensitive to externalities. We then assess a set of stylized fiscal policies (basic income, carbon taxation, Green incentives, and a combined scheme) and find that their effectiveness depends strongly on the inequality regime and on the regressivity embedded in the fiscal mechanism, revealing multidimensional trade-offs between transition speed, cumulative environmental destruction, growth, and fiscal pressure.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper calculates the rate at which a quantum superposition decays due to coupling with a scalar field near black holes. By analyzing different vacuum states—Boulware, Unruh, and Hartle-Hawking—the authors show that only thermal effects associated with Hawking radiation induce measurable decoherence. This demonstrates the profound dependence of quantum coherence on the chosen description of the spacetime environment.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete calculation of decoherence in curved spacetime environments, linking fundamental concepts like Hawking radiation and quantum field theory in curved spacetime directly to open quantum system dynamics (decoherence).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how quantum spatial superpositions of a particle decohere when coupled to a quantum scalar field in the curved spacetime backgrounds of static black holes (Schwarzschild and Reissner-Nordström).

**Main result.** Decoherence is found to vanish in the Boulware vacuum but is induced by thermal effects associated with Hawking radiation in Unruh and Hartle-Hawking vacua, yielding a non-zero rate even for extremal black holes.

**Method.** The decoherence functional is derived locally using coherent state generation techniques, relating the rate to the low-frequency behavior of the Wightman function calculated via asymptotic matching methods.

**Model / system.** The system models a quantum superposition of a static particle coupled to a real scalar field ($\Phi$) in curved spacetime. The analysis uses the interaction Hamiltonian derived from the coupling $S_{int} = \lambda \int d	au \Phi(x(	au))$.

**Key observables.** Decoherence functional, entanglement particle number $\langle N angle$, and the decoherence rate ($\Gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Black hole mass ($M$), charge ($Q$), coupling constant ($\lambda$), separation distance ($d$), and vacuum choice (Boulware, Unruh, Hartle-Hawking).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as treating the interaction profile as rectangular and using asymptotic matching methods for mode functions in different regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the DSW gedankenexperiment, develops the coherent state formalism and local rate expression, applies it to Schwarzschild black holes, extends the analysis to Reissner-Nordström black holes, and concludes by summarizing the vacuum dependence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the decoherence of a quantum spatial superposition of a static particle in Schwarzschild and Reissner-Nordström black holes. By treating the particle as a localized classical source coupled to a quantum scalar field, we reformulate the decoherence process in the Danielson-Satishchandran-Wald (DSW) gedankenexperiment through coherent state generation and derive the local expression for the decoherence functional in terms of the Wightman function. In the long-time limit, the decoherence rate is shown to be characterized by the low-frequency behavior of the Wightman function. We then employ the asymptotic matching method to calculate the analytical expressions of the Wightman functions in the Boulware, Unruh, and Hartle-Hawking vacua. We show that the decoherence behavior depends on the quantum state of the environmental field. While the Boulware vacuum gives vanishing decoherence for a static superposition, the thermal effects associated with Hawking radiation in the Unruh and Hartle-Hawking vacua can induce nonvanishing decoherence.

</details>

**Summary.** This work numerically investigates how rod-shaped particles move through a fixed bed of spheres, revealing two key behaviors: getting permanently stuck (trapping) or moving continuously (passing). The results show that particle shape anisotropy introduces strong dynamical constraints, causing longer rods to slow down and become more easily trapped than shorter ones.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on granular physics, the study models complex non-equilibrium transport phenomena in disordered media. The identification of distinct dynamic regimes (trapping vs. passing) governed by geometric thresholds has parallels in understanding energy dissipation or flow through porous quantum structures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the percolation behavior of rod-like particles moving through a static, disordered bed of larger spheres under gravity.

**Main result.** Two distinct regimes—trapping and continuous passing—are identified, with percolation velocity depending strongly on particle aspect ratio and pore geometry. Longer rods are found to be more susceptible to geometric trapping than short rods.

**Method.** The research relies heavily on Discrete Element Method (DEM) simulations to model the mechanical interactions between the rod-like particles and the static sphere bed.

**Model / system.** The system consists of frictionless, non-interacting rod-like particles percolating through a disordered packing ($\phi=0.60$) of larger spheres. The rods are modeled as collinear small spheres, and dynamics are governed by linear spring-dashpot contact forces.

**Key observables.** Trapping probability ($N_{	ext{trap}}/N$), characteristic penetration length ($\lambda$), percolation velocity ($	ilde{v}_p$), mean contact number ($\langle Z angle$), and rod orientation distributions $P(	heta)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rod aspect ratio ($A$ or $L^*$), size ratio ($R = d_L/d_S$), gravitational acceleration ($g$), and the geometrical trapping threshold ($R_t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Frictionless contacts are assumed for the primary analysis, and rod-rod interactions are disabled in some simulations. The system is treated as quasi-static or governed by constant mean velocity in the passing regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the initial packed bed structure ($g(r)$), the exponential decay of trapping probability vs. depth, phase diagrams mapping trapping/passing regimes based on particle geometry, and orientation distributions for trapped versus percolating rods.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the transition between trapping and passing states by varying rod length and size ratio, quantifying this transition using scaling laws derived from simulation data, and analyzing local contact geometries to explain the observed dynamical constraints.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We numerically investigate percolation of independent frictionless glued-sphere rod-like particles under gravity through a disordered static bed of larger spheres. We identify two distinct regimes: a \emph{trapping} regime, where rods stop after percolating a limited distance in the bed and a \emph{passing} regime, where rods percolate continuously with constant mean velocity. The transition between these regimes is governed by the length of the rod and the geometrical trapping threshold for spherical particles based on the rod diameter and the minimum pore throat diameter defined by three touching large spheres. The percolation velocity for all rod geometries, including the single sphere limit, collapses onto a single curve when scaled with the gravitational acceleration and the bed sphere diameter. The results also demonstrate that short rods percolate nearly twice as fast as long rods due to the geometric constraints associated with the disordered pore structure of the static bed. Consequently, long rods are more susceptible to trapping via specific contact configurations with the bed spheres, which differ from those for short rods. These results reveal how shape anisotropy introduces dynamical constraints and thresholds in granular percolation, with implications for predicting segregation in mixtures of non-spherical particles.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced theoretical frameworks using Optimal Transport and functional inequalities to quantify how fast a system relaxes to equilibrium. By relating free energy dissipation to geometric distances like the Wasserstein metric, it provides powerful, rigorous bounds on relaxation rates in stochastic physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, geometric tools (Optimal Transport) to analyze non-equilibrium dynamics in classical statistical mechanics systems, which is foundational knowledge for understanding thermalization processes relevant to open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to quantify the dissipation during the relaxation of conservative stochastic systems towards their steady state by establishing geometric lower bounds using functional inequalities.

**Main result.** It develops and compares several thermodynamic bounds, including the Otto-Villani inequality (HWI) and the Logarithmic Sobolev Inequality (LSI), providing a geometric measure for the instantaneous speed of relaxation.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on Optimal Transport theory (Wasserstein distance), functional inequalities connecting relative entropy to transport metrics, and deriving differential bounds via calculus identities.

**Model / system.** The primary model is an overdamped Brownian particle governed by a Langevin equation, leading to the Fokker-Planck evolution for the probability density. Examples include relaxation in Landau-Ginzburg potentials or harmonic traps.

**Key observables.** Relative Entropy ($D[p\|p^*]$), Wasserstein Distance ($W(p, p^*)$), and the rate of change of relative entropy ($rac{d}{dt} D[p\|p^*]$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), potential curvature/convexity ($\kappa$), and time ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes conditions on convexity (e.g., $\kappa > 0$ for LSI) or restricts the dynamics to regimes where relative entropy decreases along geodesics.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare different flow types (Gradient Descent, Physical Flow, OT) for potentials like the Mexican hat potential and illustrate relaxation in harmonic traps, showing when various bounds saturate or fail.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the stochastic dynamics and free energy to introducing optimal transport concepts. It then derives and compares multiple functional inequalities—the Otto-Villani bound, HWI, and LSI—analyzing their mathematical derivations and physical implications for relaxation speed.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The dissipation in the relaxation of an ensemble of conservative stochastic systems towards the steady state is quantified by the free energy difference. Functional inequalities within the framework of [F. Otto and C. Villani, J. Funct. Anal. 173, 361 (2000)] are here revisited which connect the free energy dynamics and optimal transport, offering a geometric perspective on the instantaneous speed of relaxation in the presence of potential barriers. These are illustrated with numerical relaxation experiments on Landau-Ginzburg potentials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive theoretical and numerical framework to calculate how directional persistence affects the search efficiency of cells moving in an annular space. By modeling the random walk using correlated processes, the authors derive analytical corrections to the mean first-passage time, confirming that persistent motion significantly accelerates target finding compared to purely diffusive movement.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on biological transport, the mathematical framework—solving time-dependent partial differential equations (PDEs) with complex boundary conditions and incorporating memory effects via kernels—is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics problems in confined geometries.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study quantifies the mean first-passage time (MFPT) for a random walker to reach an absorbing target within a confined two-dimensional annular domain.

**Main result.** Directional persistence ($\kappa$) significantly reduces the search time compared to isotropic motion, with this benefit saturating at high persistence values. The MFPT can be accurately determined by combining diffusion-limit approximations with boundary layer corrections.

**Method.** The analysis employs a combination of analytical techniques (Fourier mode reductions, perturbation expansions) and advanced numerical solvers (semi-Lagrangian methods, event-driven Monte Carlo simulations).

**Model / system.** The model is a velocity-jump process describing cell migration in an annulus ($a < r < R$). It incorporates correlated random walks (CRW) via von Mises turning kernels to simulate directional persistence and can be extended to biased motion.

**Key observables.** Mean First Passage Time ($ar{T}$ or $T_{MFPT}$), relative gap between numerical solvers, effective diffusivity ($D_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Persistence parameter ($\kappa$), annulus radii ($a$ and $R$), turning rate ($\mu$), bias strength ($d	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a 2D annular geometry, constant speed motion, and relies on approximations like the fast-turning limit for analytical tractability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of the domain, mesh grading strategies (power-law graded meshes), and comparisons between direct solver results and Monte Carlo simulations showing convergence diagnostics.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem and modeling various random walk types to deriving analytical approximations in limits ($\kappa 	o 0$ or $\kappa 	o \infty$), followed by rigorous numerical implementation using advanced solvers and extensive cross-validation against MC simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the mean first-passage time of a random walker to a small absorbing target at the center of a two-dimensional annulus with a specularly reflecting outer boundary. The problem is motivated by natural killer cell migration toward a target cancer cell, where the goal is to quantify how long it takes immune cells to reach the target and how search efficiency depends on directional persistence and chemotactic bias. Cell motion is modeled as a velocity-jump process. We first consider a correlated random walk with a von Mises turning kernel, with a concentration parameter controlling directional persistence. We then extend the model to a biased correlated random walk using a phase-shifted turning kernel that represents preferential motion, for example following a concentration gradient. Our analysis combines closed-form benchmarks for simple and biased random walks, Fourier-mode reductions of the transport equations for the correlated and biased correlated models, and a fast-turning perturbation expansion that gives an analytical correction to the diffusion-limit mean first-passage time for the random walker. Our analytical results are supported by numerical methods that include a semi-Lagrangian solver in radial and angular coordinates, a stationary discretisation designed to handle biased transport, and an event-driven Monte Carlo simulator for cross-validation. Together, our results provide a framework relating persistent and biased immune-cell motion to target-search times in confined two-dimensional domains.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Quantum-Classical Hierarchical Equations of Motion (QC-HEOM), a novel technique for simulating open quantum systems. It successfully merges the strengths of path integral and hierarchical equations by separating thermal effects from residual quantum memory. This results in an extremely efficient method that accurately models complex, anharmonic dynamics at low temperatures where conventional methods struggle.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a computationally tractable framework (QC-HEOM) to study complex open quantum dynamics in regimes (low temperature, anharmonicity) where standard HEOM methods fail or become prohibitively expensive due to the exponential growth of required auxiliary objects.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is simulating the time-evolution of open quantum systems, specifically addressing non-Markovian dynamics and accurately capturing residual quantum memory effects.

**Main result.** The proposed QC-HEOM method achieves superior efficiency compared to conventional HEOM by separating thermal fluctuations from quantum memory, especially at low temperatures. It reproduces benchmark results for complex anharmonic systems with significantly fewer auxiliary objects.

**Method.** It combines the ensemble-averaged classical path reference from the Quantum-Classical Path Integral formalism with a hierarchical treatment of quantum influence functionals (QC-HEOM).

**Model / system.** The method is applied to general open quantum systems, exemplified by the asymmetric spin-boson model and the seven-site Fenna–Matthews–Olson (FMO) complex. The Hamiltonian involves system coupling to an environment bath.

**Key observables.** Population dynamics $P(t)$ over time; convergence quantified by time-averaged root-mean-square (RMS) population error ($\Delta_j$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T=300 	ext{ K}, 77 	ext{ K}, 10 	ext{ K}$); Hierarchy depth ($L$); Bath spectral density parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The formalism uses the Wigner approximation when extending to anharmonic environments, and it assumes that the bath correlation function can be represented by a sum of exponentials for the HEOM structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare population dynamics $P(t)$ across different temperatures ($300 	ext{ K}, 77 	ext{ K}, 10 	ext{ K}$), showing QC-HEOM tracking benchmark results with much shallower hierarchy depths than standard HEOM.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of existing methods (QCPI, conventional HEOM) and then develops the QC-HEOM formalism. It rigorously demonstrates its equivalence to established methods for harmonic baths before applying it successfully to anharmonic systems like FMO across varying temperatures, concluding with efficiency comparisons.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a quantum-classical hierarchical equations of motion (QC-HEOM) approach for simulating non-Markovian open quantum systems. The method combines the ensemble-averaged classical path reference of the quantum-classical path integral formalism with a hierarchy of auxiliary quantum influence functionals. By incorporating thermal fluctuations through an ensemble average over reference trajectories, the hierarchy is required to represent only the residual quantum memory associated with the imaginary part of the bath response function. Consequently, unlike conventional hierarchical equations of motion, QC-HEOM does not require Matsubara or Padé expansions of the thermal kernel and exhibits only weak temperature dependence of the hierarchy size. Furthermore, because thermal fluctuations are supplied through reference classical trajectories, the framework naturally extends beyond harmonic baths and enables the incorporation of anharmonic and molecular environments through externally generated trajectories. We derive the formalism and demonstrate its exactness for a harmonic bath. Applications to an asymmetric spin-boson model and the seven-site Fenna--Matthews--Olson complex illustrate the accuracy of QC-HEOM. It reproduces benchmark quasi-adiabatic path integral and hierarchical equations of motion results while requiring substantially fewer auxiliary objects, particularly at low temperatures. These results establish QC-HEOM as an efficient framework for treating residual quantum memory in quantum-classical descriptions of open-system dynamics. The separation of thermal fluctuations from residual quantum memory through the use of Wigner trajectories provides an approximate route toward hierarchical treatments of complex anharmonic environments that are inaccessible to conventional HEOM approaches.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates the ability to engineer and control spin-orbit coupling in semiconductor quantum dots by precisely tailoring their confining electric potentials at the atomic scale. By combining STM/STS measurements with advanced k.p theory, the authors successfully model and verify that SOC dictates measurable energy splittings and magnetic responses, paving the way for next-generation spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete example of how fundamental solid-state physics concepts (SOC) can be experimentally manipulated using nanoscale electrostatic engineering, offering pathways toward realizing controllable spin qubits in semiconductor platforms relevant to quantum information science.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is achieving precise, controllable tuning of spin-orbit coupling (SOC) within atomically defined semiconductor quantum dots (QDs). This control moves beyond simple approximations by tailoring the local electrostatic confinement potential.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that SOC-induced energy splittings and magnetic responses in QDs can be accurately predicted and controlled by designing the geometry of the confining electric fields, matching experimental observations across isotropic and anisotropic potentials.

**Method.** The research combines advanced Scanning Tunneling Microscopy/Spectroscopy (STM/STS) measurements with theoretical modeling based on an effective Hamiltonian derived from a multiband k.p model.

**Model / system.** The physical system is electron confinement in InSb surface quantum dots, engineered by patterning individual Cs ions to create designer electrostatic potentials. The theory uses an effective Hamiltonian incorporating kinetic energy, parabolic/triangular confinement potentials, Zeeman terms, and SOC terms beyond the standard Rashba description.

**Key observables.** Zero-field splitting (ZFS) of electronic states, magnetic field dependence of energy levels ($	ext{d}I/	ext{dV}$ spectra), and spatial decay profiles of the ground state wavefunction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Confinement strengths ($\kappa_x, \kappa_y$), SOC coupling constants ($\lambda_{	ext{SOC}}$), effective electron mass ($m^*$), and magnetic field strength ($B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model relies on approximating the confinement potential as a sum of triangular and parabolic terms, and treating SOC perturbatively to an effective Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate STM images and $	ext{d}I/	ext{dV}$ maps for QDs with varying geometries (circular vs. anisotropic), showing characteristic energy splittings and wavefunction spatial decay patterns under applied magnetic fields.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the experimental platform (Cs patterning on InSb) and measurement techniques (STM/STS). It then develops a generalized theoretical Hamiltonian incorporating SOC, solving it for both isotropic and anisotropic confinement regimes to predict measurable energy splittings and spatial features.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tuning spin-orbit coupling is essential in controlling both spin and charge in confined semiconductor nanostructures, yet it is rarely a truly controllable parameter. Here, we show control over the spin-orbit Hamiltonian in quantum dots and the resulting quantum states by tailoring the confinement potential with atomic-scale precision. Using scanning tunnelling microscopy and spectroscopy, we pattern individual Cs ions into designer quantum dot structures on the surface of indium antimonide, in which electrons from a two-dimensional electron gas are confined with chosen in-plane electric-field gradients. We then quantify the atomic level structure, both spatially resolving the orbital character of the electronic states and their magnetic-field evolution. We demonstrate that the level structure, including the induced zero-field splitting, can be tailored by the designed geometry of the local electric fields. These effects can be described using a Hamiltonian that allows consistent treatment of the confinement-induced spin-orbit coupling beyond the conventional Bychkov-Rashba description. This Hamiltonian is derived from a multiband k.p model and takes the energy dependence of the relevant physical parameters into account. Such precise control of spin-orbit coupling in semiconductor quantum dots is relevant to quantum and spintronic technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops stochastic thermodynamics for systems moving on curved spaces whose metrics change with time. It introduces a crucial correction term ($\dot{G}_t$) into the energy balance equations to ensure that entropy production always obeys the Second Law, even when geometry changes. This framework is vital for quantifying thermodynamic costs in dynamic physical settings like diffusion on evolving membranes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful tools for analyzing non-equilibrium processes where the environment or geometry itself is changing over time, which is relevant for understanding open quantum systems coupled to dynamic baths or evolving media.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is constructing a thermodynamically consistent framework for stochastic thermodynamics when the underlying physical space has a time-dependent metric.

**Main result.** The authors derive necessary correction terms ($\dot{G}_t$) to the standard heat and work definitions, ensuring that the total entropy production rate rigorously satisfies the Second Law of Thermodynamics even as the geometry evolves.

**Method.** The methodology involves analyzing energy balance using overdamped Langevin dynamics on curved manifolds, employing relative entropy calculations between forward and backward processes in the $\epsilon 	o 0$ limit.

**Model / system.** The system is an overdamped Langevin process evolving on a nonrelativistic curved space described by a time-dependent metric $g_t$. The analysis uses Fokker-Planck equations and concepts from geometric measure theory.

**Key observables.** Entropy production rate ($\dot{\Sigma}_t$), Heat rate ($\dot{Q}_t$), Work rate ($\dot{W}_t$), and the metric correction term ($\dot{G}_t$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time dependence of the metric ($g_t$) is crucial, as are the overdamped regime assumption and the small noise limit ($\epsilon 	o 0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes the topology remains constant (diffeomorphic) and that the heat bath thermalizes much faster than the manifold evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of $\dot{Q}_t$, $\dot{W}_t$, and $\dot{G}_t$ for a sphere example, comparing correct non-negative entropy production against naive calculations that violate the Second Law.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the dynamics on curved spaces to establishing detailed balance conditions via relative entropy measures. It then derives the full thermodynamic decomposition of energy change, culminating in showing the dynamics are a gradient flow of the relative entropy functional under a Wasserstein metric.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct stochastic thermodynamics of overdamped Langevin systems on nonrelaticvistic curved spaces with time-dependent metrics. The time dependence of the metric contributes to the energy balance by performing work on the kinetic energy, which is instantaneously dissipated as heat in the overdamped regime. This contribution makes our framework thermodynamically consistent so that entropy production satisfies the second law of thermodynamics. As a special case, when the metric evolves according to backward Ricci flow, the entropy balance exhibits a structure similar to Perelman's entropy functional. Our framework provides a way to quantify thermodynamic costs in dynamics on time-evolving spaces such as diffusion on membranes.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper details the development and application of trap-quenched collimation for Bose-Einstein condensates to enhance atom interferometry tests of the Universality of Free Fall. By combining novel experimental techniques with sophisticated modeling, the authors predict that dual-species BECs can achieve the necessary low expansion energies required for unprecedented precision in space-based fundamental physics measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a cutting edge intersection of quantum sensing and advanced atomic physics, demonstrating how precise control over matter-wave expansion in space can push fundamental measurement limits beyond terrestrial capabilities.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve tests of the Universality of Free Fall (UFF) using Bose-Einstein condensates (BECs), which requires exquisite control over expansion energies and center-of-mass dynamics for dual-species systems.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a trap-quenched collimation technique on single-species BECs in space, theoretically predicting simultaneous collimation for a ${}^{41}	ext{K}–{}^{87}	ext{Rb}$ mixture that meets the energy requirements for $10^{-15}$ accuracy UFF tests.

**Method.** The research combines experimental implementation of trap-quenched collimation with detailed theoretical modeling, using effective models and *ab initio* simulations to track BEC size dynamics during trapping and free expansion.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a BEC trapped in an atom chip setup, tested aboard the International Space Station. The model involves simulating time-dependent magnetic field interactions via gauged atom chip potentials and using effective models for post-release ballistic/semi-free expansion.

**Key observables.** 2D and 3D expansion energies ($E_{exp}$), Center-of-Mass (CoM) oscillation dynamics, and the Thomas-Fermi radii of the BEC during Time-of-Flight (ToF).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Expansion energy levels ($\sim k_B \cdot 78 	ext{ pK}$ for single species; $\sim 43-48 	ext{ pK}$ for dual species), interrogation time up to $700 	ext{ ms}$, and target accuracy of $10^{-15}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fitting complex dynamics using effective models, which requires approximations like assuming no inter-species interaction initially and simplifying the magnetic field gradients during ToF.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the trap-quenched collimation scheme (Fig. 1), CoM oscillation control (Fig. 2a/b), and comparisons between simulated vs. measured expansion energies in various reference frames (Fig. 3, Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from introducing the UFF test requirement to detailing the trap-quenched collimation technique, presenting single-species measurements, developing sophisticated models for size dynamics, and finally extending the theory to predict optimal dual-species performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dual-species atom interferometry in space promises precise tests of the Universality of Free Fall (UFF), with a sensitivity that grows quadratically with the extended interrogation time accessible in weightlessness. These tests demand exquisite control over the expansion energies of both condensed sources as well as over their differential center-of-mass dynamics. We propose a trap-quenched collimation technique featuring in-trap excitations of collective modes compatible with state-of-the-art atom-chip setups. Using NASA's Cold Atom Laboratory aboard the International Space Station, we demonstrate it on a single-species $^{87}$Rb condensate. By controlling the center-of-mass release dynamics we observe free expansion times up to 700 ms and measure a two-dimensional expansion energy of $k_B \cdot 78\pm 9 \;\mathrm{pK}$ in the imaging plane. A detailed model of the magnetically-induced dynamics indicates that this corresponds to a two-dimensional expansion energy of about $k_B \cdot 15^{+12}_{-5}\; \mathrm{pK}$ along two of the condensate's eigenaxes. Finally, we theoretically study this trap-quenched collimation scheme for a $^{41}$K-$^{87}$Rb mixture, predicting a simultaneous collimation that meets the expansion energy requirements for a state-of-the-art UFF test at the $10^{-15}$ accuracy level.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically explores the complex magnetic ground states of frustrated spin systems using advanced ML-enhanced VMC methods. It demonstrates that increasing frustration melts the altermagnetic order, leading to an exotic state featuring coexisting bond-nematic and SPT valence bond solid orders. This advances understanding of quantum phases near magnetically ordered materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on emergent nematic order arising from quantum fluctuations near a magnetically ordered phase is crucial for understanding exotic quantum phases beyond simple magnetism or superconductivity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence of bond-nematicity in insulating altermagnetic materials when quantum fluctuations cause the melting of the established altermagnetic order.

**Main result.** Melting the altermagnetic order leads to a novel phase characterized by coexisting symmetry protected topological (SPT) valence bond solid and bond-nematic orders, driven by magnon pair condensation.

**Method.** The research employs advanced computational techniques combining Variational Monte Carlo (VMC) with machine learning approaches using symmetry enhanced neural network architectures (GCNNs).

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the spin-$1/2$ square lattice $J_1-J_2-\delta$ model, which exhibits altermagnetic ordering under specific exchange coupling conditions.

**Key observables.** Bond-nematic correlation functions ($\hat{O}_{\alphaeta}$), bond-nematic structure factor ($N_{\alphaeta}^f(\vec{q})$), staggered magnetization ($m_s^2$), and the excitation spectrum (chiral magnon splitting).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The exchange modulation parameter $\delta$, the degree of geometric frustration, and the lattice size $L$ are key parameters defining the studied regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations assume results extrapolate to the thermodynamic limit; LSWT is used for excitation analysis but neglects magnon-magnon interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate exchange couplings on the Brillouin zone, chiral splitting of magnon modes at various momenta, and finite size scaling plots for order parameters like staggered magnetization and bond-nematic correlations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the model and methods (VMC/NQS) to confirming altermagnetism via magnon splitting. It then analyzes the highly frustrated regime where magnetic disorder sets in, culminating in the identification of coexisting SPT VBS and bond-nematic orders through symmetry analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study appearance of bond-nematicity in insulating altermagnetic materials induced by increased frustration and quantum-fluctuations driven melting of the altermagnetic order. Using novel machine learning approach that combines symmetry enhanced neural network architectures and variational Monte Carlo we consider the spin-$1/2$ square lattice $J_1-J_2-δ$ model known to have altermagnetic ordering in the regime of small geometric frustration and a gapless spin liquid phase in the regime of strong frustration and small exchange interaction modulation parameter $δ$. In the regime where exchange modulation is relatively large, resulting in the significant splitting of the magnon modes with different chiralities in the altermagnetic regime, we find that melting of the altermagnetic order by increased frustration leads to an intriguing phase that hosts coexisting symmetry protected topological valence bond solid and bond-nematic orders. The phase is characterized by condensation of magnon pairs that results in bond-nematicity, breaking of U(1) spin rotation and $\mathbb{Z}_2$ spin inversion symmetries and chiral splitting of the triplon-like energy levels in the excitation spectrum. Whilst numerous recent studies address non-trivial impact of altermagnetic moments on various properties in altermagnetic materials, like electronic band structure and superconductivity, influence of strong quantum fluctuations and phases that can result from melting of the altermagnetic order are much less explored. Our study therefore presents an important step in identifying exotic phases of matter that can emerge in vicinity of the altermagnetic order.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the critical issue of noisy phase gradient estimation when using Neural Network Quantum States (NQS) for complex quantum many-body systems. The authors introduce a theoretically superior direct estimator and an adaptive mixture that combines it with standard methods. This combination dramatically reduces optimization variance, enabling accurate simulations in regimes characterized by complex phases, such as those found in synthetic gauge fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** For a theoretical physicist working on AMO or open quantum systems, this work provides a crucial computational tool for simulating Hamiltonians with inherent gauge fields or non-trivial topology, allowing access to regimes previously limited by numerical noise.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The optimization of Neural-Network Quantum States (NQS) is fragile when the ground state possesses a non-trivial complex phase structure, leading to convergence stalls.

**Main result.** A direct local energy derivative estimator ($\hat{g}_{dir}$) and an adaptive mixture combining it with the standard score-function estimator significantly reduce variance, achieving much lower errors than standard methods on flux ladder systems.

**Method.** The authors compare three estimators for the phase gradient force: a standard stochastic estimator, a direct local energy derivative estimator, and a theoretically derived adaptive mixture of the two. The advantage is shown to stem from noise reduction in the estimator itself.

**Model / system.** The work uses NQS parameterized by separated amplitude-phase ansatzes to simulate quantum many-body systems, benchmarked on a 100-site flux ladder (gauge fields) and chiral XXX chains.

**Key observables.** Median relative error of the phase gradient force convergence; variance ($	ext{Var}$) and covariance ($	ext{Cov}$) between estimators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Flux strength ($\Phi$), system size ($L=100$ sites), mixing coefficient ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes a separated amplitude-phase parameterization for the direct estimator; convergence analysis relies on idealized flow bounds and is not claimed for all arbitrary network structures.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare training trajectories showing that $\hat{g}_{dir}$ significantly outperforms standard baselines, especially when networks are widened or deepened. The adaptive mixture trajectory ($\lambda$) shows a dynamic shift towards the low-variance direct signal during optimization.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem (phase gradient noise), introduces and analyzes two estimators ($\hat{g}_{std}$ vs $\hat{g}_{dir}$), develops the theoretically optimal adaptive mixture, and validates its superior performance across multiple complex physical benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neural-network quantum states (NQS) are a leading variational tool for quantum many-body physics, yet their optimization is fragile whenever the ground state carries a non-trivial sign or complex phase structure, a situation generic to gauge fields, broken time-reversal symmetry, and fermionic statistics. We trace this fragility to the stochastic estimator of the phase gradient rather than to network expressiveness. The phase sector of the Monte Carlo energy gradient is a noisy score-function estimator; differentiating the local energy instead yields a direct estimator that is unbiased for the same phase force, has far lower variance, and requires only a separated amplitude--phase ansatz. Demonstrated on a 100-site flux ladder, a small network trained this way reaches $0.89\%$ median error, where tuned standard baselines plateau at $1.8\%$ and wider or deeper standard-gradient networks degrade from $8.4\%$ to $24.6\%$. The advantage carries over to chiral XXX chains: the direct estimator again converges to a markedly lower error than the standard one, across $α$ and size; it grows with flux and vanishes in zero-flux controls. An adaptive-mixture of the two estimators is provably never worse in variance than the better endpoint at the optimal mixing coefficient, with seed-resolved diagnostics tracing much of the gain to eliminating failed runs. Estimator design thus emerges as a first-class lever for complex-valued neural quantum states.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates how to maximize measurement precision (Quantum Fisher Information per Dose) in optical interferometers suffering from photon loss. By comparing sequential strategies—especially one incorporating active control—against passive methods, the authors demonstrate that controlled multi-pass interactions yield near-quantum-limited performance under strict dose constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete experimental evidence for optimizing resource utilization in quantum metrology, directly addressing practical constraints like sample damage which is a major hurdle for implementing high-precision quantum sensors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing dose-efficient quantum phase estimation techniques for optical interferometry when light intensity is severely limited (dose-limited regimes) and photon loss is present.

**Main result.** Control-enhanced sequential strategies significantly outperform both the classical limit and parallel N00N state benchmarks, achieving Quantum Fisher Information per Dose ($\xi$) that approaches the fundamental quantum limit.

**Method.** The authors compare multiple quantum sensing protocols (Single Pass, Multi-Pass, Control-Enhanced Sequential) by calculating the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) normalized by the total dose used.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a two-arm Mach-Zehnder Interferometer (MZI) incorporating a lossy sample in one arm. The analysis models photon loss using transmissivity ($\eta$) and compares different sequential interaction strategies.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information per Dose ($\xi$), phase estimation uncertainty ($\delta\hat{\phi}$), and the ratio $\xi/\xi_{QL}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transmissivity ($\eta$), total dose constraint ($D_{th}$), number of passes ($N$), and initial state splitting parameter ($\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on post-selection techniques to analyze the surviving, information-carrying components of the quantum state.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $\xi/\xi_{QL}$ for various strategies (SP, MP, CS) as a function of loss ($\eta$) and passes ($N$), demonstrating the superiority of the Control-Enhanced Sequential strategy.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the dose-limited regime, derives the QFI per dose metric ($\xi$), compares sequential vs. parallel strategies (MP vs. N00N), and culminates in showing that active control (CS) maximizes $\xi$ by approaching the quantum limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optical interferometry is a cornerstone technique for precise phase measurements across various fields. In many applications, for example, biological imaging, it often necessitates stringent limits on light intensity to prevent adverse effects on light-sensitive samples, a condition known as dose-limited regimes. Maximizing the precision per dose is therefore crucial. In quantum metrology, quantum correlations enable high precision in phase estimation while adhering to dose constraints. Nevertheless, photon loss, including absorption by a sample, substantially diminishes the benefits of quantum enhancement in interferometry. In this work, we experimentally investigate a dose-efficient approach to quantum phase estimation using sequential strategies in the presence of loss. Performance of sequential strategies with and without control is evaluated through quantum Fisher information (QFI) per dose. Experimental results show that both sequential strategies exceed the classical limit and outperform the parallel strategy using unbalanced N00N states. Notably, the control-enhanced sequential strategy attains superior QFI per dose, approaching the quantum limit. These results highlight the promise of sequential strategy for imaging and sensing in resource-constrained scenarios, marking a significant step toward practical and efficient quantum metrology in lossy environments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes quantum error correction decoding under realistic circuit noise, comparing heuristic decoders against an optimal maximum-likelihood benchmark using Density Matrix Propagation. By developing pruning methods to manage the exponential complexity of syndrome histories, the authors quantify how much performance is lost by practical algorithms compared to theory. This work advances the understanding of fault tolerance feasibility for complex codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the practical limitations of fault tolerance by providing rigorous benchmarks for decoding algorithms. The techniques developed for bounding error rates via partial syndrome information are crucial for scaling QEC simulations beyond current limits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving accurate and efficient decoding for fault-tolerant quantum computation when subjected to realistic circuit-level noise, which introduces temporal correlations.

**Main result.** The study benchmarks various practical decoders against an optimal maximum-likelihood benchmark, finding significant performance degradation for some algorithms (like BP+OSD) in relevant noise regimes.

**Method.** The authors employ Density Matrix Propagation (DMP) to compute the optimal decoding decision by tracking the density matrix through syndrome measurement histories. They use pruning techniques to manage the exponential complexity of the full syndrome tree exploration.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes Quantum Error Correction (QEC) using stabilizer codes, such as the repetition code and cellular automaton/triangular codes. The noise is modeled as circuit-level noise affecting syndrome measurements over multiple rounds.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate ($\epsilon_L$), conditional post-measurement state ($\varrho_s$), and the fraction of relevant syndrome histories contributing to $\epsilon_L$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rate ($p$), code distance ($d$), number of syndrome extraction rounds ($r$), and the computational complexity scaling with $2^{ns}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is using pruning techniques to bound the summation over exponentially many possible syndrome histories, yielding a lower bound ($\epsilon_{	ext{low}}^L$) on the true error rate.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate matching graphs for specific codes (e.g., $T_2$), showing how error mechanisms relate to detectors and logical observables under noise. Other figures compare $\epsilon_L$ vs. physical error rate ($p$) for different decoders, highlighting the required fraction of syndrome histories needed for accuracy.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from establishing the need for optimal decoding benchmarks (DMP) to detailing the mathematical framework ($\varrho_s$, TLS decomposition), and finally applying this framework by comparing multiple state-of-the-art decoders across various code families under increasing noise levels, while analyzing computational bottlenecks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Accurate and efficient decoding is a crucial component for achieving fault-tolerant quantum computing. Realistic circuit-level noise introduces temporal correlations and degeneracy, making optimal (maximum-likelihood) decoding computationally intractable in general. As a result, practical decoders rely on heuristic approximations, and it is generally difficult to quantify how suboptimal they are, as this strongly depends on the code and noise model considered. In this work, we study the accuracy of practical decoding algorithms under circuit-level noise by comparing them against a maximum likelihood decoding benchmark. Our approach propagates the density matrix through the full memory experiment and computes the optimal decoding decision for each syndrome history. We introduce pruning techniques with rigorous bounds, allowing us to access larger numbers of syndrome-extraction rounds. We apply this framework to small instances of the repetition code and a cellular automaton code, and benchmark minimum-weight perfect matching (MWPM), belief propagation with ordered statistics decoding (BP+OSD), Tesseract, and Planar decoders against optimal decoding. While standard decoders remain close to optimal for the repetition code, we find significant deviations for the cellular automaton code, with BP+OSD deteriorating already in experimentally relevant noise regimes. Moreover, the pruning method developed here highlights that, at low physical error rates, only a narrow fraction of syndrome histories contributes significantly to the logical error rate.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores dynamical multiferroicity—the generation of magnetization using circularly polarized light interacting with phonons—to find new magnetic materials. By performing ab initio calculations on diverse frameworks, the authors demonstrate that flexible MOFs can host exceptionally large phonon-induced magnetic moments. This suggests a pathway for optically controlling magnetism in next-generation functional materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept connects lattice dynamics (phonons) directly to magnetic properties in a controllable, light-induced manner. This bridges solid-state physics, optics, and magnetism, which is relevant for understanding emergent quantum phenomena in engineered materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates dynamical multiferroicity, which is the mechanism by which magnetic fields can be generated optically via circularly polarized phonons in various crystalline materials.

**Main result.** Framework materials, particularly $	ext{Zn}(	ext{NH}_4)(	ext{formate})_3$, are identified as highly promising candidates because their flexible structures allow for large phonon-induced magnetic moments exceeding those of traditional inorganic oxides like $	ext{SrTiO}_3$.

**Method.** The study relies on ab initio calculations (DFT/DFPT) to compute phonon properties, including the calculation of phonon magnetic moments ($\mu_{\pm}$) and estimating the maximum achievable magnetization using the Lindemann melting criterion.

**Model / system.** The physical systems are diverse inorganic and organic framework materials (MOFs), ranging from perovskites to coordination compounds. The model involves coupling lattice vibrations (phonons) with magnetism, treating the magnetic moment generation as a consequence of phonon circular motion.

**Key observables.** Phonon magnetic moment ($\mu_{	ext{ph}}$), degree of circular polarization ($S$), and maximum magnetization density ($M_{	ext{melt}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magnitude of the magnetic moments (compared to $\mu_n$ or $\mu_B$) and the phonon frequencies (especially those in the mid-IR range, $\ge 12 	ext{ THz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The maximum magnetization is often estimated using the Lindemann criterion, which provides an upper bound on achievable displacements.

**Figures summary.** Figures systematically present crystal structures alongside atomic trajectories and phonon density of states plots for numerous materials, illustrating how different structural motifs yield varying levels of calculated magnetic moments.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the theoretical framework (dynamical multiferroicity), applying it to model systems ($	ext{SrTiO}_3$, $	ext{ScF}_3$), and then systematically surveying a large library of materials, with a strong emphasis on demonstrating the superior potential of MOFs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dynamical multiferroicity, which describes the magnetic fields generated by circularly polarized phonons in materials, is an established mechanism for optical control of magnetism. Here we perform ab initio calculations of dynamical multiferroicity in inorganic and organic framework materials, with the goal of identifying materials which enable the generation of large magnetic fields by light. We find the metal--organic framework material Zn(NH$_4$)(formate)$_3$ to have modes with magnetic moments almost twice that of SrTiO$_3$; these modes involve circular motions of NH$_4^+$ hydrogen ions with high gyromagnetic ratios. The complex structure and flexibility of framework materials can allow such angular momentum localization, and also increase the maximum light-induced magnetization permitted by the Lindemann melting criterion.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces Optimal Quantum Measurement Decoding (OQMD), a technique that boosts quantum classification accuracy by training the readout process using only single-qubit gates. Experiments on the Iris dataset show that OQMD provides significant performance gains, sometimes surpassing those achieved with deeper, more complex circuits, highlighting that measurement optimization is crucial for near-term quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum machine learning, the methodology of optimizing measurement bases via trainable single-qubit rotations is relevant to understanding how local control fields can enhance readout fidelity in complex quantum measurements, which has parallels in sensing or state preparation protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work addresses improving low-CNOT quantum classification accuracy by optimizing how measurement outcomes are mapped to classical labels, a technique called Optimal Quantum Measurement Decoding (OQMD). This aims to boost performance without incurring the high error rates associated with adding more two-qubit gates.

**Main result.** OQMD significantly improves peak classification accuracy, notably achieving 83.33% on minimal circuits (0 CNOTs), which surpasses the best results from deep, complex circuits (18 CNOTs). This suggests that readout optimization can yield substantial gains even when entanglement depth is limited.

**Method.** The core method involves jointly optimizing trainable single-qubit rotations applied immediately before measurement with the parameters of a variational quantum circuit. Performance comparison relies on analyzing best-observed peak accuracies using statistical tests like Mann–Whitney U, rather than mean values.

**Model / system.** The study uses Variational Quantum Classifiers (VQCs) tested on the Iris dataset for multiclass classification. The model structure involves an initial feature map, a variational ansatz parameterized by $	heta$, and the novel OQMD readout layer parameterized by $\phi$.

**Key observables.** Classification accuracy ($\%$), CNOT count (depth proxy), Peak hold-out test accuracy ($	ext{Acc}_{\max}$), Learning improvement ($\Delta$), Mann–Whitney U statistics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** CNOT depth (ranging from 0 to 18), number of trainable single-qubit rotation parameters, optimization algorithm choice (e.g., COBYLA, L-BFGS-B).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis emphasizes peak performance over mean performance due to the discrete and non-Gaussian nature of run-level accuracies; it also assumes that OQMD's overhead is negligible compared to CNOT gate errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate circuit comparisons (minimal vs. complex), learning curves showing optimization progress, and heatmaps summarizing peak accuracy gains across different rotation codes and optimizer choices.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of VQC limitations due to measurement error; it introduces OQMD as a readout-layer fix; it details experimental comparisons across varying CNOT depths (0 to 18); finally, it presents quantitative results showing performance gains and discusses the implications for hardware resource trade-offs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Near-term variational classifiers incur substantial error and latency from two-qubit gates, yet practitioners often assume that additional entangling depth is the default route to higher accuracy. This work studies Optimal Quantum Measurement Decoding (OQMD): optimizing how quantum outcomes are mapped to classical labels by training a readout layer before measurement, jointly with the variational circuit, without adding CNOTs. Experiments use trainable triple single-qubit rotations as one concrete, hardware-native realization of OQMD; other single-qubit parametrizations fit the same classical outer loop. On the Iris benchmark with a 30-point stratified test split, the best observed 0-CNOT configuration with OQMD reaches 83.33\% accuracy, with a 96\% at 9 CNOTs, exceeding the best 18-CNOT controls (56.67\%) and the best 18-CNOT configuration with OQMD (66.67\%) under a common protocol. A six-point CNOT-depth series from 0 to 18 (fixed optimizer, iteration budget, random-seed count, and ZXZ readout) shows that the highest raw scores need not occur at the largest template, so aggregate complexity is not summarized by CNOT count alone. Because run-level accuracies are discrete and non-Gaussian, we emphasize best-observed scores and, where a global comparison of pooled runs is required, Mann--Whitney $U$ tests rather than parametric tests on means. Across architectures, OQMD shows statistically consistent but magnitude-dependent gains: large peak lifts on minimal circuits coexist with a small pooled mean shift on complex 18-CNOT runs ($p\approx 0.03$) that is not ``universal'' in the sense of uniformly large practical effects.%

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes a multi-pass linear-optical interferometer using Jones matrices to determine the physical mechanism behind observed superresolution. The authors demonstrate that the effect stems from the coherent accumulation of polarization rotation across successive passes, which is robustly observable in both quantum and classical light regimes. This clarifies that the enhancement scales geometrically with the number of passes rather than solely relying on nonclassical photon resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a detailed quantum optical analysis of how geometric path length enhancement (multiple passes) can lead to superresolution effects by exploiting coherent polarization dynamics, offering insights into measurement scaling beyond simple resource counting.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to clarify the physical origin of superresolution and claimed supersensitivity observed in a multi-pass linear-optical interferometer setup.

**Main result.** Superresolution is shown to arise from coherent polarization-state rotation accumulated over multiple passes, which can be demonstrated using both single-photon quantum mechanics and classical light sources.

**Method.** The analysis employs a rigorous Jones-matrix formalism applied to the round-trip evolution of the interferometer unit cell, supplemented by comparison with classical wave implementations.

**Model / system.** The system is a linear-optical multi-pass interferometer involving components like HWP, QWP, PBS, and mirrors. The core analysis focuses on tracking polarization state transformations using Jones matrices across multiple passes (N).

**Key observables.** Accumulated phase ($\Phi_N$), superresolution fringe frequency ($1 \pm \cos(N\phi)$), and the scaling behavior of the Fisher information.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of coherent round trips ($N$) acts as a geometric enhancement parameter, distinct from measurement repetitions. The polarization rotation angles ($\varphi, \xi$) are key inputs.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes lossless linear optics and that the superresolution mechanism is governed by coherent polarization-basis realignment rather than nonclassical photon statistics.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the interferometer setup (HWP, M, PBS) and show experimental comparisons demonstrating fringe frequency doubling when increasing the number of passes from N=1 to N=2 using CW light.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the Jones-matrix formalism for the unit cell transformation, deriving the total round-trip operator, analyzing the resulting phase accumulation in the circular polarization basis, and finally comparing this theoretical model against experimental observations and related coherence frameworks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum information science has traditionally relied on nonclassical resources, such as entangled photon pairs and squeezed states, to achieve measurement performance beyond classical limits. Here, we revisit the multi-pass photonic scheme reported in Nature 450, 393 (2007) to clarify the physical origin of the observed superresolution and the associated claim of supersensitivity. Using a rigorous Jones-matrix formalism, we show that the round-trip evolution of the HQMQ linear optics unit is equivalent to the product of two reflections in polarization space, resulting in an effective rotation operator. This equivalence reveals that the accumulated phase arises from coherent polarization-state rotation on the Poincare'e sphere. The resulting phase accumulation is interpreted geometrically as a progressive realignment of the polarization state during successive forward and backward propagations. To validate the theoretical model, a classical-wave implementation is experimentally conducted, analyzed, and compared with the corresponding Jones-matrix solution. Finally, the scaling behavior of the Fisher information is analyzed to examine the origin of the claimed supersensitivity. The results are further compared with a recently developed coherence de Broglie wavelength framework, which achieves identical superresolution through repeated coherent interactions in a cascaded interferometeric architecture.

</details>
