**Summary.** This paper theoretically demonstrates that advanced cognitive functions like path integration and anticipation do not require complex, hard-wired connections. Instead, they emerge naturally as stable, self-organized properties of a simple competitive neural network layer governed by basic learning rules and adaptation mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on neuroscience, the mathematical machinery—stability analysis of coupled nonlinear dynamics, pattern formation in continuous media, and emergent structure from local rules—shares deep theoretical parallels with models used in condensed matter physics for phase transitions or glassy dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how complex cognitive functions like prospective coding and path integration can emerge spontaneously in self-organizing neural networks without requiring pre-wired, structured connectivity.

**Main result.** Prospective coding and path integration are shown to be naturally co-emergent properties arising from the simple dynamics of a single competitive network layer through Hebbian plasticity and firing-rate adaptation.

**Method.** The authors developed a theoretical framework using continuous attractor neural networks (CANNs) models, employing analytical techniques like Gaussian ansatz projections and stability analysis via eigenvalue problems.

**Model / system.** The model is a two-layer CANN incorporating Hebbian plasticity, firing-rate adaptation, and global inhibition. It simulates activity bumps moving across a spatial domain, mimicking biological circuits like the entorhinal cortex.

**Key observables.** Asymmetry parameter ($\gamma$), intrinsic bump velocity ($v_{int}$), learned connectivity profiles ($J(x, x')$), and the ability to track external speed signals via baseline current modulation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Plasticity rates ($\eta_J$, $\alpha_J$), adaptation time constants ($	au, 	au_v$), global inhibition strength ($k$), and input velocity ($v$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating continuous populations with Gaussian profiles for analytical tractability; stability analyses often assume weak inhibition or slow plasticity timescales.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of connectivity, the dependence of asymmetry ($\gamma$) on adaptation strength and velocity, and quantitative evidence showing how external speed currents modulate the bump's propagation speed in real-time.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses from establishing basic feedforward learning rules leading to Gaussian weights, then incorporating recurrence for self-sustaining activity bumps, and finally introducing an external speed current to demonstrate precise path integration capability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continuous Attractor Neural Networks (CANNs) traditionally rely on pre-wired recurrent connectivity to model spatial representations, path integration, and anticipatory dynamics. However, the biological mechanisms through which this structured connectivity emerges via learning remain relatively unexplored. This work presents a theoretical framework revealing how continuous attractor connectivity and its computational properties self-organize through Hebbian plasticity, firing-rate adaptation, and global inhibition. We show that translationally invariant inputs naturally drive the emergence of stable, Gaussian-profiled feedforward weights. Crucially, anticipatory dynamics arise spontaneously within these feedforward architectures, shifting the activity bump forward without requiring recurrent excitatory collaterals. This predictive shift can be linearly amplified across multilayer networks, consistent with anticipatory activity observed in the superficial layers of the entorhinal cortex. Furthermore, introducing recurrent interactions allows the network to learn connections capable of self-sustaining a moving bump of activity. Finally, by modulating the network with an external, time-varying baseline current that encodes speed, the system adjusts its intrinsic velocity to function as a precise unidirectional path integrator. Ultimately, this study suggests that prospective coding and path integration are not manually engineered features, but rather naturally co-emergent properties of a single self-organizing competitive network.

</details>

**Authors:** D. -S. Wang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.14034v1>

**Summary.** This paper unifies disparate quantum error correction techniques—including distillation and error mitigation—by embedding them into a general framework based on state-adaptive or channel-adaptive coding. It clarifies the relationships between these methods and proposes a theoretical model for self-correcting quantum memory systems. This provides a powerful, unified guide for designing reliable quantum information processing protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep theoretical unification of several advanced topics in quantum information (QEC, resource theory, dynamics), offering new conceptual tools for designing robust quantum hardware protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to unify various quantum error-manipulating techniques, such as distillation, error mitigation, and dynamical decoupling, under a single general framework.

**Main result.** It establishes that these diverse techniques are special cases of state-adaptive or channel-adaptive Quantum Error Correction (QEC), leading to a model for self-correcting quantum memory.

**Method.** The authors employ an abstract theoretical framework by extending QEC concepts using superchannels, fine-grained projectors, and analyzing resource conversion within the context of Quantum Resource Theory.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses general quantum information processors, focusing on code spaces defined by projectors $P$. Specific models include ensemble codes derived from AKLT systems for self-correction in memory applications.

**Key observables.** Code projector $P$, error operators $\{E_i\}$, Fidelity function $F(ho, \sigma)$, and the coding rate $\alpha = k/n$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Error parameter $\epsilon$, code distance $d=2t+1$, and temperature $eta^{-1}$ (in passive protection contexts).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that error correction can be generalized to state-adaptive or channel-adaptive settings, allowing for the inclusion of previously separate protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining QEC generally, then introducing SA and channel-adaptive extensions. It systematically shows how distillation, error mitigation, and DD fit into this unified structure before proposing a concrete self-correcting memory model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, it is shown that many quantum error-manipulating techniques, such as distillation, error mitigation, and dynamical decoupling, are special cases of the most general framework for quantum error correction. This unifying perspective is achieved by extending quantum error correction to include state-adaptive and channel-adaptive settings, as well as multi-stage coding scenarios. Based on this insight, a model of self-correcting quantum memory is also proposed. This work clarifies the relationship among these techniques and illustrates, through explicit constructions, how the unified perspective can guide the design of reliable quantum information systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates using parametrically driven multi-qubit couplers (like SNAIL) to achieve high-connectivity gate operations on superconducting qubits. By analyzing the system Hamiltonian, the authors demonstrate methods to selectively activate two-qubit ($\sqrt{	ext{iSWAP}}$) and three-qubit gates across non-adjacent qubit pairs. This advances quantum computing hardware by providing pathways for complex interactions needed for quantum simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware limitations in superconducting quantum computing by engineering complex, tunable interactions necessary for advanced algorithms and simulations like Lattice Gauge Theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is enhancing connectivity and achieving high-fidelity quantum gates (two-qubit, three-qubit) in superconducting processors beyond nearest-neighbor interactions.

**Main result.** Multi-qubit couplers, such as the SNAIL circuit, enable all-to-all coupling within a plaquette, demonstrating $\sqrt{	ext{iSWAP}}$ and three-qubit gates with fidelities reaching 95% or higher.

**Method.** The study employs numerical and analytical analysis of parametrically driven Hamiltonians, utilizing techniques like the Jacobi-Anger expansion to model time-dependent coupling strengths.

**Model / system.** The system consists of superconducting transmon qubits coupled via tunable multi-qubit couplers (dc-SQUID or SNAIL). The Hamiltonian is controlled by external flux and AC driving fields to selectively activate desired interactions.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity ($F$), gate time ($t_{	ext{gate}}$), resonance spread ($\Delta\omega$), and coupling strengths ($g_{12}, g_{123}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Asymmetry parameter ($\alpha$), external flux bias ($\phi_{	ext{dc}}$), drive amplitude ($\delta$), and qubit frequencies ($\omega_i$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often involves truncating the Hilbert space or using time-averaged approximations, which can lead to discrepancies when compared to full simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate circuit layouts (dc-SQUID, SNAIL), state population evolution during gate operations for various pairs/couplers, and plots showing coupling coefficients derived from Bessel functions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by introducing the physical platform and connectivity challenge, detailing the Hamiltonian modeling using both SQUID and SNAIL couplers, deriving the effective interaction strengths via normal mode analysis, and finally presenting fidelity benchmarks for two-qubit and three-qubit gates relevant to quantum simulation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting quantum processors could significantly profit from enhanced connectivity together with precise control of interactions and gates between qubits. Here we investigate plaquettes of four qubits that are coupled via a central tunable coupling circuit, so that not only gates between qubits connected by an edge of the plaquette can be executed but also between qubits across the diagonal. By numerically and analytically analyzing parametrically driven processes, we explore $\sqrt{\text{iSWAP}}$-gates between any pair of qubits, also across the diagonal, as well as three-qubit interactions and gates. For experimentally available circuit parameters, we for example find $\sqrt{\text{iSWAP}}$-gates with a gate time of 50 ns and 99.9\% fidelity, which is decreased to 99.4\% if two such gates are executed in parallel on disjoint qubit pairs in the plaquette. For three-qubit gates we find fidelities of 95\% fidelity at a gate time of 200 ns.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QCI Connect, a modular, full-stack quantum computing platform designed to overcome hardware fragmentation. It establishes a standardized architecture using an orchestrator and open interfaces to connect various QPUs and simulators. This allows domain experts to develop and run complex quantum applications regardless of the underlying physical quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a high-level software framework that abstracts hardware differences, which is crucial for any theoretical work aiming to test algorithms on diverse physical platforms without rewriting the core logic.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The fragmentation and lack of standardization across various competing quantum computing architectures and software stacks hinder the ability to bring the full power of quantum hardware to domain experts via the cloud.

**Main result.** QCI Connect provides a reference, modular, full-stack architecture that unifies disparate quantum hardware by separating concerns into distinct, standardized layers connected via open interfaces.

**Method.** The platform employs a layered, service-oriented, and containerized architecture utilizing an orchestrator, message broker (Redis), and canonical representations like OpenQASM to manage job workflows across heterogeneous resources.

**Model / system.** The system is QCI Connect, a full-stack platform designed to integrate various QPUs (e.g., trapped ions, neutral atoms) and HPC systems via standardized connectors and an orchestrator service.

**Key observables.** QPU status/occupation, job submission/result retrieval, and custom benchmark statistics like runtime and convergence metrics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Job priority flags (low, medium, high); the canonical format subset of OpenQASM 3 used for internal communication.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The design assumes that new components adhere to agreed system interfaces and task schemas for seamless integration; current hardware limitations restrict dynamic classical control within the canonical format.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the high-level architecture showing interaction between Physical, System, and Application Layers (Fig 1), component interconnections (Fig 2), job submission workflow via GUI/SDK (Figs 3-5), and the SDK's internal structure (Fig 6).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the necessity of a unified stack, presents the layered architecture (Physical $ightarrow$ System $ightarrow$ Application), describes core components like the orchestrator and message broker, specifies standards like OpenQASM, and outlines developer tools via the SDK/ALF.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a world of various competing quantum computing architectures, hardware-agnostic, full-stack platforms are necessary to bring the full power of quantum computing hardware to domain experts via the cloud. QCI Connect and its Software Development Kit provide a reference architecture for a full-stack platform with a modular design and open-source interface definitions, built to facilitate a community-driven application ecosystem. Here, we present its overall design and features, central interfaces, and lessons learned, both for users of the platform and as a reference guide for future developments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a Generalized Lyapunov Function (GLF) to characterize stability in complex nonlinear dynamical systems that lack simple gradient structures. They propose using Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) to learn this potential landscape by embedding physical constraints directly into the loss function. The method successfully demonstrates its utility across various non-gradient models, providing a powerful tool for analyzing system attractors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical dynamics, the concept of constructing a global potential landscape via machine learning mirrors techniques used in finding effective Hamiltonians or energy functionals in open quantum systems where simple conservation laws are broken.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is constructing a global Lyapunov-type scalar potential landscape for nonlinear dynamical systems that are not gradient systems, which is crucial for understanding stability and transitions.

**Main result.** The authors successfully developed a data-free PINN framework to construct Generalized Lyapunov Functions (GLFs) that correctly map invariant sets to low-potential or constant-potential structures across various non-gradient flows.

**Method.** A Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) is employed, minimizing a loss function that embeds the Lyapunov inequality and a weak divergence-scale compatibility condition directly into the training process.

**Model / system.** The method is tested on canonical nonlinear systems including linear oscillators, the Hopf normal form, the van der Pol oscillator, and complex three-dimensional Hopf-link flows.

**Key observables.** The learned potential landscape ($\phi$), which must satisfy $\dot{\phi} \le 0$ along trajectories; invariant sets are characterized by constant or low potential values.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weighting factors ($w_d \ll w_p \simeq w_0$) used in the total loss function to balance monotonicity enforcement against divergence constraints.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is designed to be data-free and does not assume the underlying vector field is close to a gradient system, though selecting appropriate weights is critical for convergence.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate PINN-generated landscapes and profiles for linear systems (stable/unstable foci), the Hopf normal form showing potential geometry, and results for the van der Pol oscillator and 3D linked cycles.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the GLF concept to unify various potentials, proposes the data-free PINN methodology with its specific loss terms ($L_{Lyap}, L_{zero}, L_{div}$), and then validates this framework by applying it sequentially to increasingly complex non-gradient systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A scalar potential landscape is one of the most useful ways to understand the stability and transition of a dynamical system. For non-gradient dynamics, however, the construction of a global Lyapunov-type scalar for nonlinear flows with recurrent structures remains a major obstacle. We introduce the generalized Lyapunov function, a scalar function that is non-increasing along deterministic trajectories, as a unifying notion of nonequilibrium potential. Ordinary Lyapunov functions, Freidlin--Wentzell quasi-potentials, and Ao-type potentials are recovered as special representatives. We then propose a data-free physics-informed neural-network framework in which the Lyapunov inequality and a weak divergence-scale compatibility condition are directly embedded into the loss function. The method is tested on linear systems, the Hopf normal form, the van der Pol oscillator, and a three-dimensional Hopf-link flow with two linked limit cycles. The learned landscapes agree with available analytical benchmarks and reveal the invariant sets as low-potential or constant-potential structures, providing a practical route to potential-landscape construction for nonlinear non-gradient systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the Bond-Operator Theory (BOT) as an analytical framework to study the strongly correlated $t$-$J$ model. By reformulating electron operators using localized spins and holon fermions, BOT systematically expands physical quantities in powers of $1/n$. The theory successfully reproduces key magnetic and superconducting properties, showing strong quantitative agreement with state-of-the-art numerical simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism/superconductivity, the use of an advanced operator formalism that systematically handles constraints and provides a controlled series expansion is valuable for theoretical physics dealing with complex many-body Hamiltonians.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop an analytical, constraint-free theory for the $t$-$J$ model, which is crucial for understanding high-temperature superconductivity in cuprates.

**Main result.** The Bond-Operator Theory (BOT) provides a systematic expansion in powers of $1/n$, yielding quantitative agreement with advanced numerical methods for ground state energy and excitation spectra.

**Method.** The core method is the Bond-Operator Theory, which reformulates electron operators using localized spins and mobile holon Fermi-operators to eliminate physical constraints inherent in simpler models.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the one-band $t$-$J$ model on a square lattice, described by an effective Hamiltonian involving hopping ($t$) and exchange coupling ($J$). The theory handles both magnetically ordered and disordered phases.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy, staggered magnetization, magnon spectra, polaron spectrum, and two-hole bound state binding energies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The expansion parameter is $1/n$, where $n$ relates to the maximum number of quasiparticles per unit cell. The model parameters are $t$ and $J$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that physical quantities can be expanded as a regular series in powers of $1/n$; however, the first-order expansion is noted to be insufficient for full quantitative accuracy.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare calculated spectra (magnons, polarons) and spectral weights using BOT against results from Self-Consistent Born Approximation (SCBA), Exact Diagonalization (ED), and Green's Function Monte Carlo (GFMC).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the BOT formalism by mapping electron operators to spin/holon operators, derives the effective Hamiltonian, calculates observables via diagrammatic expansion in $1/n$, and compares results for various excitations (magnons, polarons) against established numerical benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a bond-operator theory (BOT) for analytical consideration of the $t$-$J$ model and its extensions with longer-rage hopping terms. This technique is based on previously suggested representation of electron operators via localized spins 1/2 and spinless Fermi-operators of mobile holons which requires no constraint between them. We introduce a representation of operators of spins, holons, and electrons in (extended) unit cell containing several lattice sites via a zoo of Bose- and Fermi-operators acting in the Hilbert space of all quantum states of the whole unit cell. BOT provides a regular expansion of physical quantities in powers of $1/n$ using conventional diagrammatic technique, where $n\ge1$ is the maximum number of introduced quasiparticles (bosons and fermions) which can occupy a unit cell. The suggested representation reproduces commutation algebra of all operators at any $n>0$ and allows to consider both magnetically ordered and disordered phases. Some elementary excitations described in the BOT by separate bosons or fermions appear in common approaches as bound states of conventional quasiparticles. In particular, there are two-hole bound states (Cooper pairs of two holes) which are described within the BOT by separate bosons. We discuss in detail properties of the $t$-$J$ model on the square lattice with no more than two holes (polarons). Although the expansion parameter $1/n$ is not small in the physically meaningful case of $n=1$, we obtain a good quantitative agreement with previous numerical findings at $n=1$ even in the first order in $1/n$ after taking into account a few simple diagrams. Self-consistent calculations in the first order in $1/n$ bring our results to a very good quantitative agreement with previous numerical findings of the ground state energy, staggered magnetization, and spectra of magnons, polarons, and lowest-energy two-hole bound states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses Borel resurgence theory to analyze the thermal transition between two competing vacuum decay mechanisms: the bounce (quantum tunneling) and the sphaleron (thermal barrier crossing). By analyzing high-order perturbative expansions of the free energy, the authors successfully determine the critical temperature and whether the transition is first or second order for specific potential models. This demonstrates a powerful technique to extract non-perturbative physics without relying on traditional semiclassical approximations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology provides a powerful, purely perturbative route to calculate non-perturbative quantities like tunneling rates and critical temperatures, which is highly valuable in understanding phase transitions in quantum field theory or condensed matter systems where semiclassical methods are traditionally used.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the thermal transition between two competing non-perturbative saddle points—the bounce (tunneling) and the sphaleron (thermal jump)—in a quantum system with a metastable vacuum.

**Main result.** The Borel resurgence analysis successfully determines the transition temperature, order (second or first), and one-loop prefactors purely from high-order perturbative expansions, without needing semiclassical inputs.

**Method.** The core method involves calculating the perturbative expansion of the thermal free energy and extracting non-perturbative information by analyzing its Borel singularity data $(A, b, S)$.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on quantum mechanical systems with metastable potentials, specifically modeling cases leading to second-order (cubic potential) and first-order (quintic potential) phase transitions.

**Key observables.** Thermal free energy $F(eta)$, Borel singularity location ($A$), characteristic exponent ($b$), and Stokes constant ($S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$ or $eta$), the transition temperature ($T_c$), and the coupling/potential parameters defining the specific models.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on treating the weak-coupling expansion as a semi-classical approximation and assumes that Borel resurgence theory can fully encode the non-perturbative physics across the transition.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the extracted Borel singularity $A(T)$ against classical saddle actions, showing smooth joining for second-order transitions and clear kinks/crossings for first-order transitions in the quintic model.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically applies Borel resurgence to compute high-order coefficients of the free energy. It then analyzes how the extracted parameters $(A, b, S)$ characterize the nature (second vs. first order) and location ($T_c$) of the thermal transition between the bounce and sphaleron saddle points for different potentials.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the thermal transition between the bounce and the sphaleron in quantum mechanics with a metastable vacuum from the viewpoint of Borel resurgence. For two models representing a second-order and a first-order transition, we compute the perturbative expansion of the thermal free energy to high orders and extract the leading Borel singularity data $(A,b,S)$ as functions of temperature. The Borel singularity location $A$ reproduces the on-shell action of the dominant saddle on both sides of the transition, joining smoothly in the second-order case and developing a kink in the first-order case. The characteristic exponent $b$ jumps between $0$ and $1/2$ across the transition, counting the zero modes of the corresponding saddle. The Stokes constant $S$ matches the one-loop determinant around the saddle. The perturbative expansion around the false vacuum thus determines the transition temperature, the order of the transition, and the decay rate including the one-loop prefactor without relying on semiclassical inputs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a general theoretical framework using symmetry-broken DFT to explain metal-insulator transitions in paramagnetic transition-metal oxides. It shows that the electronic state depends critically on the competition between spin disorder and atomic positional displacements, successfully distinguishing why $	ext{NaFeO}_3$ is insulating while $	ext{NaOsO}_3$ is metallic.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the concept of emergent electronic behavior arising from competing structural/magnetic degrees of freedom (disorder) is relevant to understanding complex quantum many-body systems where multiple order parameters compete.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the fundamental physical mechanism governing whether the high-temperature paramagnetic (PM) phase of transition-metal oxide paramagnets is metallic or insulating.

**Main result.** The metal-insulator behavior in these PM phases is governed by a competition between spin disorder (local moment distribution) and positional symmetry breaking (atomic displacements).

**Method.** Symmetry-broken Density-Functional Theory (DFT) calculations are employed to model the electronic structure of both insulating and metallic states.

**Model / system.** The research focuses on transition-metal oxides, specifically comparing $	ext{NaFeO}_3$ (insulating PM phase) and $	ext{NaOsO}_3$ (metallic PM phase). Calculations utilize spin special-quasirandom structures to model disorder without assuming long-range order.

**Key observables.** Band gap size ($	ext{eV}$), local magnetic moment distribution, and magnitude of off-center atomic displacements ($	ext{\AA}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The competition between spin disorder strength and the degree of positional polymorphism is key; strong correlation effects are explicitly avoided.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach avoids invoking strong electronic correlations by using DFT methods that model symmetry breaking via configurational averaging (spin-SQS).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the DOS, showing gap closing for $	ext{NaOsO}_3$ and gap opening/insulating behavior for $	ext{NaFeO}_3$, correlating these changes with moment distributions and atomic displacements.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by contrasting insulating vs. metallic PM phases in oxides. It then develops a general framework based on analyzing how spin disorder interacts with positional symmetry breaking, using DFT calculations on specific examples ($	ext{NaFeO}_3$ vs $	ext{NaOsO}_3$) to validate this competition.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Numerous transition-metal oxides have low-temperature antiferromagnetic (AFM) states and high-temperature paramagnetic (PM) phases, where the AFM state is usually insulating while the PM phase can be either insulating or metallic. Without involving strong correlation, we use symmetry-broken density-functional theory (DFT) to obtain the PM phases of insulating NaFeO3 vs the recently discovered metallic NaOsO3. We develop the understanding of insulating and metallic behaviors in paramagnetic oxides by analyzing the interactions between magnetic and positional symmetry breaking: The insulating gap is governed by the competition between the spin disorder that induces a distribution of different magnitudes of local magnetic moments and the polymorphous distribution of off-center atomic displacements. NaFeO3, on the other hand, has large positional displacement with small spin-disorder-induced moments distribution, leading to insulating PM phase, whereas NaOsO3 has a pronounced spin-disorder-induced moments distribution that forces the PM phase to become metallic. Our work identifies this symmetry-breaking competition as a general framework to bridge seemingly disparate metal-insulator behaviors in transition-metal oxides paramagnets without invoking strong correlation.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a highly efficient classical simulation framework for Szegedy quantum walks and related algorithms. It bypasses the need to construct massive unitary matrices by decomposing evolution into fundamental operator actions like updates and reflections. This allows researchers to numerically study complex quantum processes, such as search and annealing, on large graphs.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, scalable computational tools for simulating quantum dynamics that are otherwise intractable due to exponential Hilbert space size, bridging theoretical quantum algorithm design with practical numerical implementation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing efficient classical simulation methods for Szegedy quantum walks and related phase-estimation algorithms that avoid constructing large, explicit unitary evolution operators.

**Main result.** The framework achieves optimal O(N^2) complexity for dense graphs and linear scaling with edges for sparse graphs by simulating fundamental update and reflection operators directly.

**Method.** Simulation relies on expressing the walk evolution using fundamental operators (Update $V$, Reflection $R_0$) and employing techniques like expansion-recomposition or working in a reduced subspace representation for sparse graphs. Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE) is used to implement amplitude amplification steps.

**Model / system.** The model is the Szegedy quantum walk on a graph with N nodes, operating in an $N^2$-dimensional Hilbert space. Applications include quantum simulated annealing and quantum search algorithms.

**Key observables.** Probability distributions of finding the system at marked nodes or measuring specific phases ($	heta$) during QPE.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph size (N), number of edges, simulation time steps, and precision limits related to QPE repetitions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The update operator $V$ is not unique, but the framework shows consistency for certain measurements. For sparse graphs, a reduced subspace representation $\mathcal{H}_R$ is used, assuming all necessary operators map within this space.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate probability distributions over time steps and marked nodes for quantum search; diagrams show QPE circuits and the mathematical procedure of expansion-recomposition in the reduced vector space.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the walk evolution using fundamental operators, detailing the numerical simulation techniques (especially for sparse graphs via subspace reduction), applying these methods to complex algorithms like QPE and amplitude amplification, and finally demonstrating results on search problems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum walks provide a versatile framework for quantum algorithms across a wide range of applications. We develop efficient classical simulation methods for Szegedy quantum walks that avoid explicit construction of the full unitary evolution operator. Unlike previous approaches restricted to a particular walk formulation, our framework is built from fundamental update and reflection operators, enabling the simulation of a broader class of Szegedy walk formulations. We further extend these methods to phase-estimation-based algorithms coupled to the walk, including implementations suitable for large sparse graphs. The resulting methods achieve optimal $O(N^2)$ complexity for dense graphs with $N$ nodes. For sparse graphs, the computational cost scales linearly with the number of edges, which is $O(N)$ in many cases. We implement the framework in the Python package SQWLib and illustrate its capabilities through simulations of representative algorithms, including quantum simulated annealing and quantum search on graphs. These results provide a practical tool for studying Szegedy-walk-based algorithms numerically beyond purely analytical treatments.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces an open-source Julia package, SmoQyElPhQMC.jl, designed for scalable Quantum Monte Carlo simulations of electron-phonon coupled models. By implementing advanced techniques like the Checkerboard Approximation and Kernel Polynomial Method preconditioning, it achieves near-linear scaling in system size and inverse temperature. The package is used to study phenomena such as charge density wave formation in canonical models like Holstein and oSSH.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of highly efficient numerical tools like SmoQyElPhQMC.jl to tackle strongly coupled lattice dynamics is crucial for understanding emergent quantum phases relevant to superconductivity and charge ordering in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to perform efficient and scalable Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations for electron-phonon coupled models.

**Main result.** The package successfully enables near-linear scaling in system size and inverse temperature, allowing the study of CDW and BOW order formation in Holstein and oSSH models.

**Method.** It utilizes advanced QMC techniques, including Hybrid Quantum Monte Carlo (HQMC) updates combined with the Checkerboard Approximation (MSCHK) and Kernel Polynomial Method (KPM) preconditioning for linear scaling.

**Model / system.** The focus is on electron-phonon coupled Hamiltonians, specifically generalized spin-symmetric models like the Holstein model and the oSSH model. These systems involve coupling between itinerant electrons and lattice vibrations (phonons).

**Key observables.** Charge structure factor ($S_{cdw}$), bond order wave structure factor ($S_{bow}$), single-particle electron spectral function $A(k, \omega)$, and correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The simulations are performed at finite temperatures ($eta$) and involve coupling strengths ($\lambda_h=0.25$, $\lambda_o=0.36$) and phonon frequencies ($\Omega/t = 0.5$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include the MSCHK approximation to reduce scaling from $O(eta N^3)$ to $O(eta N)$, and using pseudofermion fields for determinant calculation.

**Figures summary.** Figures show structure factors ($S_{cdw}, S_{bow}$) increasing with system size/inverse temperature, indicating CDW/BOW order. The spectral function $A(k, \omega)$ reveals gap openings consistent with insulating CDW order in the Holstein model.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the package, details the Hamiltonian structure ($\hat{H} = \hat{U} + \hat{V} + \hat{K}$), explains the mathematical machinery for achieving linear scaling (MSCHK, pseudofermion fields, KPM preconditioning), and concludes with benchmark results on Holstein and oSSH models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce version 1.0 of the SmoQyElPhQMC package, an open-source Julia code for performing scalable quantum Monte Carlo simulations of electron-phonon coupled model Hamiltonians. SmoQyElPhQMC is built upon the SmoQyDQMC codebase and implements improved versions of the algorithms presented in [B. Cohen-Stead \textit{et al}., Phys. Rev. E {\bf 105}, 065302 (2022)] to enable linear-scaling simulations of a broad class of uncorrelated $e$-ph models both in system size and inverse temperature. By extending the functionality of the flexible scripting interface introduced in SmoQyDQMC, the SmoQyElPhQMC package continues to allow users to adapt it to different workflows and interface with other software packages in the Julia ecosystem. The code for this package can be downloaded from our GitHub repository at https://github.com/SmoQySuite/SmoQyElPhQMC.jl or installed using the Julia package manager. The online documentation, including examples, can be obtained from our documentation page at https://smoqysuite.github.io/SmoQyElPhQMC.jl/stable/.

</details>

### [Spherical metadensity functional learning for inhomogeneous classical fluids](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14370v1)

**Authors:** Stefanie M. Kampa, Matthias Schmidt, Florian Sammüller

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel neural metadensity functional capable of modeling structural properties of inhomogeneous classical fluids in spherical geometries. By training on simulation data, it accurately predicts key correlation functions and demonstrates thermodynamic consistency across multiple calculation pathways. This represents an advanced ML approach to solving complex many-body statistical mechanics problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluids, the rigorous application of machine learning to derive complex many-body functionals and verify fundamental physical symmetries is a powerful methodological tool applicable to quantum systems where exact functional forms are unknown.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a neural metadensity functional approach to model classical fluids with truncated interactions, specifically adapting the theory for three-dimensional spherical geometry.

**Main result.** The framework successfully predicts structural properties and verifies thermodynamic consistency relations (like test particle sum rules) by comparing results derived from the neural network against established theories like Fundamental Measure Theory (FMT).

**Method.** It uses supervised training of a neural network to represent the one-body direct correlation functional, $c_1$, which captures dependence on both density and pair potential in curved space.

**Model / system.** The system is inhomogeneous classical fluids interacting via repulsive pairwise potentials ($\phi(r)$), studied under various confinements such as spherical cavities or test particle setups. The theory builds upon Density Functional Theory (DFT) principles.

**Key observables.** One-body and two-body direct correlation functions ($c_1, c_2$), density profiles ($ho(r)$), radial distribution functions ($g(r)$), and the excess chemical potential ($\mu_{exc}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spherical geometry effects (curvature), scaled pair potential ($eta\phi$), and thermodynamic parameters like temperature ($eta$) and chemical potential ($\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the functional dependence of $c_1$ can be accurately approximated by a neural network, and careful handling of spherical symmetry and geometric weighting functions is required.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate density profiles in various confinements (cavities/shells), demonstrate Henderson inversion recovering pair potentials from $g(r)$, and compare calculated two-body correlation functions ($c_2$ and $ar{c}_2$) across different theoretical routes (FMT vs. Neural Functional).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the DFT framework, detailing the adaptation of local learning for spherical geometry (incorporating curvature terms), deriving relationships for two-body correlations ($c_2$), and finally validating the functional's thermodynamic consistency across multiple calculation routes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop classical density functional learning to address fluids with truncated pairwise interparticle interactions in three-dimensional spherical geometry. Simulation data for systems with randomized repulsive pair potentials provide the basis for supervised training of a neural metadensity functional, thereby making efficient use of results for radial distribution functions in the bulk fluid via the test particle route. Specifically, we develop spherical local learning in order to represent the one-body direct correlation functional in terms of a neural network, which captures spatial curvature effects as well as the metadensity functional dependence on the thermally scaled pair potential. The framework yields efficient access to inhomogeneous structuring and related physical phenomena that occur in fluids and general solvents when adsorbed against curved solutes and confined inside of spherical and planar cavities. Test particle setups facilitate accurate prediction of the bulk fluid pair structure and verification of thermodynamic test particle sum rules via functional line integration. Applying the metadensity functional for Henderson inversion allows one to infer accurately the pair potential from the bulk radial distribution function. We address implications of the geometrical setup for two-body quantities and obtain the two-body direct correlation functional from automatic differentiation. For the hard sphere fluid, we confirm metadensity functional predictions against results from a standard neural density functional with fixed pair potential as well as to an analytic functional as given by fundamental measure theory. Simulation results provide further reference and corroborate reliable results of the spherical neural metadensity functional across a broad range of applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops the mathematical framework to treat tensor network representations of quantum states as points on a manifold. By applying advanced differential geometry, it rigorously characterizes the inherent gauge symmetries by showing that the physical state space corresponds to a unique quotient Riemannian manifold. This provides a powerful geometric foundation for analyzing and optimizing these complex many-body quantum descriptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools—geometric invariants like Riemannian metrics on quotient spaces—to analyze and compare different numerical representations of quantum states derived from tensor networks, which is crucial for developing stable optimization algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is mathematically characterizing the redundancy (gauge freedom) inherent in representing quantum states or circuits using tensor networks.

**Main result.** The authors establish a Riemannian fundamental theorem by showing that the physical state space can be identified with a quotient manifold derived from the full tensor network manifold via a Riemannian submersion.

**Method.** The work employs advanced differential geometry, specifically utilizing concepts like group actions, Riemannian submersions, and isometric group actions to map the redundant parameter space onto a unique, geometrically defined quotient manifold.

**Model / system.** The model is general tensor networks (e.g., MPS, PEPS) used to represent high-dimensional quantum states or depth-two quantum circuits. The mathematical framework involves endowing these network structures with Riemannian manifold metrics.

**Key observables.** Gauge freedom, the resulting quotient tensor network manifold structure ($\mathcal{B}$), and the metric tensors defining this geometry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($D$), physical dimension ($d$), and the number of layers/gates in the circuit representation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on assuming that certain tensor components are full-rank, which allows for the application of Riemannian submersion theorems derived from isometric group actions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate various tensor network structures (V1 to V6) and visualize the projection process: $	ext{Manifold} 	o 	ext{Quotient Manifold}$ using diagrams showing gauge action on different circuit types (e.g., Figure 2, Figure 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from general geometric prerequisites (Riemannian submersions and group actions) to defining the tensor network manifold structure for specific families (MPS, PEPS), culminating in proving that gauge freedom allows for a unique quotient space characterized by a Riemannian submersion.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tensor networks provide a powerful framework for efficiently representing high-dimensional data and many-body quantum states. Endowing tensor networks with a Riemannian manifold structure provides a natural setting for numerical optimization and analysis. A central feature of tensor networks is their gauge freedom, whose characterisation (captured by so-called fundamental theorems) underlies both their intrinsic structure and the design of numerical algorithms. In this work, we study the interaction between the Riemannian manifold structure and the gauge freedom for several families of tensor networks. Using group actions and Riemannian submersions, we establish a Riemannian fundamental theorem for the tensor network families studied.

</details>

**Authors:** Kaushlendra Kumar  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.13824v1>

In co- ordinates with Dirac axis n = (0, 0, 1), the bare two-eigenvalue Dirac operator controls only directions transverse to n. Points in the same slice z = z0 have finite distance |r −s⊥|/γ, where γ = |d+ −d−| is the Dirac eigenvalue gap. Any off-slice sep- aration contains an unconstrained component parallel to n, and the corresponding bare spectral distance is infinite.</figcaption>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2. Scalar-anchored calibration geometry. The scalar states ωL, ωR define link lengths ℓL = µ−1 L , ℓR = µ−1 R , with d(ωL, ωR)2 = ℓ2 L + ℓ2 R. Inside the qubit block, the middle- sector distance is the calibrated chord d(ωρ(r), ωρ(s)) = Λ−1|r −s|.</figcaption>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that the standard trace distance metric used for quantifying distinguishability between qubit states can be rigorously recovered from the Connes spectral distance by embedding the system into an extended 'scalar-qubit-scalar' model. This establishes a deep, quantifiable link between noncommutative geometry and quantum information theory metrics. The result is crucial because it shows how geometric structures derived from spectral analysis can precisely predict operational limits in quantum measurement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful geometric framework connecting abstract mathematical tools (spectral triples/Connes distance) directly to fundamental physical quantities in quantum information theory (trace distance). It suggests that operational metrics can be derived from underlying spectral structures, which is highly relevant for theoretical physics exploring quantization procedures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to determine if the standard operational metric for qubit state discrimination, the trace distance, can be mathematically recovered from the Connes spectral distance within a finite noncommutative model.

**Main result.** The equal-prior Helstrom trace-distance geometry of qubit states is successfully recovered from the Connes spectral distance via a calibrated relationship involving a scale factor $\Lambda$. This establishes a deep connection between non-commutative geometry and quantum information theory metrics.

**Method.** The authors employ techniques from noncommutative geometry, specifically calculating the Connes spectral distance using a Dirac operator on an extended algebra incorporating scalar reference sectors. They derive relationships by optimizing over these auxiliary variables to match known quantum metrics.

**Model / system.** The system models qubit states ($ho$) living on the Bloch ball, which is embedded into a larger structure comprising two scalar reference sectors ($C_L, C_R$) coupled isotropically to the qubit block ($M_2(\mathbb{C})$). This 'scalar-qubit-scalar' model extends the geometry beyond what the bare qubit algebra can capture.

**Key observables.** Trace distance $T(ho, \sigma)$, Connes spectral distance $d_D(\varphi, \psi)$, and the optimal success probability $	ext{p}_{	ext{succ}}(ho, \sigma)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strengths ($\mu_L, \mu_R$) defining the scalar link lengths ($\ell_L, \ell_R$), which determine the overall calibration scale $\Lambda = (\mu_L^2 + \mu_R^2)^{1/2}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the equal-prior case and relies on extending the qubit algebra with two scalar reference sectors to ensure the full Bloch ball geometry is captured.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the necessity of the scalar-anchored model by showing limitations in the bare $M_2(\mathbb{C})$ setting. Figure 2 visually represents the calibrated geometry, detailing how the overall distance relates to individual link strengths ($\ell_L, \ell_R$) and the middle-sector distance.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the failure of simpler models (Figure 1), then introducing the scalar-anchored model to derive key distance formulas relating spectral metrics to trace distances (Theorem III.1). Subsequent sections refine this calibration, deriving specific relationships for different types of state pairs and confirming consistency relations like the Pythagorean theorem in the scalar sectors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the equal-prior Helstrom trace-distance geometry of qubit states is recovered from Connes spectral distance in a finite scalar-qubit-scalar model. The two scalar reference sectors couple isotropically to the qubit block through identity Dirac links, so that the full Bloch ball, including mixed states, inherits its standard chordal trace-distance geometry from the finite spectral metric. The scalar-sector distances serve a distinct calibration role: they determine the individual link lengths, satisfy a Pythagorean consistency relation, and reconstruct the middle-sector scale.

</details>

**Authors:** Meng Cao, Kun Zhou  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.14253v1>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced algebraic tools by proving a unified $\tau$-monomial decomposition theorem for matrices over finite fields. It applies these tools to construct numerous new infinite families of optimal pure quantum $(r, \delta)$-LRCs using the Matrix-Product code framework. The results yield record-breaking quantum codes and establish novel connections between classical locality properties and quantum error correction bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep connections between abstract algebra (matrix decompositions over finite fields) and the concrete bounds on quantum error-correcting capabilities, which is fundamental for designing robust quantum computation protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is constructing new quantum error-correcting codes, specifically optimal pure quantum (r, delta)-Locally Recoverable Codes (LRCs).

**Main result.** The authors established a unified $\tau$-monomial decomposition theorem and constructed multiple new infinite families of such LRCs, including 30 instances that are simultaneously optimal, best-known, and record-breaking.

**Method.** The work heavily relies on the Matrix-Product (MP) construction method using specialized defining matrices called $\tau$-optimal defining ($\tau$-OD) matrices derived from algebraic theorems over finite fields.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum error correction, dealing with quantum states encoded in subspaces of $(\mathbb{C}^q)^{\otimes n}$. The construction links the properties of classical linear codes (like $(r, \delta)$-LRCs) to their quantum counterparts via MP codes.

**Key observables.** Quantum code parameters $[[n, k, d]]_q$, and the locality/recovery parameters $(r, \delta)$ for LRCs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The field characteristic (odd vs. even), the size of the finite field $\mathbb{F}_{q^2}$, and various indices ($k_1, k_2, l_1, l_2$) governing code lengths and dimensions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The existence of specific algebraic structures, such as invertible self-adjoint matrices admitting a $\tau$-monomial decomposition, is crucial for the construction theorems to hold.

**Figures summary.** References are made to Tables I, II, III, and IV, which provide detailed numerical comparisons showing that the newly constructed codes surpass existing records in Grassl's database regarding minimum distance $d$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing foundational algebraic theorems (the unified $\tau$-monomial decomposition theorem) to applying these results to construct infinite families of quantum codes, and finally specializing these constructions to yield optimal pure quantum $(r, \delta)$-LRCs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we employ MP codes whose defining matrices are $τ$-optimal defining ($τ$-OD) matrices to construct new quantum codes and quantum $(r,δ)$-LRCs. Specifically, we report the following results: We establish a unified $τ$-monomial decomposition theorem for invertible self-adjoint matrices over finite fields of arbitrary characteristic, which generalizes the result in "Quantum codes using the $τ$-OD MP construction" where the characteristic was required to be odd. Based on this theorem, we prove the existence of $τ$-OD matrices over $\mathbb{F}_{q^2}$ for any characteristic and demonstrate that there exist several new infinite families of $τ$-OD matrices over $\mathbb{F}_{q^2}$ of characteristic $2$. As an application of MP codes involving $τ$-OD matrices, we construct several infinite families of quantum codes with flexible parameters. Within this framework, we present $222$ record-breaking quantum codes that surpass the best-known records maintained in Grassl's database.   We propose two effective schemes for constructing optimal pure quantum $(r,δ)$-LRCs via MP codes. Accordingly, we construct four new infinite families of optimal pure quantum $(r,δ)$-LRCs with flexible parameters. Notably, we report an interesting phenomenon by exhibiting $30$ optimal pure quantum $(r,δ)$-LRCs derived from our framework; that is, there exist quantum codes that are not only optimal pure quantum $(r,δ)$-LRCs but also, according to Grassl's database, best-known, optimal, or record-breaking quantum codes. To the best of our knowledge, the new discovery that quantum codes are simultaneously optimal pure quantum $(r,δ)$-LRCs and record-breaking quantum codes has not been previously reported in the literature.

</details>

### [Quantum Horizon: An evaluation of quantum computing as a threat to Bitcoin and Ethereum](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14484v1)

**Authors:** Iosif M. Gershteyn, Jacob A. Alber  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.14484v1>

**Summary.** This work assesses the threat of quantum computers to Bitcoin and Ethereum, concluding that Shor's algorithm breaks signatures but mining remains relatively safe. The authors forecast a probabilistic arrival of a CRQC and argue that while the risk is real, it is largely mitigable through proactive governance and migration efforts.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO physics, the paper uses complex modeling techniques (Monte Carlo) to assess technological risk against fundamental quantum computing capabilities (Shor's algorithm), which is highly relevant to understanding the limits of current information security paradigms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper evaluates the threat posed by quantum computers, specifically Shor's algorithm, to the elliptic-curve digital signatures used by Bitcoin and Ethereum.

**Main result.** While a cryptographically relevant quantum computer (CRQC) poses a real threat via signature breaking, the risk is bounded because many assets are migratable, making governance, not technology, the primary constraint.

**Method.** The authors combine Monte-Carlo forecasting with physical qubit scaling models and expert surveys to predict CRQC arrival timelines. They also use simulation models for on-chain attack success rates.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on cryptographic primitives (ECDSA, BLS) securing cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin and Ethereum. The threat is modeled against the required logical qubit count needed to run Shor's algorithm.

**Key observables.** CRQC arrival odds (e.g., 30% by 2040), number of irreducibly at-risk BTC coins ($\sim 2.3$ million), and percentage of exposed ETH ($50–65\%$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Required logical qubits for breaking secp256k1 ($\sim 1,200–2,330$), quadratic speedup from Grover's algorithm, and the time required for governance action.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The threat is contingent on fault-tolerant hardware; the forecast uses a bimodal distribution combining physics scaling with expert opinion; migration feasibility depends on community coordination.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Bitcoin's supply split into permanently-at-risk, migratable, and protected coins. Other figures compare quantum 'hashrate' vs. network hashrate and show the combined bimodal distribution for CRQC timing.

**Paper structure.** The paper first distinguishes between Shor's (signature threat) and Grover's (mining threat). It then models the hardware scaling to forecast CRQC arrival, analyzes the specific exposure of BTC and ETH supply, and concludes by emphasizing that governance dictates the timeline for mitigation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computing poses a real, broad-based, but bounded and substantially mitigable threat to Bitcoin and Ethereum. We separate the two quantum algorithms that public discussion routinely conflates: Shor's algorithm breaks the elliptic-curve signatures (ECDSA over secp256k1, BLS over BLS12-381) that authorize spending, whereas Grover's algorithm does not meaningfully threaten proof-of-work mining, which is protected by a merely quadratic speedup, fault-tolerant per-operation costs, a square-root parallelization wall, and difficulty adjustment. Folding hardware scaling, the falling resource requirement, a fault-tolerance readiness lag, and expert surveys into a single Monte-Carlo forecast yields a wide, bimodal arrival distribution for a cryptographically relevant quantum computer: about a one-in-six chance by 2035, near 30% by 2040, and about 60% by 2050. Exposure is concentrated and mostly migratable: of Bitcoin's roughly six million quantum-exposed coins only about 2.3 million are irreducibly at risk, while 50 to 65% of Ether sits at key-revealed accounts that can adopt post-quantum signatures. A timely migration beats even an optimistic 2035 machine, so the binding constraint is governance, not technology. A survey of the top twenty cryptocurrencies finds none fully post-quantum. Reproducible models accompany every quantitative claim.

</details>

<a id="paper-2606.14677"></a>
### [Quasilinear Equivalence Checking for Detector Error Models](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14677v1)

**Authors:** Mathys Rennela  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.14677v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2606.14677_figures/2606.14677_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 2: Tanner graph reduction using rewriting rules R. Error mechanisms with identical targets are fused together algebraically via p12 = p1 ⊕p2 (Rule R1).</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper develops a formal, algebraic theory for Detector Error Models (DEMs), which are used to characterize noise in quantum circuits. It introduces an efficient rewriting system that guarantees a unique normal form for any DEM term. This allows researchers to create the first static decision procedure to rigorously check if two different error descriptions represent the same underlying physical fault model.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides foundational mathematical tools for verifying quantum computation correctness by providing rigorous methods to compare complex noise models derived from physical hardware constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a complete, decidable mathematical theory for Detector Error Models (DEMs) to rigorously check if two different descriptions of quantum hardware noise are equivalent.

**Main result.** The authors provide an efficient quasilinear time decision procedure based on computing a unique normal form derived from the DEM structure via rewriting systems and Tanner graphs. This establishes structural equivalence for non-adaptive QEC pipelines and provides sound procedures for partially-adaptive circuits.

**Method.** The methodology involves constructing an equational theory for DEMs, defining a confluent rewriting system (PROP), and characterizing equivalence by comparing unique normal forms derived from the structure's invariants (Tanner graphs).

**Model / system.** The model is the Detector Error Model (DEM), which formally describes physical error mechanisms in quantum circuits. These errors are characterized by probabilities, specific detectors triggered, and logical observables flipped during a fault event.

**Key observables.** Detector outcomes ($\{0, 1\}^D$), observable outcomes ($\{0, 1\}^O$), and the associated probability distributions (scope distribution $\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Error probabilities ($p < 0.5$); circuit depth/size ($|E|$); scope structure (Skeleton $ho(t)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial analysis assumes detector indices are absolute; later work addresses the limitations of assuming observable-separation for full decoder-equivalence in adaptive circuits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Tanner graph reduction, showing how error mechanisms with identical targets are fused algebraically using probability combination rules (e.g., $p_{12} = p_1 \oplus p_2$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the DEM structure and its algebraic theory to proving the existence of a unique normal form via rewriting systems, establishing invariants using Tanner graphs, and finally developing increasingly general decision procedures for equivalence (scope-local vs. full decoder-equivalence) applicable to adaptive QEC.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A Detector Error Model (DEM) is a structured representation of error mechanisms in quantum circuits, which has gained popularity in quantum compilation pipelines for its ability to capture fault-tolerance at a circuit level. It lists error mechanisms as instructions targeting detectors and observables, specifying for each physical fault channel the probability that the fault fires, the detectors it triggers, and the observables it flips.   In this paper, we develop an equational theory for DEMs, with its associated categorical semantics. We present a sound, terminating, confluent rewriting system for DEM terms, formulating it as a symmetric monoidal theory (a PROP) over the Giry monad. We prove that every DEM term has a unique normal form, which can be computed efficiently in quasilinear time $O(k|E|\log|E|)$, where $|E|$ is the number of instructions and $k$ bounds the size of a target set. This provides a complete set of invariants (via Tanner graphs) for structural DEM equivalence. We provide the first static decision procedure for DEM equivalence, with rigorous correctness guarantees. It is complete (decides full decoder-equivalence exactly) for non-adaptive quantum error correction (QEC) pipelines, and scales to a sound and applicable decision procedure for partially-adaptive circuits (lattice surgery, distributed QEC, ...) without suffering exponential overhead. We discuss its application to the verification and optimisation of quantum compilers.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the challenge of making Large Language Models understand quantum mechanics by mapping unitary operators into the LLM's latent space. It introduces an autoregressive synthesis method that predicts quantum gates sequentially, conditioned on both the current state (via PTM) and natural language instructions. This approach significantly outperforms existing quantum compilation methods, suggesting a path toward quantum-aware foundation models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant because it proposes a novel paradigm for integrating symbolic reasoning (LLMs) with structured physical computation (quantum circuits), potentially leading to automated discovery or optimization tools for quantum algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is bridging the gap between Large Language Models (LLMs), which are inherently blind to quantum representations like unitary matrices, and the need for them to reason about these operations.

**Main result.** The proposed multimodal alignment framework successfully enables language-conditioned synthesis of quantum circuits, achieving state-of-the-art performance competitive with complex baselines across various circuit lengths.

**Method.** The authors map unitary operators (represented as Pauli Transfer Matrices) into the LLM's latent space using a lightweight encoder and projector. Synthesis is performed autoregressively by predicting one gate at a time based on the residual PTM state, conditioned on both visual (PTM) and textual inputs.

**Model / system.** The system involves synthesizing quantum unitaries ($U$) for Clifford+T circuits over a Pauli rotation gate set. The representation used is the real-valued Pauli Transfer Matrix (PTM), which captures the unitary transformation in a matrix form suitable for embedding into an LLM.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F(P)$) of synthesis progress, defined as $	ext{Tr}(P)/4^n$, where success is marked when $F \ge 0.999$. Success rates and mean fidelity are key comparative metrics against baselines.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the system ($n$-qubits), the patch size used for encoding (e.g., $16	imes 16$), and the training data scale (up to 9.2M circuits).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that the PTM representation can be effectively projected into the LLM's latent space, allowing the model to treat quantum state evolution as a sequence prediction task.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall multimodal alignment workflow (U -> PTM -> Encoder -> LLM Input). Other figures compare synthesis success rates and fidelity traces against established baselines like greedy search and RL approaches across varying circuit lengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem gap, details the multimodal alignment architecture using PTMs as visual inputs alongside text. It presents results showing superior scaling and performance over existing quantum compilation methods, culminating in demonstrations of language-conditioned synthesis capabilities.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can Large Language Models (LLMs) understand and reason about quantum operators? Despite their remarkable capabilities in mathematics and symbolic reasoning, LLMs remain inherently blind to quantum representations such as unitary matrices. In this work, we take a step toward bridging this gap by introducing an approach that maps unitary operators into the latent space of an LLM, enabling unified modeling over quantum and linguistic inputs. We instantiate this idea on Clifford+T circuit synthesis over a Pauli rotation gate set, where our model achieves results competitive with state-of-the-art methods and scales consistently with training data, with no signs of saturation. Our approach further enables language-conditioned synthesis, allowing gate constraints unseen during training to be specified directly in natural language. This work suggests a path toward quantum--aware foundation models that can natively interpret and reason about quantum operations, which could have broader implications reaching across quantum compilation and algorithm discovery.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper explores a quantum generalization of 'compact graphs' by examining the relationship between classical and quantum automorphism structures. The core finding is that any graph satisfying the quantum definition of compactness must also satisfy the stricter, classical definition. This establishes a deep connection between algebraic symmetry theory and geometric properties of graphs.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO systems, the rigorous mathematical framework developed for analyzing symmetries (automorphism groups) using $	ext{C}^*$-algebras and state spaces provides powerful tools applicable to understanding symmetry breaking or structure preservation in complex quantum many-body models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a quantum analogue of 'compact graphs,' which are classically defined by properties of their fractional automorphism polytope, and determine if this quantum notion is equivalent to the classical one.

**Main result.** The natural quantum analogue of compactness is shown to be classical: if the set of quantum fractional automorphisms equals the full fractional automorphism polytope, then the graph must also satisfy the condition for classical compactness.

**Method.** The authors use techniques from $	ext{C}^*$-algebras and state space analysis on the quantum automorphism group algebra to define and analyze sets like the quantum fractional automorphism polytope ($	ext{QP}(A)$).

**Model / system.** The system under study is an abstract graph $X$ and its associated symmetry structures, including the classical and quantum orbital algebras derived from its automorphism groups.

**Key observables.** Quantum fractional automorphism set ($	ext{QP}(A)$), Classical/Fractional Automorphism Polytopes ($P(A)$, $S(A)$), Quantum Orbital Algebra ($	ext{QO}(X)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph structure (e.g., regularity, distance-regularity) and the fundamental magic unitary defining the quantum symmetries.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing inclusions between various matrix algebras derived from the automorphism group representations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes; the focus is on algebraic relationships and set inclusions (e.g., $P(A) \subseteq QP(A) \subseteq S(A)$).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining quantum analogues of classical graph properties, establishing key structural theorems relating these sets ($	ext{QP}(A)$ is closed/convex), and culminating in the main result that quantum compactness implies classical compactness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Compact graphs are graphs for which the fractional automorphism polytope has no genuinely fractional vertices. This paper proposes a quantum analogue of this idea by evaluating the fundamental magic unitary of the quantum automorphism group on states, which we show to produce a closed convex set of doubly stochastic matrices sitting between the classical automorphism polytope and the full fractional automorphism polytope. Our main result is that the natural quantum analogue of compactness is classical, that is, a quantum compact graph is classically compact.   We also relate this set to the quantum orbital algebra and obtain a hierarchy of classical and quantum compactness pseudo notions. The framework recovers familiar consequences of compactness through commutants and suggests quantum analogues of generous transitivity and distance-transitivity. We also isolate examples and open problems indicating where quantum symmetries may strictly refine the classical compactness theory.

</details>
